Preface



2 CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
1. Introduction
The Sumerian language is attested for approximately three thousand years and can be considered the oldest written language. It is still debated whether Sumerian was the language of the earliest texts known from Mesopotamia, the so-called Proto-Cuneiform or archaic texts dating to the last third of the fourth millennium BCE. Proto-Cuneiform texts widely lack phonetic indicators and signs marking morphology. The first secure attestation of Sumerian dates to the 29th century BCE. Among the so-called archaic texts from Ur there is ample evidence for morphemes. Cuneiform developed and changed significantly during its use from the last third of the fourth millennium until the first century CE (see Chapter 1.2, below). As a spoken language with its core area in the southern part of Iraq Sumerian probably died out at the turn from the third to the second millennium BCE or shortly thereafter; pockets of Sumerian-speaking people may have still lived on. However, Sumerian was never abandoned and even flourished, in the early second millennium BCE as a language of literature and science. Even in Akkadian sources, the reader is constantly confronted by Sumerian, as Sumerian logograms are used similar to abbreviations. The final remnants of Sumerian can be found in the so-called Graeco-Babyloniaca dating to the first century BCE and CE. Ranked second among the cuneiform languages, Sumerian attests to c. three million word attestations.1
This grammatical overview focuses on the Sumerian of the last third of the third millennium BCE, namely the texts of the Lagash II and Ur III periods, as well as the first half of the second millennium BCE, the so-called Old Babylonian period, thus ranging from c. 2200 to c. 1600 BCE. Occasional glimpses into earlier (so-called Old Sumerian or Early Dynastic texts) and later evidence (e.g., Middle Assyrian bilingual texts) will be added here and there.
1.1. Dialects and/or sociolects
Similar to cuneiform script itself, Sumerian developed over time and also exhibits local differences. Scribal idiosyncrasies and local writing conventions may indicate different forms of dialects. The designation “Sumerian” derives from the Akkadian word šumerûm. The Sumerian equivalent is e m e g e 7 - r , which probably means something like “native/noble tongue.”2 This is also the designation of the main dialect or register used in most texts, e.g., royal inscriptions, letters, most literary works, and so forth. It can be referred to as Standard Literary Sumerian.
Apart from Emegir the textual record attests to a register usually referred to as “women’s tongue” called e m e - s a l . Emesal is rendered in a somewhat different orthography, which reflects a different sort of pronunciation. The Sumerian word for “deity,” d i ĝ i r (written with the sign AN), for instance, is conventionally written d i m 3 - m e - e r in Emesal. Lexical texts of much later periods (end of second mill. BCE; the so-called Emesal-Vocabularies) and bilingual texts that juxtapose Sumerian written in Emesal and its Akkadian rendering help to find correspondences between terms in Emegir and Emesal. Emesal is used, for instance, in the direct speech of female deities and women in literary texts, but also in liturgical texts in the context of the profession of the g a l a -priest (e.g., in lamentations). It is not(!) used in direct speech of women in other types of texts, e.g., court documents. While Emesal was originally probably a gender-specific variety of
1 See STRECK 2010. 2 Compare the designation for the geographical or political entity Sumer, k i - e n - g e - r .



CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION 3
3
Sumerian, its use in cult may have been a secondary development.3 Apart from Emegir and Emesal lexical texts sometimes also attest to other types of sociolects or jargons as, e.g., the carpenter’s tongue etc. The following table provides a few examples of Emegir words juxtaposed with their Emesal equivalents:
Emegir Emesal Akkadian Meaning
lu2 m u - l u awīlu man munus n u - n u s sinništu woman
geme2 g i 4 - i n amtu maid servant ĝeš m u iṣu wood
kalam k a - n a - a ĝ 2 mātu land en u 3 - m u - u n bēlu lord
nin g a - š a - a n bēltu lady
šum2 z e 2 - e ĝ 3 nadānu to give du10-g z e 2 - e b ṭābu good
eri u r u 2 ālu city
1.2. Sources
Sumerian can be divided into various phases, but it is important to bear in mind that at any given period there are local traditions and slight differences in the language, and of course idiosyncrasies of scribes. These result in different orthographies. Peripheral sites often tend to produce Sumerian texts that are written “non-orthographically” or syllabically: e.g., d i - m i - i r for d i ĝ i r , “god.” The following paragraphs only contain a rough overview of the use and status of Sumerian in different historic periods.
1.2.1. Pre-Early Dynastic / Archaic period (c. 3300–2900 BCE)
Whether Proto-Cuneiform (c. 3300–3000 BCE) was invented by a Sumerian-speaking population is not implausible, but still debated. 85 % of the earliest texts are administrative records. The bulk of this material comes from the site of Uruk. The almost complete absence of syllabograms or morphological indicators does not allow any linguistic observations. The first more secure attestations of the Sumerian language—notation of morphological forms—in the textual record are attested in the so-called Archaic texts from Ur dating approx. to the 29th century BCE (ED I-II period).4 In these texts personal names show clear Sumerian forms: e.g., m e s - p a 3 - d a (written DAa PA3 DUBa; UET 2, 160 o 3).5
1.2.2. Early Dynastic period (c. 2600–2300 BCE)
This phase can be divided into the so-called Fāra- or ED IIIa-period (c. 2600–2475 BCE) and the Presargonic or ED IIIb-period (2475–2300 BCE). By the ED IIIa-period in the 26th century BCE the morphology of Sumerian increasingly appears in the textual record. Texts originate from the sites of Fāra (ancient Šuruppak), Tell Abū Ṣalābīkh, and others. The order of signs within a text unit (i.e., a text cell) is not yet fixed in this stage, which often hampers the correct interpretation of morphemes. In this period appear the first literary sources (e.g., Keš Temple Hymn; Proverb
3 See MICHALOWSKI 2023.
4 Absolute dates for the ED I-II are impossible to establish at the moment. They range from roughly 2900 to 2600 BCE. See the discussion in SALLABERGER & SCHRAKAMP 2015. 5 This name contains a non-finite form, for which see Chapter 16.



4 CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
Collection, za3-mim-hymns). Some of these texts are written in a particular orthography known as UD.GAL.NUN, which replaces signs by alternative ones, based on semantic, phonetic, or other kinds of associations (Figure 1).
Figure 1 Example of UD.GAL.NUN-orthography in a text from Fāra
Apart from scholarly texts (literary and lexical), we also have a sizable corpus of administrative and legal documents6 as well as royal and votive inscriptions.7 The Sumerian of the ED IIIb period (2475–2300 BCE), namely the Presargonic period, is sometimes referred to as Old Sumerian. The earliest letters date to this period.
1.2.3. Sargonic or Old Akkadian period (2300–2150 BCE)
Although this period uses predominantly Akkadian in official texts, there are some Sumerian and Sumerian-Akkadian royal inscriptions. Some of these inscriptions are known from later copies (predominantly of the Old Babylonian period). Administrative, legal texts, and letters are often composed in Sumerian as well.8
1.2.4. Lagaš II, Ur III, and Isin-Larsa period (2150–1900 BCE)
After the end of the Old Akkadian dynasty Sumerian is again predominantly used in the textual record. Most noteworthy for the slightly earlier Lagaš II phase are the texts by the city governor Gudea. He was probably a contemporary of Ur-Namma, the first king of the Ur III dynasty. A great number of royal inscriptions on statues and other material supports survive from this period. One of his major building projects, the construction of the Eninnu-sanctuary for the local god Ninĝirsu, was commemorated in a long inscription, i.e., two (or three) cylinders with more than 1,300 lines still preserved. This textual corpus represents the most important source material for pre-Old Babylonian Sumerian. For the Ur III period (c. 2100–2004 BCE) the sources apart from economic texts are scarce. This is, however, quite likely owed to the chances of discovery. The Yale Babylonian Collection holds one of the earliest catalogs of literary works dating to the period,
6 The corpus of administrative texts concerns, in particular, the rich archival documents from the institutional household called the e2-mi2 in the city state of Lagaš, which number around 1,700 documents. The city state of Umma left a sizable corpus of c. 1,000 administrative texts as well. 7 Kings of the Presargonic period left a rich corpus of royal and historical inscriptions. Important examples are the Stele of Vultures or the texts that concern the conflict between the city states of Lagaš and neighboring Umma. The corpus is edited in FRAYNE 2008 and the ETCSRI-project. 8 Often the presence of one verbal form or preposition establishes whether a text needs to be read in Sumerian or Akkadian.

which lists 42 different literary compositions. The texts of the so-called Isin-Larsa period (20001900 BCE) are still very much in the tradition of the preceding Ur III period.
1.2.5. Old Babylonian period (c. 1900–1600 BCE)
In the Old Babylonian period Sumerian was a language of scholarship. It was the language taught in the “tablet house” (~ school) by professional scribes to young apprentices. The curriculum was rather stable at places such as Nippur. Local traditions, however, co-existed as well. While Akkadian was the language of economy and communication (e.g., letters), and to some extent literature, it had not an important place in the “school” curriculum. We can assume that the Sumerian of this period was quite different in sound and even to some extent grammar compared to the preceding centuries. At least in the earlier phases of the Old Babylonian period, archival documents (e.g., legal documents, contracts, etc.) were still composed in Sumerian.
1.2.6. Middle Babylonian and Middle Assyrian periods
By the second half of the second millennium BCE a number of Sumerian compositions (e.g., the Ninurta epics Lugal-e and An-gim) were translated into Akkadian in full. The majority of sources come from Assyria and its capital Assur, where scholarly texts were imported either as war booty (see Tukulti-Ninurta Epic) or by Babylonian scribal families settling there. The scribes copied from already bilingual sources. The Sumerian appears sometimes defective and is prone to copying errors (phonetic variants etc.). Babylonia in this period shows a high degree of inventiveness in Sumerian inscriptions (e.g., short prayers and dedications on Kassite seals).
1.2.7. First millennium BCE
In the course of the last third of the second millennium BCE the written lore increasingly stabilized. Sumerian in the first millennium BCE was still rather productive, but much also derived from earlier written sources. Particularly noteworthy are liturgical texts, many of which are written in Emesal (see Chapter 1.1) and often came down to us in bilingual manuscripts.
The last Sumerian is attested in the first century BCE and CE in the context of the so-called GraecoBabyloniaca. These few texts contain a cuneiform inscription (e.g., an extract of a lexical text) on one side and a Greek transcription on the other.
1.3. The Study of Sumerian
The first full description of Sumerian grammar was published by Arno Poebel in 1923. But already more than half a century earlier, in 1855, Sir Creswicke Rawlinson, published non-Semitic inscriptions from Babylonia. The Irish philologist Edward Hincks recognized the new language as being agglutinative, i.e., a language which forms phrases by linking elements together. In 1869, the French Assyriologist Jules Oppert suggested to call this language Sumerian since some inscriptions known then contained the title “king of Sumer and Akkad” (l u g a l k i - e n - g e k i u r i - k e 4 ). Two years later, Archibald Sayce published an article with grammatical observations on Sumerian. Several scholars such as Paul Haupt (1858–1926) and George Bertin (1848–1891) contributed valuable insights into Sumerian in the following decades.
After WW2, Anton Deimel published his Sumerisches Lexikon consisting of nine volumes. Deimel’s Lexikon is the only complete Sumerian dictionary to this day. In 1949 the German Assyriologist Adam Falkenstein (1906–1966) published Grammatik der Sprache Gudeas. Basis



6 CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
for this work were the inscriptions of the city governor Gudea of Lagaš. The corpus of Sumerian literature that originated from the excavations at Nippur was reconstructed by Samuel Noah Kramer in the 1980s. These efforts led to the publication to the still valid The Sumerian Language by Marie-Louise Thomsen.
1.4. Sign values and how to read Sumerian
During the first half of the third millennium BCE the Akkadian- or Semitic-speaking population gradually took over the cuneiform writing system. In this process already established sign values were adopted and, if needed, adapted to suit the needs of Akkadian.9 Cuneiform is a logosyllabic script, i.e., characters can serve as logographs or word signs and syllables. The signs are polyvalent or polyphonous, i.e., a sign can have different readings. Additionally, the same sound can be represented by different signs, although Sumerian is more restrictive in the use of a particular sign than Akkadian. Frequently, the context helps to determine the intended reading, which is often easier in Akkadian than Sumerian due to its syllabic structure (e.g., i-šak-ka-nu instead of *i-reška-nu). Sumerian, unless it is also written syllabically (or non-orthographically), differs significantly, as the subsequent example demonstrates:
e2-ninnu anzu2mušen babbar2-ra-am3 kur-ra du10 mi-ni-ib2-bad “The Eninnu (i.e., temple) – it is a sparkling/white Anzu-bird spread (its) wings in the mountains.”
There are plenty of iconographical examples that depict the Anzu-bird spreading its wings. However, it is not excluded here that the compiler of the text intended ambiguity. The signs transliterated as d u 1 0 and b a d can be read š a r 2 and u s 2 respectively. Thus, the second line can also mean “in the mountains it killed numerously.” In most contexts, however, there is little doubt on the correct reading.
The so-called Early Dynastic Sign Lists (EDSL) with manuscripts from the sites of Fara and elsewhere frequently repeat signs in ensuing entries. The sign KA, for instance, is repeated five times. These repetitions correspond to the various readings of this sign. Later syllabaries such as Ea add the readings syllabically (and also the respective meaning in Akkadian).
EDSL Proto-Ea
Pronuncation (1) Sign (2) Akkadian (3) KA ka-a KA pû, “word”
KA zu-u2 KA šinnu, “tooth” KA gi-ri KA appu, “nose” KA e-nim KA awātu, “word” KA gu2-u2 KA rigmu, “voice” – du-u2 KA qabû, “to say” – pi-i KA
The textual record attests to various syllabaries; the most important among them are Ea and Diri. Each of these lists contains entries with either three or four subcolumns: (1) a pronunciation, (2) the respective logogram (group), (3) an analytical sign name, and (4) an Akkadian equivalent. The
9 For a short overview of this process see SERI 2010.



CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION 7
7
entries in syllabaries such as Ea are usually arranged according to sign shape of the logogram given in subcolumn (2). The pronunciation in the preceding subcolumn is written syllabically, e.g., gi-ri for the sign KA. Modern syllabaries or sign lists use this information in order to establish sign values: g i r i 1 7 . Local traditions might lead to the establishment of new sign readings; others were rejected over the course of time. The indices, however, were kept.
Subcolumn (3) in these syllabaries contains an ancient sign name or designation for the logogram or logogram group given in the preceding subcolumn. Sign names were mainly in use in the 1st millennium BC. While there are rather simple sign names such as a-a-u2 for the sign A, the more complex a sign was the more complex was its analytical description. The sign inventory contains many complex graphemes consisting of various parts. The frame sign LAGAB was often inscribed with another sign. While in modern transliterations it can be rendered LAGAB×A for the sign A inscribed with LAGAB, ancient lexicographers rendered it ša2 la-gab-ba-ku a-a-u2 i-gub (note that ša2 and i-gub are both pseudoSumerian forms only used in this technical description). The whole expression/sign name would mean something like: “in the middle of (the sign) LAGAB (the sign) A is set.” Subcolumn (4) links the reading of a sign with an equivalent in Akkadian. It is this subcolumn that helped establish semantic fields for Sumerian lexemes. A subseries of Ea was Aa. This series followed the sequence of signs in Ea, but added a much higher number of Akkadian equivalents.
The subsequent two examples show the variety of readings of a given sign. Establishing readings often depends on the context. Was a sign used as syllabogram or did it have its own nominal/verbal meaning?

There are several reading conventions for Sumerian texts. In the earlier days of research, sign values derived from the large reference works that were found in Assurbanipal’s manuscript collection. In recent years, however, the readings established by much earlier lexical manuscripts dating to the first half of the second millennium BCE are given precedence. Up to 2006, readings of Sumerian texts widely followed F. Ellermeier, Die Sumerischen Lautwerte (1980), and R. Borger, Mesopotamisches Zeichenlexikon (2004). The readings in Borger’s sign list are still adhered to, particularly when Sumerian logograms are used in an Akkadian context. In 2006, Catherine Mittermayer published her Altbabylonische Zeichenliste, which put forward updated



CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION 9

readings based on the works of Pascal Attinger.10 In 2021, the latter published his Glossaire sumérien, which contains an up-to-date list of sign readings.11
While recent publications of Sumerian literature more and more adapt to these updated readings, the traditional readings are upheld in other contexts, e.g., in administrative and legal documents of the Old Babylonian period. Online databases often also adhere to the more traditional readings. Since Sumerian has many homonyms, it is important to correctly indicate the correct identification in the transliteration: k u 3 is not the same as k u 4 . The different homonyms or signs are differentiated by indices. In some Sumerological traditions k u 2 or k u 3 are represented by accents: k ú and k ù respectively. The use of subscript digits, however, is preferable.
1.5. Sign values – short and long readings
A word-final consonant was often probably dropped, unless it is followed by a vowel (e.g., k i e n - g e - r a , “in Sumer” or “of Sumer”).12 A majority of CVC(VC) signs also have a short CV(CV) sign value. Although short readings are usually preferred, there is no overarching consensus for transliterations.13 The following table contains a few examples:
Short reading Long reading Meaning
du10 dug3 “(be(come)) good” ku3 kug “(be(come)) pure” sipa sipad “shepherd” du11 dug4 “speak, say”
10 For this system of transliteration see ATTINGER 2021: 57–62. 11 COHEN 2023 does not update his readings based on ATTINGER 2021. 12 *ki-en-ger.a or ki-en-ger.a(k). 13 Long sign readings are used, for instance, in the ETCSL project.



10 CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
In this overview, short readings are generally preferred, since very often a syllabic sign (re)introduces the word-final consonant: e.g., e r i k u 3 - g a instead of e r i k u g - g a, “in the holy city”). Note that the (unpronounced) classifier KI (place names) precedes the syllabogram: l u g a l u r i 5 k i - m a or l u g a l u r i m k i - m a .
1.6. Hyphenation
Like sign values there was never a final consensus, unlike in Akkadian, on hyphenating units of speech that belong together. In his grammar, Arno Poebel, connects the parts of a phrase considering it as a unit. This involves words, affixes, and clitics. Later on, Adam Falkenstein, uses hyphens to connect genitive constructions, adjectives, and some non-finite constructions. Brams Jagersma thoroughly discussed the problem of recognizing units of speech and differentiates between (1) orthographic words, (2) phonological words, and (3) grammatical words.14 Orthography is of little help in Sumerian to determine word boundaries, although the end of a line always marks a word boundary. Early Dynastic texts usually have shorter lines and thus it is easier to determine word boundaries. Grammatical words appear to be the most significant criterion for determining word units in Sumerian. Sumerian is an agglutinating language, i.e., morphological elements (e.g., object markers, plural markers) are affixed to a widely unchanged base. Prefixes and suffixes together with the stem or base they are attached to make up one single unit. In a genitive construction clitics are grammatical words in their own right.
There is no final consensus on hyphenation, unlike in Akkadian. Hence, adjectives are either linked to the noun they qualify or not. The latter is the more used nowadays. In editions one finds both i g i - z i b a - š i - b a r and i g i z i b a - š i - b a r , “he gazed righteously upon it/him/her.” But note the example e 2 - g a l , (lit.) the “big house,” which is a lexical unit meaning “palace.”
1.7. Word semantics – a word on lexical lists
Word lists, also referred to as lexical texts, are among the earliest texts (c. 3300–3200 BCE). Lists established writing conventions and were therefore essential for the establishment of early script. Throughout their history lexical lists collected words, signs, sometimes also expressions and grammatical forms. In the early second millennium BCE Sumerian entries occasionally received Akkadian equivalents added as annotations or glosses in smaller script. By the mid- and late second mill. BC translations fully infiltrated lexical lists and usually included in a separate column. The subsequent example contains a passage from a late lexical text, whose incipit was s i g 7 . a l a n = nabnītu (“being, creation”):

Following the common layout of bilingual lexical lists the Sumerian terms occur in the subcolumn on the left and their respective Akkadian equivalents are listed in the right-hand subcolumn. The manuscript of the lexical text Nabnītu above contains two columns per side. Its content is divided into sections by horizontal rulings. One of these sections deal with the Akkadian word mātu, “land.” This lexical text arranges its entries according to the phonetic shape of the Akkadian equivalents. The sign MIN or “2” in the right-hand subcolumn means “Ditto.”
Lexical lists such as this example form the basis for modern Akkadian dictionaries such as the Chicago Assyrian Dictionary (CAD) or Akkadisches Handwörterbuch (AHw). Both dictionaries frequently refer to lexical entries or other bilingual texts. The main publication of lexical texts is the series Materials for the Sumerian Lexicon (MSL). These volumes provide transliterations (only occasionally translations) of the major lexical texts from Mesopotamia. The subsequent example are three entries from the Old Babylonian Grammatical Texts (OBGT) published in MSL IV:
Sumerian Akkadian m u - u n - ĝ a r iš-ku-un “he placed” m u - n i - i n - ĝ a r u2-ša-aš-ki-in “he made place” m u - u n - d a - ĝ a r iš-ku-un-šu “he placed him/it”
The left-hand subcolumn contains a Sumerian finite verbal chain, which is equated to an Akkadian verbal form (Sum. ĝ a r ; Akk. šakānu).

2. Writing System
Literature: EDZARD
A treatment of the Sumerian language is only complete with a brief discussion of the writing system that was used to render the language. Writing developed in the alluvial plain of southern Mesopotamia shortly after the mid-fourth millennium BCE. Several technological developments can be found in the archaeological record beforehand and contemporaneously, which contributed to the emergence of a notational system to record incoming and outgoing goods, etc. These developments are best seen in Uruk and it is likely that Uruk institutions were the driving force in establishing this early writing system.
By roughly 3300 BCE a fully-fledged inventory of ideographic signs was established. Signs—both pictographic and abstract—were drawn into clay tablets using a pointed writing tool. Drawing in clay soon proved to be too cumbersome, and scribes started to represent signs by impressing the stylus rather than dragging the tool over the clay. A close-up inspection of archaic texts shows multiple impressions to, e.g., represent curved lines in signs. This modification in the writing technique may have sped up the ensuing abstraction of signs.
Over the ensuing centuries, the mechanics of inscribing signs became more standardized and simplified. Curved elements of signs were modified. The abstract sign for “sheep” (UDU), in the archaic text a circle with a cross in the middle, became a squarish sign. Instead of fifteen or even more impressions scribes of the third millennium only needed six impressions. This does not mean that signs overall used less impressions. Some periods (in particular, the ED IIIa and the Old Akkadian periods) use calligraphic sign forms with many optional impressions.
Cuneiform characters consist of one up to a dozen and sometimes even more impressions by the stylus. Complex graphemes may cluster several signs: e.g., LAGAB×A.DA.ḪA, i.e., the empty space in the squarish/rectangular sign LAGAB is inscribed with the sequence A, DA, and ḪA.
The order in which the stylus is impressed to form a sign is seldom arbitrary. Accomplished scribes used a specific wedge order. E.g., stacks of horizontal wedges were usually started from the bottom. The displacement of the clay created by impressing the stylus often helps to discern, in which order a sign is inscribed.
2.1. Types of signs and their use
The Sumerian writing system consists of roughly 600 individual signs. Among these are several ligatures and sign combinations. In view of their function(s) the sign repertoire contains the following categories, but it must be noted that the boundaries between these categories are fuzzy:
2.1.1. Logograms or word signs
Each cuneiform character can be read in numerous ways. The various sign readings are attested in ancient syllabaries (see above, Chapter 1.4). The list Ea and its various precursors (called ProtoEa) indicate the readings for single logograms (and their Akkadian equivalents). A logogram or word sign may often bear a semantic connotation: the logogram KA depicts a head with hatchings



2 CHAPTER 2. WRITING SYSTEM
around the area of the mouth.15 On the phonetic level, however, this logogram has different readings: d u 1 1 - g , “to speak,” k a - g , “mouth,” e n i m , “word,” g u 3 , “voice,” g i r i 1 7 , “nose” (older k i r i 4 ), z u 2 , “tooth.” The use of some signs is only limited to logographic representations: e.g., the sign SAG is used in its logographic meaning s a ĝ , “head” (also met aphorically), only. Single graphemes can be reduplicated: the two signs can be written next to each other in a sequence (e.g., DU.DU = LAḪ5), on top of each other (DU&DU = LAḪ4), or intersecting each other (GI%GI = GI16). Single graphemes can also be clustered to form a complex grapheme. The most common practices are ligatures and inserting a subordinate sign into applicable space of a matrix sign. Very common matrix signs are the box sign ĜA2 and the square sign LAGAB, both of which allow space for one or more signs.16 Clustering forms new semantic meaning(s). Base signs can also be slanted (usually by 45 degrees) towards the right or left. This method was known as tenû.17
Compound logograms are those that consist of two or more logograms. These are collected in the list Diri. The values for such compound logograms (or Diri-compounds) often cannot be derived from the values of the different constituents. The designation for the city governor, e n s i 2 , for instance, is a Diri-compound consisting of the signs PA, TE, and SI. Divine names and geographical names often are Diri-compounds as well: e.g., the Euphrates is read b u r a n u n in Sumerian and is written i7UD.KIB.NUN.
2.1.2. Syllabograms or phonograms
While each sign in the sign repertory may have its own meaning(s), quite a number of signs can also have a grammatical function and play part in rendering Sumerian morphology, either as syllabic signs in the verbal chain or as case markers in the noun phrase. While the afore-mentioned sign KA has several different readings depending on its use as verb (d u 1 1 - g ) or noun (k a - g , e n i m , g u 3 , z u 2 , etc.), its reading k a can also be used as syllabogram, e.g., to indicate a double genitive or genitive+locative: e 2 - l u g a l - l a - k a means “in the house of the king.”
15 Ancient lexicographers referred to these hatchings as gunû, often written gu-nu-u2 such as in, e.g., i-gi-gu-nu-u2, i.e., IGI.gunû = SIG7. The English word gunification is sometimes used. 16 Lexical texts such as Ea describe such signs with a complex description. The complex grapheme LAGAB×A, for instance, is referred to as ša2 la-gab-ba-ku a-a-u2 i-gub. The spelling ša2 may derive from the Sumerian term ša3g, “heart, inner part,” and i-gub is probably a rendering of i3-gub, “it stands.” Thus, this expression can mean: “In the middle of the LAGAB-sign an A-sign stands.” Some matrix signs became widely obsolete towards the end of the third millennium; e.g., the sign DUG; for matrix signs see WAGENSONNER 2022: 190–202. 17 The descriptive name of the grapheme ŠU2, for instance, is bar-te-nu-u, “slanted sign BAR.”

8 CHAPTER 2. WRITING SYSTEM
The sign MU read m u can mean “year,” “line,” and “offspring”; read m u ḫ a l d i m it denotes the “cook.” The reading m u , however, plays an important role to flag verbal forms as conjugation prefix: e.g., m u - u n - š u m 2 , “he gave it.” In the third millennium several other syllabograms are attested. The sign URUDA (usually meaning “copper”), for instance, has a reading d a b 6 and is used in verbal forms such as in the short sentence k u 6 - b e 2 l u 2 n u - b a - d a b 6 - k a r - r e 2 , “no-one shall take away its fish.”
Apart from non-orthographic texts, Sumerian has a limited number of words that are spelled syllabically. Some of these are loanwords derived from Akkadian: e.g., m a - d a , “land” (< mātu), p u - u ḫ 2 - r u - u m , “assembly” (< puḫrum; see Chapter 5.2.7).
2.1.3. Phonetic indicators
To some extent phonetic indicators or matres lectionis can be discussed together with syllabograms. Scribes occasionally felt the need to add some indication of a reading to a text. For this purpose, they used very often the format of glosses or annotations. Fig. 2 contains a few annotations in smaller script, which are placed in the vicinity of the main text, which they belong to. Historically, some phonetic indicators became part of compound logograms. The aforementioned writing for the Euphrates, b u r a n u n , consisting of the signs UD, KIB, and NUN, contains the phonetic indicator n u n . The Sumerian word for “ear, understanding,” ĝ e š t u - g , is often written with the three signs (in different configurations) GEŠ, TUG2, and PI. The first two are phonetic indicators; the third sign is a logogram for the word ĝ e š t u - g . Therefore, modern transliterations render this spelling: ĝ e š - t u 9 ĝ e š t u (older ĝ e š t u 2 ).

2.1.4. Classifiers or determinatives
Cuneiform script uses a small set of classifiers or determinatives. These signs flag nouns or proper nouns (e.g., divine names, place names, etc.) and indicate the semantic category they belong to. The use of classifiers is not obligatory and their frequency varies from period to period. Classifiers are somewhat bound to writing conventions. In a lexical environment, however, they are applied more rigidly (Fig. 3). Classifiers in cuneiform script are usually more prototypical: A simple sign of a fish (KU6) marks designations for species of fish; a simple water bird (MUŠEN) species of bird. Probably a piece of wood (GEŠ) deals both with types of wood and objects made of wood.
Classifiers are written either before or after the word they signify. With a few exceptions the position of a classifier in relation to the word it signifies stays unchanged. The classifier for place names (KI) is always written after the signified word. While the reality in speech of some classifiers is debated, but difficult to prove, KI certainly is not pronounced (e.g., l u g a l u r i 5 k i - m a , “the king of Urim [i.e., Ur]”). The classifier for divine names comes before and remained silent as well. The fish classifier was, as early syllabic spellings from Ebla show, probably pronounced (at least occasionally). The subsequent table contains a selection of classifiers and their position. Please note that each classifier is also a noun and as such could be part of a noun phrase!

Occasionally a signified word bears two classifiers. The highest god in the Sumerian pantheon is Enlil (e n - l i l 2 ). The same two signs were used to write his city Nippur (Sum. n i b r u ). In a few instances the place name EN.LIL2ki is preceded by a divine classifier. In these instances, which always denote the place Nippur, d e n - l i l 2 can be viewed as a frozen spelling. Sometimes a place name marked by the classifier KI might also be preceded by URU, “city.”
Classifiers are usually transliterated as superscript signs, although in the text artefact they have the same size as any other cuneiform character.
2.1.5. Numerical notations
Number signs are a separate class of signs, which however are not relevant here for the Sumerian grammar. Numerals will be discussed in Chapter 10.

3. Phonology
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 13–22; JAGERSMA 2010: 31–67; MICHALOWSKI 2004: 28–30; ZOLYOMI 2017: 27–36; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 35–38
The discussion of Sumerian phonology can fill many pages and depends on many complex considerations. On the following pages, it is treated only cursorily. Some aspects of phonology are also influenced by the establishment of new sign values. This concerns, in particular, voiced and unvoiced consonants. Consider, for instance, the traditional reading b a r a 2 - g compared to the reading p a r a 1 0 - g , “dais, pedestal” (Akk. parakku), which is now often used.
In the following vowels and consonants are treated separately. Questions such as the possibility of Sumerian being a tonal language are only treated tentatively.
3.1. Vowels
In cuneiform script, Sumerian indicates four vowels, which correspond widely to their Akkadian counterparts. There is some evidence for vowel length, which is based on loans of Sumerian into Akkadian: e.g., kakku < gag, “peg, nail; weapon,” vs. kāru < kar or */kār/, “quay.” Based on the high number of homophonous words, it is not excluded that Sumerian was a tonal language similar to Chinese.
Besides /a/, /e/, /i/, and /u/ there was probably also a vowel /o/, for which there is some evidence in Old Akkadian.21 The vowels /e/ and /i/ are not always clearly distinguished: e.g., the verbal prefix /i/ appears often as /e/ in ED IIIb texts. Similarly, the terminative infix /ši/ is often written with the sign ŠE3 in earlier texts.22
3.1.1. Vowel harmony
Vowel harmony is reflected strongly in Sumerian texts, either within a base/root and in morphology. Sumerian words cannot begin in a consonantal cluster as, e.g., “bl” or “sk.” If a Sumerian word is bisyllabic, hence consisting of two syllables, the vowels are often repeated: b a l a ĝ , “harp,” instead of *blaĝ or s i k i l , “pure,” instead of *skil. However, this is not a general rule and there are a number of bisyllabic words, that have two different vowels (e.g., b u g i n , “bucket”).
On the morphological level (e.g., within nominal and verbal chains) vowel harmony does not affect the base, but the surrounding elements: e.g., š u m 2 - m u - d e 3 for * š u m . e d . e , “in order that he gives,” or l u g a l - ĝ u 1 0 - u m for * l u g a l - ĝ u . a m , “he is my king.”
3.2. Consonants
While Sumerian sign values are mainly based on the information provided by ancient syllabaries such as Ea or Diri, clues for the phonology of Sumerian are widely based on Sumerian loanwords in Akkadian and how Akkadian represents Sumerian consonants, e.g., e 2 - g a l and Akk. ekallum, “palace,” or para10-g (previously bara2/barag) and Akk. parakkum, “dais.” As indicated in Chapter
21 See HASSELBACH 2005: 101 with note 8. 22 Both the readings g i 4 and g e 4 , “to return,” are valid based on the extant evidence.

1.7, syllabaries such as Ea provide sign values written in syllabic script. It should be noted, however, that the earliest form of this syllabary dates to the first half of the second millennium BCE. Nonetheless, the phonemes are also used for earlier texts.
Grammars and grammatical overviews put different emphasis on Sumerian phonology. Despite some ongoing debate, the information below is widely based on the thorough study of phonemes in JAGERSMA 2010. The following table is based on JAGERSMA 2010: 33; see also SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 36. The values given within square brackets follow the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA).

3.2.1. Stops
A stop or plosive is a consonant sound that is characterized by the sudden blocking of some part of the oral cavity. In Sumerian there are two sets of stops. Transliterations indicate one series as voiced, the other as voiceless. The pronunciation of Sumerian stops is based on loanwords. Semitic languages have three sets of stops: voiced, voiceless, and emphatic, although there are slight differences in the various languages. Some evidence for Akkadian stops comes from spellings of Greek names in cuneiform script. Transliterations represent the stops b [p], d [t], and g [k] as voiced stops, but in the third millennium BCE these consonants were voiceless and only around 2000 became widely voiced. Thus, in earlier evidence they correspond to the Akkadian stops /p/, /t/, and
23 SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 36 suggest a voiceless tap.



CHAPTER 3. PHONOLOGY 3
3
/k/. This is confirmed by early Sumerian loanwords in Akkadian as in ekallu < e 2 - g a l , “palace,” or utukku < u 2 - d u 1 1 - g , “demon.” The same applies to loanwords in the other direction: d a m g a r a 3 < tamkāru, “merchant.” Loanwords attested after 2000 BCE show that the Sumerian stops became voiced: e.g., guzalû < g u - z a - l a 2 , “throne-bearer.” At word-final position, however, Sumerian stops retained their voiceless pronunciation.
The voiceless aspirated stops p [ph], t [th], and k [kh] were written with separate sound signs opposed to the stops b [p], d [t], and g [k]. Loanwords offer the most evidence for the different qualities of these sounds and changes over time. In the third millennium, early Sumerian loanwords in Akkadian show that /p/, /t/, and /k/ were represented by Akkadian /p/, /t/, and /k/: e.g., temennu < t e m e n , “foundation.” Around 2000 BCE, a sound change in Akkadian changed the pronunciation of these stops from plain voiceless to voiceless aspirated.
– The labial stop, b [p]:

CHAPTER 3. PHONOLOGY 5
5
e n s i 2 - k iššiakku city governor
– The glottal stop – [ʔ] is not represented in transliterations. It lost its status as independent consonant during the second half of the third millennium BCE. All VC and V type syllables may have originally started with either /h/ or the glottal stop. The presence of a glottal stop can be reconstructed for some words based on writing environment and particular spellings. E.g., in the spelling g a l a - e - n e , “the lamentation priests,” the plural suffix *–ene is not assimilated to the /a/ in g a l a . Therefore the original form may have been */galah/ or */galaʔ/.
3.2.2. Affricates
Affricates are two-part consonant sounds. They begin by stopping the air from leaving the vocal tract and then releasing it through a constricted opening (similar to fricatives). In Sumerian two consonants – z [ts] and ř [tsh] – can be identified as affricates. – Loanwords help to narrow down the pronunciation of z [ts]. This sound was loaned to Akkadian and is rendered there as /s/: e.g., asû < a - z u , “physician.”
z i - d kīnu just, righteous
z i - g tebû to rise
z u 2 šinnu tooth
z u 2 - l u m suluppû date
ĝ e š g u - z a kussû chair, throne
– The presence of the affricate sound ř [tsh] can be deduced from the use of the sign DU as syllabogram, which offers the sign values ra2 (or řa2) and re6 (or ře6). The sound lost its independent status after the mid-third millennium. Words that originally ended with the sound ř [tsh] show now endings either in /r/ or /d/. In the texts from Lagash and Umma we see with Gudea first a change to /r/: e.g., š u n a - n i - b a - r e , “may it not be released” (Gudea St. B, col. ix:26). Compare also g u 4 - ř e 6 (ED IIIb administrative account from Girsu) vs. g u 4 - r e (Ur III administrative account from Girsu). At Nippur, Isin, Ur, and Garshana earlier ř [tsh] is rendered /d/.
In the Old Babylonian period the sound became obsolete. Where the Ur III texts reflected it with /r/, the Old Babylonian sources, particularly the literary texts, have /d/. Here probably a different dialect prevailed. The bulk of literary texts originates from Nippur and thus northern Babylonia.
Taking these considerations into account, the verb for “to build” written with the sign DU3 should be rendered ř u 2 in ED IIIb, r u 2 in Lagash II and Ur III, and d u 3 in Old Babylonian sources. Compare also the loanword narû < n a - d u 3 - a , “stele” (< planted/erected stone).

k u 4 - d / r ( ř ) erēbu to (let) enter; to bring in
k u 5 - d / r ( ř ) nakāsu to cut
g u 4 - d / r ( ř ) alpu ox
3.2.3. Fricatives
Fricatives are formed by forcing air through a narrow opening. Sumerian had four of these fricatives, but only three are represented in writing. – The sibilant s [s] is secured as phoneme, since the writing system offers different signs for /s/ in combination with other sounds (e.g., sa, si, su, etc.). Its exact pronunciation is problematic due to the difficulties with Akkadian sibilants. Old Akkadian had the two sibilants /ś/ and /š/. During this period there was a sound change and /ś/ was merged with /š/.
s a r šaṭāru to write
s i p a - d rē’û shepherd
s i k i l ellu pure
s a 6 - g banû (to be) good
s e s [ š e š ] ahu brother
– The palatal fricative š [ʃ]:
š u m 2 nadānu to give
š u qātu hand
š e še’û barley
š a 3 - g libbu heart
e š 3 sanctuary
– The velar fricative ḫ [x]:
ḫ u š huššû terrifying
s a ḫ a r eperu earth, dirt
m a ḫ ṣīru august, lofty

– The final fricative, /h/, is not represented in writing, but can be reconstructed. The Sumerian word for “house,” written e 2 , originally was */haj/. Also the word for “river, canal,” written i 7 - d , originally was /hit/. While originally a full consonant, it was lost in the second half of the third millennium BCE.
3.2.4. Nasals
Nasal sounds are those sounds when air passes through the nose as a result of the lowering of the soft palate at the back of the mouth. Sumerian had three nasal sounds. – Like Akkadian the Sumerian nasal sound m [m] was probably bilabial. Loanwords such as m a - d a for mātu, “land,” or k u 3 - d i m 2 for kutimmu, “silversmith,” suggest that the sound was similar in both languages.
m e n crown
ĝ e š m a 2 eleppu boat
e m e lišānu tongue, language
a m a ummu mother
– The nasal sound n [n] was a dental sound like Akkadian. There is a noteworthy change of /n/ to /l/ in certain environments. The occupation n u - b a n d a 3 , “overseer,” is loaned to Akkadian laputtû. In Ebla (24th century BCE), the title l u g a l , “king,” is attested in the writing n u - g a l . Here probably also belong examples such as Emegir k a l a m becoming Emesal k a - n a - a ĝ 2 , “land.”
n a r nāru musician
enim
[ i n i m ] awātu word
– The third nasal sound ĝ [ƞ] falls out of use after the Old Babylonian period, but is still consequently written in the first half of the second millennium BCE. Older transliterations often use /ng/; sometimes also the phonetic sign ƞ is used to represent this consonant. It is attested in initial and final position as well as in the middle of a word (ĝ u r u š , “adult male”; d a ĝ a l , “wide”; b a l a ĝ , “harp/lyre”). Note that in Emesal texts main dialect /ĝ/ is rendered by /m/: e.g., d i ĝ i r becomes d i m 3 - m e - e r ; d a ĝ a l becomes d a - m a - a l .
d i ĝ i r ilu god, deity
ĝ a r šakānu to place, put
ĝ e n alāku to go
u ĝ 3 nīšu people

n a ĝ šatû drink
ĝ i d r u ḫaṭṭu scepter
1.1.1. Lateral
Laterals are formed by raising the tip of the tongue against the roof of the mouth so that the airstream flows past one or both sides of the tongue. Sumerian had one lateral sound: – Based on loanwords the Sumerian lateral sound l [l] corresponds to Akkadian /l/ (e.g., k i s a l and Akk. kisallu). If /l/ occurs in final position and is followed by the vowel /a/, orthography distinguishes between the use of LA and LA2. Noteworthy is the phenomenon of initial Sumerian /l/ represented as /n/ in certain conditions. Sum. l u g a l is represented as n u - g a l in Ebla texts. There is also the use of the negative prefix n u - of the Sumerian verbal chain, which is represented as l a - (l a - b a - r a - t u m 2 - m u , “he will not bring it to you”) and l i - (li- b i 2 - i n - d u 1 1 - g a - a m 3 , “it is him, who did not say it”) respectively.
l u g a l šarru king
l u 2 awīlu man
g u l abātu to destroy
1.1.2. Tap24
A tap is produced by a single contraction of the muscles so that one articulator is thrown against another. In the case of Sumerian this is the dental/alveloar sound r [r]. – Several sound signs confirm the phoneme r [r]. There is some evidence from third millennium sources that this sound was close to the stops d [t] and t [th]. Around the time of Gudea /t/ became /r/ between vowels. In Ur III there is some evidence for this sound change as well: /ra/ is sometimes written with the sound sign /ta/ (e.g., k i š i b 3 P N i b 2 t a , “PN’s seal was rolled over this”; usually written i b 2 - r a ). Around the same period there are cases where /r/ is written instead of /d/.25
l i b i r labiru old
d a m - g a r a 3 tamkāru merchant
d u b - s a r tupšarru scribe
e r i n 2 ṣābu troops
24 SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2003: 36 classify it as a vibrant, to which taps and trills belong. 25 Compare here also the spelling ra for the adverbial infix ta; see Chapter 14.1.6.

1.1.1. Semivowel
The semivowel – [j] was lost by the end of the third millennium. Transliterations do not account for it, but there are indications in the writing system that this consonant existed. The writing a - e , “into the water, by water” can also be found in the spelling a - e 3 . In Old Akkadian, the sign E3 is used as a sound sign for /je/. Thus, the spelling a - e 3 points towards a reconstructed form /ʔaj/ for a , “water.”

4. Linguistic Background of the Sumerian Language
The Sumerian language is attested from at least the first half of the third millennium BCE until the decline of cuneiform writing in the first century CE.26 Despite this long durée of attestations, Sumerian is considered an isolate in linguistic terms. So far, suggestions for possible affiliations of Sumerian to known language families such as Uralic or Dravidian remain to be very speculative.27 Like Elamite in neighboring Iran Sumerian stands alone, but its morphology and syntax can be compared to other languages.
Sumerian is an agglutinating language, i.e., words are modified by adding affixes (pre- and suffixes), which are attached to the word base. This is one of the main differences to Semitic Akkadian, which is an inflecting language. A modern agglutinating language is Turkish. In Turkish, suffixes can be added to nouns and verbs to specify number, possession, case, tense, and person. E.g., Turkish ev, “house,” ev-ler, “houses,” ev-ler-de, “in the houses,” and ev-ler-im-de, “in my houses.”
Furthermore, Sumerian is an ergative language, which is characterized by split ergativity in its verbal agreement system. (The split ergativity does not affect the noun phrase but can be observed in the verbal phrase; e.g., in the pronominal referents used for imperative and cohortative forms.) The ergative case marker (see Chapter 6.2.2.1) flags the agent of a transitive verb. It can be compared to English “by” as in “The house was built by the man.” The absolutive case (see Chapter 6.2.2.2) is an unmarked case (-Ø) and encodes both the patient of a transitive verb and subject of an intransitive verb.
Transitive Sentence lugal-e e2 mu-un-du3 *lugal.e e.Ø mu-n.řu.Ø King House he/she built Agent Patient Verb
Intransitive Sentence lugal mu-ĝen lugal-Ø mu-ĝen King he/she went Subject Verb
While ergative languages are those languages that mark the agent differently from patient and subject, accusative languages mark agent and subject similarly (nominative). Akkadian is an accusative language. Both the agent in a transitive sentence and the subject of an intransitive sentence are marked by the nominative –um: e.g., šarrum bītam ibni, “the king built the house,” vs. šarrum illik, “the king went (on campaign).”
26 See Chapter 1.2. 27 See, for instance, PARPOLA 2016a, 2016b, and 2022.



Introduction to Sumerian 15
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
4.1. Parts of speech
We need to differentiate in Sumerian between (1) words that function as their own entity and hence convey a meaning and (2) particles. A nominal case marker (see Chapter 0) attached to a noun does not constitute a word on its own: it is a (2a) nominal particle. A modal prefix (see Chapter 1) does not convey a meaning by itself either. It introduces a certain mode of speech (e.g., a wish), but only if attached to a verbal chain. It is therefore a (2b) verbal particle. Particles such as these, however, formed their own entities in late grammatical lists, which attempt to explain them by their respective Akkadian meaning. Possessive suffixes are (2c) common particles since they can be connected to both noun and verb bases.
Sumerian knows eight different parts of speech or word classes, which to some extent are reflected by the various chapters of this overview:
• Nouns (see Chapters 5 and 6)
• Adjectives (see Chapter 5.3)
• Pronouns (see Chapter 7)
• Interrogations (see Chapter 7.2)
• Adverbs (see Chapter 8)
• Numerals (see Chapter 10)
• Verbs (see Chapters 11, 12, 13, 0, 1, and Excursus B:)
• Exclamations
• Subjunctions and conjunctions
4.2. Animacy
Sumerian has no gender but defines nouns according to their animacy. Words like d u m u , “child,” can refer both to male and female children. Some words, however, are restricted to male or female referents: e.g., u r d u 2 - d (a r a d ), “male slave,” vs. g e m e 2 , “female slave.” Therefore, Sumerian nouns can be divided into animate and inanimate entities (also known as person and non-person respectively). This grammatical differentiation can be observed in the use of third person possessive suffixes (see Chapter 5.4), plural suffixes (see Chapter 5.5.2), and of course the verbal chain.
Animacy needs to be viewed from an emic point of view. Hence slaves or animals often belong to the inanimate class. A deified weapon in a literary contexts is usually animate: e.g., š a r 2 - u r 3 - e e n d n i n - u r t a - r a g u 3 m u - n a - d e 2 - e , “The šar-ur(-weapon) speaks to the lord Ninurta” (Lugal-e 121).
4.3. Word order
The word order in a Sumerian sentence Subject — Object — Verb (or Agent — Patient — Verb). It is likely that this word order influenced the Akkadian sentence structure, which sets aside Akkadian from other Semitic languages.28 Close contact to neighboring Iran may also influenced the isolate language Elamite in this manner.
28 Arabic follows the common Semitic word order Verb — Subject — Object.






17
5. Noun I: The Nominal Template
Literature: THOMSEN 1984: 55–65; EDZARD 2003: 29–45; JAGERSMA 2010: 101–135; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 37–45; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 42–52
This chapter deals with the Sumerian noun phrase or nominal template. A nominal template may be a noun qualified with modifiers or attributes (e.g., an adjective) or may consist of a sequence of nouns (together with attributes) that fulfil the same function within the sentence (e.g., direct object of a verbal action). This function is expressed by a case postposition. As will be treated further below in much more detail, the function is usually only marked at the end of the nominal template. In some periods, however, single elements (e.g., epithets) fulfilling the same function within the sentence may be marked separately.29
Nouns can be divided into substantives and adjectives. As discussed below in Chapter 5.3, the status of adjectives is somewhat problematic; some adjectives derive from verb bases. Based on its agglutinating character, Sumerian nouns are built up of different elements that can be attached to a head noun (and its modifier). These suffixes (characterized as pronouns and enclitics) express possession, number, and case.30 The different elements appear at specific places after the head noun:
12345 Head Modifier Possessor Plural Case
The subsequent two arrays of examples shall demonstrate the use of these elements:
Example 5.1
n u n “prince” n u n m a ḫ “lofty prince” n u n m a ḫ - z u “your lofty prince” n u n m a ḫ - z u - n e “your lofty princes” n u n m a ḫ - z u - n e - r a “for your lofty princes”
Example 5.2
d i ĝ i r “deity” d i ĝ i r g a l “great deity” d i ĝ i r g a l - ĝ u 1 0 “my great deity” d i ĝ i r g a l k a l a m - m a “great deity of the land” d i ĝ i r g a l k a l a m t u r - r a “great deity of the small land” d i ĝ i r g a l k a l a m t u r - r a - ĝ u 1 0 “my great deity of the small land” d i ĝ i r g a l k a l a m t u r - ĝ a 2 “the great deity of my small land” d i ĝ i r g a l k a l a m t u r - r a - ĝ u 1 0 - n e “my great deities of the small land” d i ĝ i r g a l k a l a m t u r - r a - ĝ u 1 0 - n e - d a “with my great deities of the small land”
29 This is comparable to some other agglutinating languages such as Elamite; e.g., u2 všu-ut-ru-uk-dnaḫ-ḫu-un-te ša-ak vḫal-lu-tu3-uš-din-šu-ši-na-ak-ki2-ik su-un-ki-ik an-za-an šu-šu-un-ka, “I, Šutruk-Naḫḫunte, son of HalludušInšušinak, king of Anšan and Susa.” Here, all elements, of the chain are marked by the first-person locutive (-k). See STOLPER 2004: 73–74. 30 Compare, for instance, Turkish: e.g., evlerimde, “in my house” < ev, “house,” + ler, PLUR + (i)m, POSS.1sg + de, “in.”



18
As the examples show, adjectives (here m a ḫ and g a l ) usually follow the noun they qualify. In a few instances, an adjective can also stand on its own in the front of a noun phrase (e.g., k u 3 d l u g a l - b a n 3 - d a , “the Holy, Lugalbanda”). Some divine names are preceded by an additional AN, “god, heaven,” which also might indicate a similar phenomenon: AN d m a r - t u , “the heavenly one, Martu.”
The head noun may be a simple noun (e.g., n u n or d i ĝ i r ), a compound noun (see Chapter 5.2), a pronoun (see Chapter 7), a non-finite verbal form (see Chapter 25), or a clause with a subordinate finite verbal form. While the above examples represent basic noun phrases, several nouns can also be chained together. In such a case, the case marker usually only appears at the very end of the (combined) noun phrase (Example 5.4 and Example 5.5). Texts of the ED IIIb period occasionally put the case marker after each noun phrase (Example 5.6):
Example 5.3
s u l - g e - r e Šulgi sul–ge-r.e PN.ERG
Example 5.4
g u 3 - d e 2 - a “Gudea, gu–de.a PN
e n s i 2 l a g a š k i - k e 4 the city governor of Lagaš” ensi-k Lagaš.ak.e
city governor GN.GEN.ERG
Example 5.5
u r - d n a m m a “Ur-Namma, Ur–Namma.a(k)
n i n t a k a l a - g a the strong man, ninta kalaga
l u g a l u r i 5 k i - m a the king of Ur, lugal Urim.a(k)
l u g a l k i - e n - g e k i - u r i - k e 4 the king of Sumer and Akkad” lugal Ki’enege-r Ki’uri.ak.e
In this very common noun phrase, the king’s name can be considered the head noun. The remaining three lines refer to this noun, and function like modifiers. The case marker, here an ergative, only appears at the end of the noun phrase. In earlier texts, e.g., royal inscriptions of the ED IIIb period, the case marker can also appear at each element (Example 5.6):
Example 5.6
e 2 - a n - n a - t u m u 2 “E’anatumu, E–an.a–tumu
e n s i 2 l a g a š k i - k e 4 the city governor of Lagaš, ensi-k Lagaš.ak.e
m u p a 3 - d a d e n - l i l 2 - k e 4 nominated by Enlil, mu pad.a Enlil.ak.e
a 2 š u m 2 - m a d n i n - ĝ i r 2 - s u 2 - k a - k e 4 bestowed with strength by Ninĝirsu, a šum.a Nin-Ĝirsu.ak.ak.e
š a 3 p a 3 - d a d n a š š e - k e 4 chosen in the heart by Nanše, (...)” ša-g pad.a Našše.ak.e ...



Introduction to Sumerian 19
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
As these examples show, a noun phrase can be extended almost indefinitely by adding additional, dependent noun phrases.
5.2. Word formation
Sumerian offers several possibilities to create nouns by combining words. The identification of such forms is not always straightforward. Bilingual texts may help to clarify whether adjacent nouns represent an independent lemma or a noun phrase.31 Another indicator are loanwords: e.g., k u 3 - d i m 2 ~ kutīmu, “gold- or silversmith.” Some compound forms may also be written with only one sign: e.g., l u g a l (GAL+LU2), which derives from lu2 gal, “the great man.”
5.2.1. Compound forms
A noun can be combined with (1) another noun, (2) an adjective, and (3) a nonfinite verb. If two nouns are involved, the combination may show four different types of relationship: a. The second noun qualifies or specifies the purpose of the first noun. Examples with e 2 , “house,” show this rather well: e.g., e 2 - t u r 3 , “stable” < “house” + “animal stall”; e 2 m u ḫ a l d i m , “kitchen” < “house” + “cook.” Other examples are z i z 2 n u ĝ u n [n u m u n ], “seed-emmer” < “emmer” + “seed”; d u m u s a ĝ , “firstborn” < “child” + “head”; d u m u n i n t a [n i t a 2 ], “son” < “child” + “man.” b. Literary contexts, in particular, attest to compound forms that are syntactically follow a pattern Noun2 – Noun1, which replaces a genitive construction *N1 – N2. a k (see Chapter 6.2.1): e.g., a n - z a 3 consists of a n , “heaven,” and z a 3 - g , “border,” for the meaning “horizon.” Other examples are i r i - b a r , “outside of the city” < “city” + “outside”; g u 2 z a , “necklace” < “neck” + “semi-precious stone.” c. Two nouns can also be coordinated to form an overarching concept. Examples are m a š 2 a n š e , “animals, livestock” < “goat” + “donkey”; u 2 - š e m , “herbs” < “plants” + “aromatics”; ĝ e š - g e , “canebrake” < “wood” + “reed.” d. Two nouns may also form a genitive construction: e.g., b e š e ĝ 3 - d u b - b a - k , “archivist” < “chest” + “tablet” + GEN.; e 2 - k i š e b 3 - b a - k , “storeroom” < “house” + “seal” + GEN.; l u 2 - i t u - d a , “a worker” < “man” + “month” + GEN.
If a noun and an adjective forms a new lemma, two types can be differentiated: a. Up to the Ur III period the adjective gal, “big,” may appear first. A remnant of this is also found in the writing of l u g a l , “king” = GAL+LU2. The adjective appears in first position before nouns of occupations: e.g., g a l - n a g a r , “chief carpenter”; g a l - k i n d a 2 , “chief barber”; g a l - s u g a l 7 , “chief messenger.”32 b. More common is the adjective in second position. Very common are the adjectives gal, “big,” and maḫ, “great”: e.g., e 2 - g a l , “palace”; g a l a - m a ḫ , “chief lamentation singer”; s u g a l 7 - m a ḫ , “grand vizier”; u r - m a ḫ , “lion”; ušumgal(GAL.UŠUM), “dragon”. Words such as k u 3 - s i 2 2 , “gold” < “precious metal” + “yellow” also belong here.
31 As example one may refer to the bilingual versions of the Sumerian literary text Ninurta’s Return to Nippur, whose Middle Assyrian version translates the sequence KA.ḪI with Akkadian ṭēmu (reading d i m m a ), but whose NeoAssyrian translation renders the elements individually as amātu ṭābtu (reading e n i m d u 1 0 ); see WAGENSONNER 2018: 260–61. 32 Combinations with initial GAL are well attested since the archaic period. Such designations occur in the so-called Standard Titles and Professions List Lu2 A (NAMEŠDA). Later versions of this text adding pronunciation glosses show, that the adjective was interpreted to be in second position: GAL.UKKIN, “head of the assembly” is glossed keen-gal-la; GAL.PA.DUN3 is glossed ḫu-ur-saĝ-gal.



20
The latter type is not always easy to identify, as the noun+adjective compound may also denote a noun phrase.
Very productive are combinations of a noun and a non-finite verbal form (or participle). The verb is very often a present participle, which is the unmarked verbal base in the perfective stem (see Chapter 16.2). The participle may modify the noun. Examples of this are k i - n u 2 - d , “bedroom” < “place” + “to lie”; š e - b a , “barley ration” < “barley” + “to hand out”; n i ĝ 2 - b a , “present” < “thing” + “to hand out.” The modifying particple may be preceded by another noun, which serves as its subject or object: e.g., k i - a - n a ĝ , “offering place” < “place” + “water” + “to drink”; ĝ i r i 2 k u 6 - d a r , “fish-fillet knife” < “knife” + “fish” + “to split”; n i ĝ 2 - z i - ĝ a l 2 , “living things” < “thing” + “breath” + “to be there.”
More common are compound forms, where the head noun serves as object of the present participle. These compound nouns are well attested for occupations: e.g., d u b - s a r , “scribe” < “tablet” + “to write”; k i s a l - l u ḫ , “courtyard sweeper” < “courtyard” + “to clean”; z a d i m , “stonecutter” < za, “stone” + dim2, “to fashion”; z a b a r - d a b 5 , “cupbearer” < “bronze” + “to hold”. Similar are words such as m a š 2 - š u - g i d 2 - g i d 2 , “diviner” < “goat” + “hand” + “to stretch out,” i.e., “someone who stretches out the hand into the goat.”
The present participle may also be used in compounds as action nouns: e.g., š u - l u ḫ , “cleaning” < “hand” + “to clean,” i.e., “let one’s hand clean”; šu-tag, “decoration,” i.e., “let one’s hand touch.”
Finally, the noun may also be marked with a case postposition. Rare examples are š a 3 - g e - d a b 6 , a type of garment, < “heart” + directive case + “to surround,” i.e., “which surrounds the belly”; a m a - a r - g i 4 , “freedom” < “mother” + dative case + “to return,” i.e., “who returns to the mother.”
5.2.2. Abstract nouns
Abstract concepts are expressed by adding a prefix n a m - before a substantive, adjective, or an infinite verb. These correspond to Akkadian nominal forms ending in –ūtu (e.g., šarrūtu and ṣīrūtu):
lugal nam-lugal “king” “kingship”
maḫ nam-maḫ “great” “greatness”
zu nam-zu “to know” “wisdom”
n a m - g e m e 2 servitude
n a m - ĝ u r u š virility etc.
n a m - k a l a - g a force



Introduction to Sumerian 21
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
n a m - d u m u the status of child
The word for mankind, n a m - l u 2 - l u 7 l u (n a m - l u 2 - u 1 8 - l u ), probably goes back to *n a m l u 2 - l u 2 . In mid-third millennium sources the particle n a m - is usually written n a m 2 - ( ).33 In addition to n a m - some abstract concepts are expressed by initial n i ĝ 2 , (lit.) “thing”: e.g., n i ĝ 2 g u 7 , “food,” n i ĝ 2 - b a , “gift.”
5.2.3. Occupations
Some occupations start with the particle n u -:
ĝeškiri6 nu-ĝeškiri6 “garden” “gardener”
(Akk. kirû) (Akk. nukarribu)
nu-banda3 “inspector”
(Akk. laputtû)
It is difficult to ascertain whether the particle n u - derives from or is in any form connected to l u 2 , “man/person,” which also may mark occupations. In sources from Ebla (24th cent. BCE) Sumerian l u g a l is rendered n u - g a l (see Chapter 1.1.1, above).
5.2.4. Frozen forms or deverbal nouns
A few verbal forms may become lexicalized nouns. Examples of this type use the modal cohortative and precative prefixes as well as the anterior prefix u - (see Chapter 15.1.2). These frozen forms are usually used for technical concepts such as g a - a n - t u š , “tenant” (< (lit.) “let me make him sit”), or u 3 - n a - a - d u 1 1 , “letter” (< (lit.) “when you speak to him/her”).34 The cohortative modal prefix g a - (see Chapter 15.1.7) is particularly productive.35 Such terms stop being verbal phrases. They are as such also not comparable to non-finite verbal forms (see Chapter 16), but are treated like any other noun: e.g., *g a - a n - t u š z i - d e 3 n u - ĝ e š k i r i 6 - r a ĝ e š a l m u - n a - a n - š u m 2 , “the righteous tenant gave the hoe to the gardener.”
A few frozen forms derive from precative verbal forms such as ḫ e 2 - ĝ a l 2 , “abundance,” (< “may it be there”) or ḫ e 2 - d u 7 , “ornament” (< “may it be fitting”).36
g a - a n - t u š tenant (< “let me make him sit
there”)37
33 Compare the initial entries of the Standard Professions List Lu2 A (NAMEŠDA), which may be interpreted as offices using this particle. See WILCKE 2005. 34 For “letter” Sumerian can also use the general term d u b , “tablet.” The form u 3 - n a - a - d u 1 1 is derived from the corpus of Sumerian letters themselves. It usually appears in the second line after the letter’s addressee marked with the dative. The term was loaned to Akkadian as unnedukkum. 35 See SELZ 1993. 36 Compare the PN e n - ḫ e 2 - d u 7 - a n - n a , “En-priestess, the ornament of heaven.” 37 JAGERSMA 2010: 130 understands it “who will truly dwell in it” (*ga-n(i)-tuš).



22
g a b 2 - r a drover (< “let me hit it”)
g a - a n - z e 2 - e r Underworld (< “let me destroy
him”)
g a b 2 - s a r jeweller (< “let me engrave it”)
g a - a b - s a 1 0 buyer (< “let me buy it”)
u 3 - n a - d u 1 1 - g letter (< “when you speak to him”)
Also non-modal finite verbal forms may form nouns in their own right. Examples are m a - a n s i m , “sieve” (< “he sifted it for me”); i b 2 - t a k a 4 , “remainder” (< “it left it behind”).
5.2.5. Reduplication
Basic color terms seem to be derived from reduplicated stems: Examples are b a b b a r , “white,” derived from *b a r 6 - b a r 6 , and k u k k u , “black,” derived from *k u 1 0 - k u 1 0 . Alliteration can be seen in onomatopoeic constructions such as d u m - d a m ~ z a , “to clamor.” Also verbal bases can be reduplicated, either to express plurality of the action (e.g., iterative), or a plural of the agent.38
5.2.6. Other deverbal nouns
Additionally, a verbal base can be added the suffix /u/, which creates object nouns: The noun s a r
r u , “document” ( ), derives from the verb s a r , “to write.” For the verb d a ḫ , “to add,” a
derivational noun d a ḫ - ḫ u or d a ḫ - u 3 , “addition” ( ; also ), is attested.39
5.2.7. (Akkadian) Loanwords
Occasionally, Sumerian texts contain Akkadian loanwords. The influence of Semitic on the Sumerian lexicon occurs already in the first half of the third millennium. Two types of loanwords can be distinguished, which are qualified by their respective endings: (1) Loanwords (often in earlier texts) can end in –a such as m a - d a , “land” (< Akk. mātu), or d a m - g a r a 3 , “merchant” (< Akk. tamkāru). (2) Another type are loanwords ending in –u m and thus preserving the Akkadian mimation. An example is p u - u ḫ 2 - r u - u m , “assembly” (< Akk. puḫru). (3) An additional third type are endingless loanwords that may be even older than the other two types. Examples are ḫ a r - r a - a n , “road” (< Akk. ḫarrānu), and s i l i m , “peace, good health” (< Akk. šulmu).40
5.3. Adjectives
Other than in Akkadian adjectives in Sumerian are a problematic case. Their existence as a separate word class is still debated. Sumerian has only a very limited number of basic adjectives. These express qualities referring to dimensions, colors, age, values, and physical properties. They can
38 For verbs of the so-called Reduplication class see Chapter 11.3.2. 39 See JAGERSMA 2010: 130. 40 For this latter type see CIVIL 2007.



Introduction to Sumerian 23
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
also be used as verb bases. For instance, d u 1 0 , “to be/become/make good, pleasant,” is used as non-finite form modifying a noun: k i d u 1 0 , “a good place.”
d a ĝ a l rapšu wide
g a l rabû big
m a ḫ ṣīru august, great, lofty
d u 1 0 - g ṭābu good, pleasant
t u r ṣeḫru small
d u g u d kabtu heavy
k u 3 - g ellu pure, holy
Sumerian uses often other types of words to express concepts that would be interpreted as adjectival. Abstract nouns (see Chapter 5.2.2) such as n a m - l u g a l , “kingship,” can be used in a genitive construction: p a r a 1 0 n a m - l u g a l - l a , “the royal throne.” Another way was to use verbal stems with the suffix *–a (past participle): m u n u s s a 6 - g a , “beautiful woman,” l u g a l k a l a - g a , “strong king.” In economic contexts, materials are sometimes added without marker: e.g., ĝ i r i 2 z a b a r , “bronze dagger.”
Some adjective-like constructions go back to unmarked non-finite verbal forms: e.g., n i 2 g u r u 3 , “awe-inspiring” (lit. “carrying aura”), g a l z u , “knowledgeable” (lit. “knowing great things”).
By adding the suffix - b e 2 , followed often by - e š 2 or - š e 3 , adjectives can also be transformed into adverbs and thus qualify a verbal action (see Chapter 8): e.g., b a d 3 g a l - b e 2 š u m u - r a a n - d u 7 - d u 7 , “he completed for you the fortifications in a great manner.”
5.4. Possessive suffixes
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 29–31; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 38–39; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 82–83
Sumerian can express possession by adding a possessive suffix after the head noun and modifier: e.g., m a š 2 g e g g e - z u , “your black goat.” Alternatively, the same position can be used by a noun followed by the genitive case marker (see Chapter 6.2.1): e.g., m a š 2 g e g g e s i p a - d a , “the black goat of the shepherd” (*maš geggeg sipad.a(k)). The enclitic suffixes are the following:
1 sg - ĝ u 1 0 my
2 sg - z u your (sg.)
3 sg anim.
( ) - ( a ) - n e 2 (older - a - n i ) his, her
3 sg inan. - b e 2 (older - b i ) its



24
1 pl - m e our
2 pl
( ) - z u - n e - ( n e ) your (pl.)
3 pl anim. - a - n e - n e their
3 pl inan. - b e 2 their
The suffix of the first-person singular is written with the sign MU. Older publications use the reading - m u instead of - ĝ u 1 0 . The Emesal form, however, is - m u . The same writing may also indicate the possessive suffix followed by a dative, at least in texts up to the middle of the Ur III period. Thereafter, the dative is indicated, first by - u r 2 as in l u g a l - ĝ u 1 0 - u r 2 and later in full: l u g a l ĝ u 1 0 - r a , “for my king.” The terminative case *.še is represented by - u š or - š e 3 . A phonetic change occurs when the possessive suffix is followed by the locative or genitive: e 2 - ĝ u 1 0 , “my house,”41 vs. e 2 - ĝ a 2 , “in my house; of my house.”
Example 5.7
k a n 4 42 e 2 - g a l - l a - ĝ u 1 0 “my palace gate” (< lit. “my gate of the palace”) kan egal.a(k)-ĝu
k a n 4 e 2 - g a l - ĝ a 2 “the gate of my palace” kan egal-ĝu.a(k)
The suffix of the second-person singular behaves like the first-person suffix. If it is followed by the locative or genitive, enclitic - z u becomes - z a : n u - b a n d a 3 k a l a m - z a , “the officer in/of your country.”
There are two suffixes for the third-person singular. The animate suffix is written - a - n e 2 . Very often it only appears as - n e 2 in texts: s e s - a - n e 2 , “his/her brother,” vs. e 2 - g a l - n e 2 , “his palace.” The reading ne2 instead of ni for -a-ni seems to be confirmed by rarely attested spellings with the signs NE and E: e.g., m u - n e - e m , “her name is” (*mu-ane-am); a m a - e , “his mother” (*ama-a(n)e.e).43 While the dative case is not written in earlier texts after a vocalic ending, at the end of the third millennium it may appear as - ( a ) - n e 2 - e r . The terminative case appears as ( a ) - n e 2 - e š 2 / š e 3 . Is the suffix followed by locative or genitive, we find the writing - a - n a (rarely also - a - n e 2 - a ).
The inanimate third-person suffix is - b e 2 written with the sign BI: e.g., n a - r u 2 - a - b e 2 , “its stela.” Here, the writing system uses two different signs for b e 2 and b i 2 . If the suffix is followed by locative or genitive, the suffix often assimilates as in, e.g., u 4 - b a or u 4 - b e 2 - a , “at this time, then.” In this expression -be2 can also be used as demonstrative pronoun: “on its/this day” (see Chapter 7.1). The suffix is also used to form adverbs: e.g., m a ḫ - b e 2 and m a ḫ - b e 2 - e š 2 , “in a lofty manner” (see Chapter 8). Texts of the ED IIIb-period also use the spelling - b e 6 (PI): e.g., k i - l a 2 - b e 6 , “its weight”; e n i m - b e 6 a l - t i l , “its (or: this) matter is completed.” The sequence
41 Please note that this could also be read e 2 - m u ḫ a l d i m , “kitchen.” 42 Traditionally read k a 2 . See ATTINGER 2021: 595 s.v. kan4, akan2, kankan. 43 For these rare instances see JAGERSMA 2010: 216–17. Additionally, there are also spellings with double /nn: e.g., m a - d a - a n - n e 2 , “his country.”



Introduction to Sumerian 25
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
- b e 2 - e š 2 can also be written - b e š (-b i š ): e n d u 1 1 - g a - n e 2 s a ĝ - b e š e 3 - a , “lord whose commands take precedence.”
The suffix of the first-person plural is always written - m e : e.g., e r i - m e - a , “in our city.” In texts of the Gudea or Lagaš II-period, the writing - m e can also be short for the enclitic copula - m e e n , “I am; you are,” and - m e - e š , “they are”: s i p a - m e , “our shepherd; I am a shepherd” (see Chapter 8).
The suffix of the second-person plural is either written - z u - n e - n e or - z u - n e . The spelling z u - e - n e - n e is rare: u g u - z u - e - n e - n e (Akk. elīkunu), “upon you.” Following locative or genitive does not influence the vocalic ending: i g i - z u - n e - n e - a , “in your eye, before you.”
The suffix of the third-person plural is written - a - n e - n e , which derives from the third-person singular and the plural marker - e n e (see Chapter 5.5.2). Depending on the context a form n i n 9 a - n e - n e means either “their sister(s)” or “his/her sisters.”
The forms of the independent pronoun will be discussed in Chapter 7.1.
The initial vowel of the third-person enclitic copula (*- a m ; see Chapter 8) usually contracts to the vowel of the possessive suffix: e.g., e 2 - z u - u m , “it is your house”; l u g a l - b i - i m , “it is its (i.e., of the land) king.”
Example 5.8
e 2 l u g a l - ĝ a 2 - k a “in the house of my king” e lugal-ĝu.a(k).a
n i ĝ 2 - g u 7 s e s - a - n a “the food of his brother” niĝgu ses-ane.a(k)
d i ĝ i r e r i - n a - k e 4 - n e “the gods of his city” diĝir eri-ane.ak-ene
Both the animate and inanimate third-person possessive suffixes are also used for the anticipatory genitive (see Chapter 6.2.1). Furthermore, the possessive suffixes play a role in non-finite verbal forms as adverbial clauses of time (also known as pronominal conjugation; see Chapter 16.7): e.g., ĝ a r - r a - z u - n e , “when you placed” (~ Akk. ina šakānīka/ki).
Possessive suffixes can express additional relationships between the possessor and the possessed.

5.5. Plural
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 31–33; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 45–48
Sumerian has various possibilities to mark plurality of the noun. Animate nouns usually mark the plural with the suffix * - e n e . Inanimate nouns remain unmarked: e.g., 3 g u 4 n i g a , “3 fattened oxen,” d u m u m a š 7 d b a - b a 6 - m e , “they are the seven siblings of Baba” (Gudea Cylinder B xi:11).48 Additionally, both animate and inanimate nouns can be reduplicated to express plurality. It is also not uncommon that a single noun expresses plurality on its own. Frequently, the plural is only expressed in the verbal template. In contrast to Akkadian there is no dual in Sumerian.
5.5.1. Reduplication
The easiest way of marking the plural is by reduplicating the head noun. Examples are d i ĝ i r d i ĝ i r , “gods,” or s e s - g a l - s e s - g a l , “school elders (lit. big brothers).”49 Reduplication also occurs with verbal bases. Some reduplicated forms might have resulted in variant pronunciations: e.g., d e l i ( ), “one,” > d i d l i ( ), “single ones”. d i d l i is also used in the sense of “individual” as in e r i d i d l i m a - d a u n u k i - g a , “the individual cities of the domain of Uruk” (Rīm-Sîn I, RIME 4.2.14.10, 34).
While the noun itself can be reduplicated, more common is the reduplication of an adjective, which indicates plurality: d i ĝ i r g a l - g a l , “great gods.”
5.5.2. Enclitic morpheme –ene
The suffix * - e n e appears already in the discussion of possessive suffixes (see Chapter 5.2). The second- and third-person plural suffixes contain the plural marker.
diĝir gal diĝir gal-gal “great god” “great gods”
diĝir gal-e-ne diĝir gal-gal-e-ne “great gods” “all the great gods”
If * - e n e is preceded by a vowel, the initial /e/ is usually dropped (e.g., u g u l a - n e , “overseers”). Often, if the preceding word ends in a consonant, a syllabogram is included (see the table in Chapter 2.1.2), which contains the initial /e/ of the plural marker: e.g., d i ĝ i r - r e - n e , “gods.”50

5.5.3. “mixed”
Mostly used in economic contexts, but occasionally also found in other text genres, is the nonfinite verbal form ḫ i - a , “mixed.” Examples are e r e n 2 ḫ i - a , “workers,” g u 4 - a b 2 ḫ i - a , “cattle,” or g e b e š e ĝ ḫ i - a , “diverse reed boxes.”
The numeral classifier meš ( ) found in Akkadian texts goes back to the enclitic copula of the third person plural: e.g., u r - s a ĝ g a l - m e - e š , “they are great warriors.”51
51 In texts of the Lagaš II and Ur III dynasties the copula can be shortened to - m e .
