Introduction to Sumerian 35
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
6. Noun II: Nominal Case markers
Sumerian nominal cases are expressed by a case marker, which appears at the end of the nominal template. Based on their function within the sentence, the case markers can be divided into three categories: (1) adnominal cases; (2) cases that mark agent, subject, and patient of a sentence; and (3) adverbial or dimensional cases. The cases of group (2) are usually co-referenced in the verbal template; the cases of group (3) may also be co-referenced in the verbal template. Exceptions are the adverbiative and the equative.
The distribution of cases differentiates between animate and inanimate nouns. This only concerns the cases of group (3). The following table summarizes the various suffixes (for the noun) and affixes (for the verb):52
Nominal case marker Verbal affix Animate Inanimate Adnominal case
Genitive -/ak/ -/ak/ Cases for agent, etc.
Ergative -/e/ -/e/ Absolutive -Ø -Ø Adverbial cases
Dative -/ra/ (-/a/) /a/ Comitative -/da/ -/da/ /da/ Ablative – -/ta/ /ta/ Terminative -/še/ -/še/ /ši/ Locative 1 – -/a/ /ni/ (Locative 2) (-/ra/) (-/a/) (/i/ or /e/) Directive (Locative 3) (-/ra/) -/e/ /i/ or /e/ Adverbiative -/eš/ -/eš/ Equative -/gen/ -/gen/
For the cases with initial vowel (genitive, ergative, locative 1, directive, and adverbiative) see the table in Chapter 2.1.2, which lists the possible syllabograms: e.g., l u g a l u r i 5 k i - m a , “the king of Ur” (*lugal Urim.a(k)).
6.1. Marking Direct, Indirect, and Oblique Objects
In a transitive sentence, the direct object is in the absolutive, i.e., the unmarked case (see Chapter 6.2.2.2). The absolutive is used for both animate and inanimate objects.
Example 6.1
ensi2 lagaški-ke4 dub-šen ku3-ga-ne2 mu-na-dim2 ensik Lagaš.ak.e dubšen kug-ane.Ø mu-nn.a-(n.)dim.Ø city governor GN.GEN.ERG chest pure-POSS.ABS VEN-IPP.DAT-(3sgA).to fashion.3sgP
The ruler of Lagaš fashioned her holy chest for her. (###)
The indirect object (IO) expresses the recipient of a verbal action and can be expressed by two different cases depending on whether the IO is animate or inanimate. An animate IO is marked with the dative on the noun phrase; an inanimate noun is marked with the directive. These case markers
52 For the verbal affixes, see Chapter 0.



36
agree with the IO prefix in the verbal template (see Chapter 14.1.3), as the following two examples show:
Example 6.2
lu2-dnanna-ra in-na-du11 Lu-Nanna.ak.ra i-nn.a-(n.)dug.Ø PN.DAT VP-IO-3sgA.to speak.3sgP
He spoke to Lu-Nanna.
Example 6.3
alan-e enim im-ma-šum2-mu alan.e enim.Ø i-m-b.a-(b.)šum.e
He gives a message to the statue.
In the first example, the recipient or IO of the verbal action is an individual. Lu-Nanna is marked with the dative case postposition. The verbal template agrees with the IO by inserting the corresponding animate prefix (*-nn.a-). The second example uses an inanimate IO. The noun is marked by the directive, which is agreed upon by the inanimate IO prefix in the verbal template (*b.a-).53
In contrast to the indirect object, an oblique object (OO) is an animate or inanimate noun, which is affected by the verbal action, or on which a verbal action is performed. It also serves as semantic direct object in idiomatic verbs (consisting of nominal part and verbal base), when the direct object (ABS) is already taken by the nominal part. The animate OO is marked by the dative; the inanimate OO is marked by either the directive or the locative. IO and OO share some forms (marked with dative and directive cases), but describe different functions in the sentence. This can also be seen in the verbal template. Here, the OO prefix is used instead as the following two examples demonstrate (see Chapter 14.1.7.3):
Example 6.4
den-lil2-ra dnin-maḫ mu-ni-us2 Enlil.ra Ninmaḫ.Ø mu-nn.i-(n.)us.Ø DN.DAT DN.ABS VEN-OO-3sgA.to follow.3sgP
He placed Ninmaḫ next to Enlil.
Example 6.5
kar niĝin6ki-na-ke4 ma2 bi2-us2 kar Niĝin.ak.e ma.Ø bi-(n.)us.Ø quay GN.GEN.DIR boat.ABS OO3sginan-ERG.to follow.ABS
He moored the boat at the quay of Niĝin.
In the first example the affected noun is animate and hence marked with the dative. It fulfils the function as OO, which is agreed upon in the verbal template (*-nn.i-). The second example uses an inanimate OO marked with the directive, which agrees with the inanimate OO prefix in the verbal template (*-b.i-). Both examples are transitive sentences with the verb u s 2 , “to make follow, be adjacent to.” The verb can also be used in the intransitive sense “to follow, to reach to, etc.”
53 Note that the prefix is here assimilated to the preceding ventive prefix /m/.



Introduction to Sumerian 37
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
Example 6.6
an ku3-ge ib2-us2 an kug.e i-b-us.Ø
heaven pure.DIR VP-OO-to follow.3SGS
It reached pure heaven.
In intransitive sentences, the OO prefix in the verbal template is syncopated (*-n- and *-b-). Apart from the directive inanimate OO can also be marked with the locative case. This can be seen, for instance, in the idiomatic verb enim—ĝar, “to lay claim on something.”
Example 6.7
ki-sur-ra lagaški-ka lu2 uri5ki-ke4 enim bi2-ĝar kisura Lagaš.ak.a lu Urim.ak.e enim.Ø b.i-(n.)ĝar.Ø
boundary Lagaš.GEN.LOC man Ur.GEN.ERG word.ABS OO-3sgA.to place.3sgP
The man of Ur laid claim on the boundary of Lagaš.
6.2. Adnominal cases
Sumerian has one case that can be classified as adnominal: the genitive.54 Adnominal cases do not indicate a syntactic relation with the verb and are therefore not co-referenced in the verbal chain. These are called adnominal cases because they only concern the noun phrase.
6.2.1. Genitive
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 36–39; FOXVOG 2010: 39–44; JAGERSMA 2010: 139–154; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 42–43; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 43–45.
Among the nominal case postpositions, the genitive case represents the most diverse. It is the only case that can be combined with the other nominal case markers, which must follow the genitive. Generally speaking, the genitive links together two nouns (including optional modifiers, possessive and plural suffixes). By establishing this link a new noun phrase is created.
Simple genitive construction The genitive usually establishes a link between two basic noun phrases to form an extended noun phrase. The analytical form of the genitive is * - a k , which attaches to the governed noun. The noun marked with the genitive (nomen rectum = N2) is dependent on another noun (nomen regens = N1). Syntactically, the genitive takes up the same position as the possessive suffix in the basic noun phrase (see Chapter 5.4):
Example 6.8
e 2 - a - n e 2 “his house” e-ane
e 2 a b - b a “the father’s house” e aba.a(k)
54 Strictly speaking, also the equative is adnominal as it also indicates a relationship between two nouns. The classification here follows SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023.



38
In writing, the sign AK is never used to mark the genitive. The spelling of the Elamite deity Inšušinak, however, a name of Sumerian origin, preserves the genitive ending: din-šu-ši-na-ak, (lit.) “Lady of Susa.”
If the preceding noun ends in a vowel, the initial vowel of *-ak is usually omitted. The final /k/ only appears, if the genitive is followed by a vowel, be it a possessive suffix ( - k a - n e 2 ), a plural marker ( - k e 4 - n e ) or other vocalic endings ( - k a , - k e 4 ; - k a m ). Before consonant, the /k/ is dropped. The different possibilities can be summarized as follows: – N1 N2-a(k) – N1 N2-ak-a – N1 N2-ak-e – N1 N2-ene-(a)(k)
Example 6.9
u r - s a ĝ d e n - l i l 2 - r a “for the hero of Enlil”55 ursaĝ Enlil.(a)(k).ra
u r - s a ĝ d e n - l i l 2 - l a 2 - r a “for the hero of Enlil” ursaĝ Enlil.a(k).ra
e n i m d i š t a r a n - n a - t a “with the word of Ištaran” enim Ištaran.a(k).ta
e 2 d u m u - š e 3 “to the house of the son” e dumu.(a)(k).še
As the following figure shows, each part of the genitive construction can be extended by modifiers. These modifiers can be, for instance, adjectives or participles. The noun phrase occupying the nomen rectum can additionally be followed by possessive suffix and plural marker, if these refer to the nomen rectum:
Example 6.10
e 2 d i ĝ i r - r a “the house of the deity” e diĝir.a(k)
e 2 d i ĝ i r - z a “the house of your deity” e diĝir-z(u).a(k)
e 2 d i ĝ i r - r e - n e “the house of the deities” e diĝir-ene.a(k)
e 2 d i ĝ i r - z u - n e “the house of your deities” e diĝir-zu-(e)ne.(a)(k)
55 Attested in ED IIIb royal inscriptions.
Head noun Modifier Head noun Modifier Poss. Plural Poss./Det. Plural Case
Nomen regens Nomen rectum
GEN



Introduction to Sumerian 39
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
The nomen regens can also be marked by a possessive suffix (or demonstrative pronoun) and a plural marker. These morphemes cannot be added directly to the nomen regens but need to follow the genitive construction. Syntactically, they mark the whole genitive construction:
Example 6.11
u g u l a u š - b a r - r a “the overseer of the weaver(s)” ugula ušbar.a(k)
u g u l a u š - b a r - r e - n e “the overseer of the weavers” ugula ušbar-ene.(a)(k)
u g u l a u š - b a r - r a - k e 4 - n e “the overseers of the weaver(s)” ugula ušbar.ak-ene
u g u l a u š - b a r - r e - n e - k e 4 - n e “the overseers of the weavers”56 ugula ušbar-ene.ak-ene
Headless genitive constructions The nomen regens (or N1) can also be omitted. In such a case, the construction has the pattern N2a k and is used in the meaning “the one(s) of ...” or “that of ...” The following examples use this construction followed by the third-person enclitic copula -/am/ (see Chapter 8):
Example 6.12
u m m a k i - k a m “he is one of Umma” Umma.ak-am
a - m a - r u - k a m “it is urgent < lit. it is of a flood”57 a-maru.ak-am
Occasionally, similar contexts may preserve the nomen regens. For instance, instead of g e m e 2 d b a - U 2 - k a - m e (DP 339), “they are those of Geme-Baba,” the same archive attests to the expression l u 2 g e m e 2 - d b a - U 2 - k a - m e (ED IIIb; DP 116), “they are the men of Geme-Baba.” Note that the personal name Geme-Baba itself is a genitive construction: *geme-Baba.a(k). The headless genitive also occurs with the independent personal pronoun (see Chapter 7.1):
Example 6.13
z a - ( a - ) k a m “it is yours” (lit. “it is of you”) ze.ak-am
ĝ a 2 - ( a - ) k a m “it is mine” (lit. “it is of me”) ĝe.ak-am
Multiple genitive construction An extended noun phrase can also contain two and even three genitive constructions. If another nomen regens is added, the single genitive construction becomes the corresponding nomen rectum. The genitive postposition is then added at the end. Multiple genitive construction are best expressed by using brackets:
56 Compare the ED IIIb economic text Nik 1, 97 (CDLI P221866): u g u l a g e m e 2 - n e - k e 4 - n e . 57 Alternatively ATTINGER 2021: 112, a - m a - r u - k , “water of the storm.”



40
*e2 ses lugal -ak-ak
genitive construction # 1 “the brother of the king”
genitive construction # 2 “the house of (the brother of the king)”
Example 6.14
e 2 d i ĝ i r - r a “the house of the deity” e diĝir.a(k)
d u m u e 2 d i ĝ i r - r a - k a “the child of the house of the deity” dumu e diĝir.ak.a(k)
d u m u e 2 d i ĝ i r - r e - n e - k a “the child of the house of the deities” dumu e diĝir-ene.(a)k.a(k)
d u m u e 2 d i ĝ i r - r a - k a - k e 4 - n e “the children of the house of the deity” dumu e diĝir.ak-ene
If the nomen rectum (< genitive construction) needs to be in the plural, the plural marker appears between the two genitive postpositions (only rarely attested):
Example 6.15
š e - b a ( ... ) l u 2 d b a - b a 6 - k e 4 - n e - k a m “it is the grain ration for the men of Baba.” še-ba (...) lu Baba.ak-ene.(a)k-am
Multiple genitive constructions are common. Many divine names such as d n i n - ĝ i r 2 - s u , “Lady of Girsu,” are genitive constructions. If they serve as theophoric elements in personal names, this often results in a double genitive construction: u r - d n i n - ĝ i r 2 - s u - k a - k e 4 .
Three-fold genitive constructions are theoretically possible, but rare: e.g., s a ĝ i t i i z i m d b a b a 6 - k a - k a , “the beginning of the month of the Feast-of-Baba” (*saĝ itud izim Baba.ak.ak.a(k)). The context of this expression may suggest that the last case is not a genitive, but a locative.
Anticipatory genitive The Possessor (N2) in a genitive construction can also be emphasized and put first. This is called an anticipatory genitive. In such a case the headless noun in the genitive is followed by N1 and the respective possessive suffix of the 3rd person (see ch. Error! Reference source not found.). If N 2 is animate, N1 is followed by –ane; if it is inanimate, it is followed by –be:
e2-a lugal-be2 “of the house its king/owner” *e-a(k) lugal-be
lugal-la e2-ne2 “of the king his house” *lugal-a(k) e-ane



Introduction to Sumerian 41
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
Due to the fact that the genitive modifies a noun, it frequently can be translated as an adjective: e.g., i 3 l u g a l - l a , “oil of the king” > “royal oil,” e 2 n u n - n a , “house of the prince” > “princely house.”
Adjectives may modify both the head noun (N1) as well as the possessor (N2): e.g., e2-ku3-lugalzi-da, “the holy house of the righteous king.”
6.2.2. Cases for marking agent, subject and patient
Sumerian has two cases, which mark the roles of subject and object in the transitive and intransitive sentences. Sumerian is an ergative language, which is characterized by having a distinct case marking for the subject of the transitive sentence.
6.2.2.1. Ergative (*.e)
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 35–36; JAGERSMA 2010: 154–159; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: ###
The ergative marks the agent or subject of the transitive sentence. The subject of the intransitive sentence is marked by the absolutive (see Chapter 6.2.2.2). The basic form of the ergative postposition is –e and thus it is identical to the directive or locative 2 (see Chapter 0).58 If the preceding word ends in a consonant, the writing system often employs a syllabogram (see Table 2-1): e.g., s i p a - d e 3 , “the shepherd.” However, the sign E is also common after consonants: e.g., s e s - e , “the brother”; l u g a l - e , “the king.”
If the preceding word ends in a vowel, the case postposition is often assimilated. Plene writings do occur: e.g., d u t u - u 3 for * U t u - e , “the sun god.” If the ergative postposition follows a genitive, - k e 4 is usually written (never *- a k - e ): e.g., l u g a l m a - d a - k e 4 , “the king of the land.”
The usual word order in a transitive sentence is Subject — Object — Verb:
Example 6.16
ensi2-ke4 e2 mu-du3 ensik.e e.Ø mu-(n.)du.Ø city governor.ERG house.ABS VENT-FPP.to build.ABS
The city governor built the house.
In this example the subject e n s i 2 - k is marked with the ergative. It is co-referenced by the prefix n before the verbal base. The sentence’s object is e 2 , which is marked with the absolutive.
Syntactically, a sentence can only contain one ergative. However, the ergative can consist of several of nominal phrases each marked with the ergative (see Example 5.6, above).
The ergative may also appear in non-finite constructions, which are very common in names or epithets:59
58 There is also a demonstrative enclitic - e ; see Chapter 7.5.2. 59 For non-finite verbal forms or participles see Chapter 16.



42
Example 6.17
mes an-ne2 pa3-da mes an.e pad-a
young man sky god.ERG to choose-.ABS
The young man chosen by An.
The construction in Example 6.17 is also often found in a different configuration: *m e s p a 3 - d a a n - n a , (lit.) “the chosen young man of An.” This construction is very common, particularly in ED IIIb royal inscriptions (see Example 5.6).
6.2.2.2. Absolutive (*.Ø)
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 35; JAGERSMA 2010: 159–160; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: ###
The absolutive is the case of the patient or object in the transitive sentence (see Example 6.16, above). Furthermore, the subject of the intransitive sentence is in the same case. The absolutive is an unmarked case; it has no separate morpheme (hence the notation Ø in analyses). A sentence can only have one absolutive, even though the sentence’s object may encompass several objects. The absolutive usually appears directly before the verb. However, it may also appear at the beginning in order to highlight it.
Enclitic copula
The absolutive is also used as a vocative: e.g., d i ƞ i r , “o God.” It is also the form found in entries of lexical lists.



Introduction to Sumerian 43
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
6.2.3. Adverbial cases
This chapter discusses the adverbial or dimensional cases that make up the last element of the nominal template. We will encounter these cases again in the chapter on adverbial infixes in the verbal template (Chapter 0). These cases can be mapped out as follows:
They fulfil different functions in the Sumerian sentence. The indirect object is marked with a dative, if the noun is animate, but with a directive, if the noun is inanimate: e.g., l u g a l - r a , “for the king” (*lugal.ra), vs. e 2 - g a l - l e , “at/for the palace” (*egal.e). The semantic object of a sentence may be expressed with different cases. The verb for “to love” in Sumerian consists of the nominal part k i ( - g ) and the verbal base a ĝ 2 , “to measure out.” Since k i ( - g ) is already the direct object of the verb, the semantic object is marked with the dative.60
6.2.3.1. Dative
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 40; JAGERSMA 2010: 161–165; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 73–74
The dative case marker - r a is used with animate nouns only. In its basic form (usually after word ending in consonant) it is written with the sign RA. In early Sumerian texts it is explicitly written, if the case is preceded by a vowel: e.g., n i n - a - n i / n e 2 , “(for) his lady.” Starting in the late Ur III period the case marker is reduced to /r/ and written with the syllabograms AR, ER/IR, or UR2, as the following examples show. Texts of the Old Babylonian period attest to the full writing of - r a , also after vowel.
Example 6.18
a-kal-la-ar šum2-mu-dam Akala.ra šum-ed-am PN.DAT
it is to be given to Akalla.
Example 6.19
lugal-a-ne2-er lugal-ane.ra king-POSS3sgan.DAT
60 See, for instance, the so-called compound verbs discussed in Chapter Excursus B:.
Fig. 4 Votive inscription of Amar-Suena dedicated to Ningal



44
for his/her king
Example 6.20
aia-zu-ur2 du11-ga-na-ab aia-zu.ra dug-a-nn.a-b father-POSS2sg.DAT to speak-VP-IO3sgan-ABS
tell your father
If the dative appears after the genitive case, the full form is written: e.g.,
The dative functions as the indirect object in the sentence.
The dative usually marks an animate noun, towards which or to whom an action is directed. It frequently occurs in expressions for uttering. While - / r a / is used for animate nouns only, it has a complimentary inanimate counterpart in the locative case marker - / a / (see ch. 0).
The dative is used to express the indirect object or an oblique object.
den-lil2 lugal-diĝir-re-ne-ra *Enlil lugal-diĝir-ene-(ak)-ra “for Enlil, king of the gods”
If preceded by vowels such as /u/ or /a/, the dative is represented by a syllabogram * - u r / - u r 2 or * - a r : e.g., l u g a l - z u - u r 2 , “for your king.”
6.2.3.2. Comitative
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 40–41; JAGERSMA 2010: 196–202; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: ###
The nominal case comitative -/da/ is always written with the sign DA,61 which may be derived from the Sumerian noun d a , “side.” It expresses company and can often be translated “(together) with” or “in the presence of.” The comitative attaches to both, animate and inanimate nouns.
In ED IIIb texts the comitative is usually omitted after a vowel and only appears if the preceding word ends in a consonant. It appears that the comitative may have had a similarly reduced form as the dative leading to its omission in writing: e.g., d u b - s a r - d a e - d a - s e 1 2 (HSS 3, 27) vs. n u b a n d a 3 e - d a - s e 1 2 , (TSA 13) “they live with the scribe / inspector”; or a g a 3 - u s 2 - d a e - d a ĝ a l 2 , “(commodities) are present with the soldier” (BIN 8, 377) vs. d a m - g a r a 3 e - d a - ĝ a l 2 , “(commodities) are present with the merchant” (Nik 1, 256). The reduced form is only found in word-final position. The comitative is fully present, e.g., in personal names of the form [–]-d a n u - m e - a , “without [–]” (< lit. “not being with [–]”): e.g., a m a - d a — n u - m e - a , “without a mother,” e - n e - d a — n u - m e - a , “without him/her.”
61 Used as verbal infix, it may however undergo vowel harmony: e.g., Ur Lament, line 135: u r i 2 k i e r 2 - r e b a a n - d i - n i - i b - ĝ a r , “Ur has been given over to tears.”



Introduction to Sumerian 45
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
Finally, the comitative is used to coordinate nouns following the morpheme - b e 2 , which is either identical to the possessive suffix - b e 2 or represents a separate conjunction. Nouns can be coordinated by simple juxtaposition: e.g., a n - k i - a , “in heaven and on earth.” In ED IIIb texts we find the morpheme - b e 2 following the second noun: e.g., i d i g n a b u r a n u n - b e 2 , “Tigris and Euphrates.” In these early texts already, but also later, the comitative can be added: e.g., a b 2 a m a r - b e 2 - d a , “the cow and the calf,” i 7 i d i g n a i 7 b u r a n u n - n a - b e 2 - d a , “Tigris and Euphrates.”
The comitative can also express a reason meaning “because of.” This can be seen with the verb ḫ u l 2 , “to be happy; to rejoice”: e.g., d e n - l i l 2 - l e n i ĝ d a b a m u - d a - ḫ u l 2 , “Enlil rejoiced over the offerings.” Another verb commonly used with the comitative is the compound verb a 2 a ĝ 2 , “to instruct”: e.g., d e n - l i l 2 - d a a - b a a 2 m u - d a - a n - a ĝ 2 , “who instructs Enlil?” (Šulgi G, 8).
6.2.3.3. Ablative
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 41–42; JAGERSMA 2010: 191–96
The ablative case marker -/ta/ is always written with the sign TA. It is only used with inanimate nouns. Three functions of the ablative can be differentiated:
(1) It expresses a direction moving away from a point (local and temporal): e.g., e 2 - t a , “from the house,” u 4 - b e 2 - t a , “from that day, since that time.” In this function it can also express origin: e.g., l u 2 š u š i n k i - t a - m e ( * l u š u š i n - t a - m e ( š ) ), “they are men from Susa.” In economic and legal texts we often find the expression *ki PN.ak.ta, (lit.) “from the place of PN”: e.g., k i b a - a - g a g u r u š t a - t a , “from Bâga, the sheep fattener.” Since the ablative is only added to inanimate nouns, this construction needs to be used.
(2) The ablative also has an instrumental meaning: e.g., ĝ e š t u k u l k a l a - g a - t a , “with the mighty weapon,” or a 2 d n a n š e - t a , “by the power of Nanše.”
(3) A specialized function of the ablative is its use as distributive: e.g., ḫ a r 5 g i ĝ 4 - t a 6 - š e 3 , “(silver) for 6 rings of five shekels each.”
6.2.3.4. Terminative
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 42–43; JAGERSMA 2010: 180–89;
The terminative case is usually written with the sign ŠE3, which is reconstructed as /še/. The terminative can often be differentiated from an adverbiative case (see Chapter 6.2.3.7). After a vowel we find spellings with - a š (also - a š 2 ), - e š (also - e š 2 (ŠE3)), and - u š (also - u š 2 ):
- a š a - n a - a š - a m 3 , “why?”
- a š 2 e 2 l i b i r - r a - a š 2 , “towards the ancient house”



46
-eš
-eš2
- u š e 2 - z u - u š , “towards your house”
-uš2
- š e 3 k a n 4 ĝ e 6 ĝ e p a r - r a - š e 3 , “to the gate of the cloister”
As the examples show, the terminative is usually only used with inanimate nouns and expresses a movement towards a point. Some (compound) verbs, however, use the terminative to mark an animate noun: The compound verb š u — t i , “to receive,” literally means “to bring the hand close towards someone/something”:
nin-a-ne2 sizkur2 ra2-zu-ne2 gu3-de2-a-aš2 ku3 dĝa2-tum3-du10-ge šu ba-ši-ti His lady, the Holy, Ĝatumdu, received from Gudea, his offerings and prayers (Gudea Cylinder A, iv,2)
Another common compound verb is igi~bar, “to look at someone/something”:
Similar to the ablative case marker, the terminative can also be used in a temporal sense: e.g., u4ul-li2-a-še3, “until those days.”
It also may indicate abstract ways of reasoning: e.g., a-na-aš-am3, “why < for what is it?” (see ch. 7.2).



Introduction to Sumerian 47
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
6.2.3.5. Locative
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 39; JAGERSMA 2010: 172–179;
The locative case marker is /a/, or more correctly –’a.62 It may be written -a () or with a syllabogram (see Table 2-1). The case has a wide range of uses. Its primary function is to express a location in space and time. The locative only occurs after inanimate nouns.
Example 6.21 iri-a iri.a
city.LOC
In the city
It usually does not express a movement, unless with some verbs of motion:
Example 6.22
e 2 - a m u - n a - n i - k u 4 (REC 56) e.a mu-nn.a-ni-(n.)kur.Ø
city.LOC VENT-IO-LOC-FPP.to enter.ABS
He brought (the statue) for him into the temple (lit. he made it enter in the house) (Gudea St. E, ix:5; Lagaš II)
The locative -/a/ has a wide range of uses, but primarily may denote a place: e.g., iri-a, “in the city,” uri5ki-ma, “in Ur.” It does not express a movement. As such it also has a temporal connotation: e.g., u4-bi-a, “at that time.” The locative is restricted to inanimate nouns and is complimentary to the dative.
Finally, the locative may mark the material, with which something is built or decorated:
Example 6.23 e2-a
e.a mu-nn.a-ni-(n.)kur.Ø
city.LOC VENT-IO-LOC-FPP.to enter.ABS
He brought (the statue) for him into the temple (lit. he made it enter in the house) (Gudea St. E, ix:5; Lagaš II)
62 JAGERSMA 2010: 172 argues in favor of the glottal stop based on plene spellings in Old Sumerian texts: e.g., i t u g u r u 7 d u b - b a - a , “in the month when the granaries are heaped up” (* itud guru dub-a.a).



48
6.2.3.6. Directive
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 43–44; JAGERSMA 2010: 165–72
The directive case marker,63 which is in form identical to the ergative case (see Chapter 6.2.2.1) occurs only with inanimate nouns. It expresses an indirect or oblique object and thus serves as counterpart (besides the locative in some instances) for the dative. In many instances the directive can be translated by “into, at, on, etc.” It usually denotes a direction into a place. This is shown by the following example, in which the word n a - ř u 2 - a , “stela,” is marked with the directive. Note that indirect object is also referenced in the verbal chain by the prefix b a -:64
Example 6.24
na-řu2-a-be2 izi ba-šum2 nařua-be.e izi.Ø b.a-(n.)šum.Ø stela-POSS3sg-inan.DIR fire.ABS IO3sginan-FPP.to give.ABS
He set fire to this/its stela (Ean. 1, ii 36–37; ED IIIb)
In writing, the -/e/ of the directive contracts with a preceding vowel and may, as in the preceding example, disappear. However, plene spellings do occur:
Example 6.25
ne3 mar-tu ma-da-ne2-e bi2-in-gi4-a ne Martu.a(k).Ø mada-ane.e b.i-n.gi.Ø-a.a force Martu.GEN.ABS land-POSS.DIR OO-FPP.to return.ABS-SUB.LOC
When he returned the forces of the Amorite into his country (Šū-Sîn RIME 3/2.1.4.17, 24–26; Ur III)
In the last example the directive is used differently than in Example 6.24, where the directive is understood as benefactive or recipient. Here, the verbal action is performed not for the land (m a d a - n e 2 - e ), but performed on the land. Therefore, the verbal template starts with the prefix *b.i, which syntactically is interpreted as oblique object (OO) here.65
Example 6.26
ĝešeme-sig-zu-u3 a-ĝe6 i7buranun ku3-ga teš2-ba gun3-gun3-me-en3 emesig-zu.e aĝe Buranun kug.a(k) teš-be.a gun-gun-men force Martu.GEN.ABS land-POSS.DIR OO-FPP.to return.ABS-SUB.LOC
As for your floor planks, you are a flood of pure Euphrates sparkling altogether (Šulgi R, 15; OB)
The plene spelling - z u - u 3 can only be interpreted as a directive here (JAGERSMA 2010: 170).66 As in the case of the ergative, a word ending in a consonant is followed either by a syllabogram (-Ce) or -e alone.
63 It is also sometimes referred to as locative-terminative. 64 This prefix is identical in form to the middle prefix b a - ; see Chapter 12.1.5. 65 See Chapter 14.1.7.3. The oblique object also occurs in idiomatic verbs to mark the semantic object; see Chapter Excursus B:. 66 The same spelling may occur with the ergative.



Introduction to Sumerian 49
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
6.2.3.7. Adverbiative
Literature: ATTINGER 1993: 253–255; MEYER-LAURIN 2012; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: ###
The basic form of the adverbiative is /eš/. As the terminative (Chapter 6.2.3.4) it is often written
with the sign ŠE3 ( ). In contrast to the terminative, however, it is usually preceded by a syllabogram—a Ce sign—, which takes up the final consonant of the preceding lemma (Example 6.27):
Example 6.27
z i - d e 3 - e š 2 “in a righteous manner” zid.eš
While this is the usual way of rendering an adverbiative, other ways exist already in the third millennium and also in the Old Babylonian period: e.g., u 4 - d e 3 - e š - e , “like sunlight,” k u 3 - g e e š - e , “in brilliance,” or z i - d e 3 - e š - e , “in a righteous manner.” A longer form with final /e/ is also attested in spellings such as ḫ u ĝ - ĝ a 2 - a š - š e 3 , “in the manner of a rented worker” (*ḫuĝ.eše). Spellings such as these may indicate that the original postposition was /eše/. The relation to the Akkadian terminative adverbialis -iš, which is attested already in ED IIIb texts from Ebla and in Pre-Sargonic texts, is undeniable, although the direction of borrowing remains elusive.67
Spellings such as s i l i m -ŠE3 do not automatically refer to a terminative. Here, variant spellings (s i l i m - e - ŠE3, s i l i m - m e - e š , s i l i m - e - e š ) and parallels help to clarify its interpretation as an adverbiative: s i l i m - e š 2 , “in a healthy manner.”
While the adverbiative is sometimes not considered a separate case, spellings such as g u 4 - ř a 2 k e 4 - e š , “because of the oxen” (< lit. “in the manner of that of the oxen”; *guř.ak.eš),68 show that it is a case in its own right.
6.2.3.8. Equative
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 44; JAGERSMA 2010: 202–205; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 44–45
The case postposition for the equative -/gen/ is always written with the sign DIM2 (), read - g e n 7 (older also - g i n 7 and - g i m ).69 The final /n/ is often confirmed when followed by an enclitic copula (see Chapter 8): e.g., e - n e - g e n 7 - n a m (Akk. kīma šuātīma), “it is like him/her.”70 The equative is only used with nouns and is not reflected in the verbal phrase. Its function is comparison and as such it is closely related to the use of the enclitic copula.
67 See the discussion in MEYER-LAURIN 2012: 222–36. 68 For this type of construction see Chapter 18.2.4. 69 The same sign also has the nominal reading š i d i m , “architect, etc.” (possibly deriving from š u - d i m 2 , “builder”), and a verbal reading d i m 2 , “to fashion.” 70 Final /m/ is rarely attested: Lugalbanda and the Anzu Bird, line 163: u r 5 - g i m - m a - a m 3 , “it is like this.” Lexical texts preserve the syllabic renderings - g i - i n , - g e - e n , and - g i - i m .



50
Example 6.28
a n - g e n 7 d i m 2 - m a 71 “created like heaven” an.gen dim.a
u r 5 - g e n 7 d u 1 1 - m u - n a - a b “speak so to him/her!”`72 ur.gen dug.mu-nn.a-b
u r i 5 k i - m a - g e n 7 - n a m “it is like that from Ur” Urim.a(k).gen-am
Apart from the genitive, the equative is not preceded by another case. Thus, the equative may express not only likeness, but also nuances thereof: “like in,” “like with,” etc.
Finally, the equative is often found in temporal clauses attached to subordinated verbal forms, and thus shares this function with the Akkadian preposition kīma. In the following example, the subordinated clause is syntactically reliant on a head noun u4-d, “day” > “like the day when ...”:73
Example 6.29
m u n u s k u r - š e 3 i - i m - ĝ e n - n e - e n - n a - g e n 7 Woman, since you came to the mountain. munus.Ø kur.še i-m-ĝen.en-a.gen
71 Note that in this example the sign DIM2 is written twice in sequence. 72 For the demonstrative pronoun ur5 see Chapter 7.5.6. 73 A comparative clause does not make sense here due to the person of the verb.

47 “from the city of our great god”:



56
Text I Building inscription
The first text is a typical royal inscription from the end of the third millennium BCE. It belongs to the socalled building inscriptions. Texts such as these were inscribed on stone tablets and other objects, which were enclosed into foundation boxes. Similar texts were also inscribed on clay cones, which were driven into the walls. The present text originates from Uruk.
These texts follow a certain pattern: – Receiving deity or benefactor – Agent / Subject – Patient / Object – Verb – Additional statements
The most frequent verbal form encountered in such inscriptions is m u - n a - d u 3 , “he built for him/her” (*mu-nn.a-(n.)du.Ø). This statement is often followed by the sentence ki-be2 mu-na-ge4, “he restored for him/her” (*ki-be.e mu-nna-(n.)ge.Ø), lit. “he returned it to its place.”



Introduction to Sumerian 57
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
Text II Building Inscription
The present tablet (MLC 2075), now inscribed on clay, is another example of a building inscription. In this case, it is a much later copy produced by a Neo-Babylonian scribe called Marduk-nāṣir, who found this text in the debris and copied it as part of his education. The colophon on the reverse reads: ki-i pi-i / SIG4 [A]L.UR3.RA / LIBIR.RA ša2 E2.AN.NA / mdAMAR.UTU-URI3-ir / A mib-ni-dištar / ušbal-kit, “according to the wording of an old baked brick from Eanna, Marduk-nāṣir, son of IbniIštar, has copied it.” The copy is faithful: the scribe tried to imitate the older paleography of the source and also indicated the transition of the column by a double ruling after line 4.



58
7. Pronouns
7.1. Independent personal pronouns
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 207–239; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 61–62;
The forms of the independent personal pronoun are close to the forms of the possessive suffix (see Chapter 5.2). In Sumerian, a verbal phrase can refer to the various participants using affixes. e.g., m a - r a - a b - š u m 2 - m u , *mu-r.a-b.šum.e, “he (/e/) will give (š u m 2 ) it (/b/) to you (/ra/).” The verbal suffix /e/ refers to the agent of the transitive verbal action; the infix /ra/ refers to the secondperson indirect object (or dative). The independent personal pronoun behaves like a noun and can therefore be followed by case markers (see Chapter 6) such as the genitive (e.g., ĝ a 2 - k a m , “it is mine”) or the enclitic copula (see Chapter 8).
It usually puts an emphasis on the respective verbal participant.
Independent pronoun
1st sg. ĝ e 2 6 / ĝ a 2 ( - e ) ,
2nd sg. z e 2 , z a - e ,
3rd sg. anim. a - n e / e - n e
,
1st pl. m e - e n - d e 3 ( - e n )
2nd pl. m e - e n - z e 2 ( - e n )
3rd pl. anim. a - n e - n e , e - n e - n e ,
In the above example, the agent can additionally be expressed by a - n e or e - n e , and the indirect object z a - r a , “for you.” Consider the following example:
Example 7.1
m a - r a - a b - š u m 2 - m u “he will give it to you.” mu-r.a-b.šum.e
l u 2 - u 3 d u b m a - r a - a b - š u m 2 - m u “The man will give the tablet to you.” lu.e dub.Ø mu-r.a-b.šum.e
e - n e d u b m a - r a - a b - š u m 2 - m u “He (himself) will give the tablet to you.” ene(.e) dub.Ø mu-r.a-b.šum.e
e - n e d u b z a - r a m a - r a - a b - š u m 2 - m u “He (himself) will give the tablet to you.” ene(.e) za.ra dub.Ø mu-r.a-b.šum.e
The first-person independent personal pronoun is always written with the sign ĜA2. Phonetically the form was either /ĝa/ or /ĝe/ (reading ĝ e 2 6 ). Both in absolutive and ergative position, it can be



Introduction to Sumerian 59
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
written ĝ e 2 6 - e .74 This shows, different to other nouns, that the independent personal pronoun exhibits split ergativity. Common is the use of the dative: ĝ a 2 - r a , “for me”; the comitative: ĝ a 2 d a , “with me”; or the equative: ĝ a 2 - g e n 7 , “like me.” It can also be followed by the genitive, often in combination with the enclitic copula (-/am/): ĝ a 2 - k a m , “it is mine.” The enclitic copula, now in the first person, can also attach to the independent pronoun: ĝ e 2 6 - e - m e - e n , “I myself.”
The second-person pronoun is /za/ or /ze/ and is written either with the sign ZA or the sign ZE2.75 Like the first-person pronoun it is often written z a - e or z e 2 - e , and can be followed by case markers or the enclitic copula: e.g., z e 2 - m e , “you are” (*ze.Ø-me(n)).
The third-person independent pronoun is written a - n e in earlier texts; in later texts it usually is written e - n e . Please note that the independent pronoun is written with the sign NE and not, as the possessive suffix, with the sign NI. This spelling may indicate vowel length: /anē/.

za-ra za-a-ra za-ar za-a-ar
za-da za-a-da
za-še3 za-a-še3

e-ne-da e-ne-še3 e-ne-am3

7.2. Interrogative pronouns
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 57–58; JAGERSMA 2010: 228–31; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 62–63
Questions can be expressed by using a number of pronouns, which behave like nouns and therefore can be appended by possessive suffixes (see Chapter 5.2), case markers (see Chapter 0), and the enclitic copula (see Chapter 8).
Animate a - b a who?
Inanimate a - n a what?
74 Both for the first and second singular forms the sign E may serve as phonetic indicator: ĝe26e and ze4e. 75 In more recent text editions occasionally the reading ze4(-e) can be found for za(-e). ###



60
a - n a - a š why?
Adverbial m e ( - n )
m e - a where?
m e - š e 3 where to?
The interrogative pronouns can often be followed by the enclitic copula (see Chapter 8): e.g., a-naaš-am3, “why?” The form en-na-me-še3 means “until when” (Akk. adi mati). It should be noted that the animate interrogative pronoun contains the inanimate morpheme /b/ while the inanimate pronoun contains the animate morpheme /n/.
The addition of nominal case markers and the enclitic copula adds nuanced meanings: a - n a - a š ( a m 3 ) , “why (lit. for what)?,” a - n a - g e n 7 ( - n a m ) , “how?,” m e - a , “where?,” and m e - š e 3 , “where to?,” and m e - n a - a m 3 , “when?”
Example 7.2
a-ba-ne-gen7 Who is like him? *aba-ane-gen
dumu-ĝu10 a-na nu-zu What does my son not know? *dumu-ĝu ana nu-(n.)zu
7.3. Indefinite pronoun
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 59; JAGERSMA 2010: 231–34; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 63–64
In Sumerian, nouns can be both indefinite and definite, and only the context can provide clarity. Thus, a word like e 2 can mean “the house” or “a house.” Sumerian has an indefinite pronoun, n a m e , “some, any.” It behaves like an adjective and is thus used as an attribute. The pronoun always has a non-specific meaning: k i n a - m e - t a , “from anywhere,” u 4 n a - m e - š e 3 , “until anytime.”
l u 2 n a - m e anyone
n i ĝ 2 n a - m e anything
k i n a - m e anywhere
u 4 n a - m e ever
Very often n a - m e occurs in sentences with a negative verbal form: e.g., n a - m e n u - k u r 2 - r u , “nobody will alter it,” e 2 - a š e n a - m e n u - ĝ a l 2 , “there is no barley whatsoever in the house.”
7.4. Reflexive pronouns
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 58; JAGERSMA 2010: 234–39; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 64–65



Introduction to Sumerian 61
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
The reflexive pronoun consists of the stem /ni/ or /nite/ (for the third-person singular and plural), “self,” followed by the possessive suffixes (see Chapter 5.2). Since this pronoun behaves like a noun, it may be followed by case-markers (see Chapter 6). It is usually written with the sign IM read n i 2 . For the third-person pronoun texts also attest to the spellings n e - t e - and m e : t e (written TE+ME). The latter occurs in earlier texts of the third millennium BCE: e.g., e n - m e : t e n a , “his own lord.”
Reflexive pronoun
1st sg. n i 2 - ĝ u 1 0 myself
2nd sg. n i 2 - z u yourself
3rd sg. anim. n i 2 - t e - ( a - ) n e 2 himself / herself
3rd sg. inan. n i 2 - b e 2 itself
1st pl. ? 76 2nd pl. ? 77
3rd pl. n i 2 - t e - ( a - ) n e - n e themselves
Example 7.3
ni2-ƞu10-še3 to myself *ni-ƞu-še
ni2-zu ƞa2-da mu-da-ab-sa2-e-en You compare yourself to me. *ni-zu ƞa-da mu-e.da-b.sa.en
e2-gal ni2-te-na-ka in his own palace *egal ni-te-ane-ak-a
lugal-ne-te-na his own master *lugal-nite-ane-a(k)
7.5. Demonstrative pronouns and particles
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 49–51; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 66–68; JAGERSMA 2010: 219–28
The demonstrative pronouns can be divided into those that are used as enclitics and those that occur independently.
“this”
near the speaker
“that”
within view of the speaker
“that”
outside the view of the speaker
e še re
ne-n be2
ur5
76 Expected is ni2-me. 77 Expected is ni2-zu-ne-ne.



62
Of these, only / n e ( n ) / and u r 5 are true pronouns.
7.5.1. Enclitic -be2
An enclitic demonstrative pronoun /be/ quite certainly derives from the possessive suffix of the 3rd sg. - b e 2 , “its, relating to something,” for inanimate nouns. While most often it has the meaning “that,” sometimes /be/ marks nouns closer to the speaker and thus favor a translation “this”: e.g., u 4 - b i - a , “on that day,” k i - b e 2 - š e 3 , “for that place/to its place.” Other than the possessive suffix the demonstrative pronoun can also attach to nouns of the animate class: e.g., u r 2 a m a b e 2 - n e - t a , “from these mothers’ lap.” An example k i l u 2 - b e 2 - n e - t a , “from (the place of) these men,” may also contain the personal name Lu-bene.78 Before the locative and genitive postpositions, the final vowel often contracts to /a/: e.g., u 4 - b a , “on this day,” a l a n - b a , “on this statue, of this statue.”
7.5.2. Enclitic -e
The enclitic demonstrative pronoun /e/ (or rather /’e/) appears like the ergative case marker (see Chapter 0). However, it does not contract to a preceding vowel: e.g., m u - s a r - r a - e , “this inscription.” The enclitic is used to refer something that was mentioned before: e.g., a m a r - e a b a - k a m , “Whom does this calf belong to?” (*amar-e.Ø aba.ak-am.Ø).
7.5.3. Enclitic -re or -ri
This (probably) particle usually expresses a temporal (rarely local) distance. In earlier texts it is usually written with the sign URU read re2: e.g., u 4 - r e 2 - a , “in those days,” as at the beginning of the Instructions of Šuruppak:
[ u 4 ] - r e - a u 4 s u d - r a 2 r e - a In those days, in those far remote days, ĝ e 6 - r e - a ĝ e 6 b a d - r a 2 r e - a in those nights, in those faraway nights, [ m u ] - r e - a m u s u d - r a 2 r e - a in those years, in those far remote years.
7.5.4. Enclitic -še
This particle is very rare and restricted to the use with lu2: e.g., u r d u 2 l u 2 - š e l u g a l - z u - u 3 , “Slave, is that man there your king?” (Bilgames and Aga, line 69).
7.5.5. Independent ne(n)
The demonstrative pronoun /ne(n)/ can be used independently in a sentence. It usually corresponds to Akk. annû, “this.” Occasionally the final consonant /n/ appears in writing: e.g., g u 4 - g e n 7 u 4 n e - n a ḫ e 2 - g a z , “May he be slaughtered like an ox on this day.”79 In this example, /ne(n)/ appears to be used as enclitic. Common spellings are n e , n e - e , and n e - e n , which all appear to be variant spellings for the same pronoun. It may also be predicatively as in the example a - n a a m 3 n e - e , “What is this?” Finally, it may also be followed by the enclitic copula: e.g., n e - m e u r d u 2 ḫ a - m e , “it is these who should be slaves” (*ne(n)-me(š)).
78 See the remark k i š i b 3 l u 2 - b e 2 - n e in Ur III documents occurring after several seal references before: “the seals of these men.” 79 Also compare the writing u 4 n e - e n - a , “on this day.”



Introduction to Sumerian 63
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
7.5.6. Independent ur5
A pronoun u r 5 is always written with the sign ḪAR. It occurs often with the equative postposition: e.g., u r 5 - g e n 7 d u 1 1 - m u - n a - a b , “speak thus to him!” According to the royal song of praise Sulge F, several of the king’s duties are mentioned followed by a short refrain: s u l - g e a m a n e 2 - e u r 5 - r e b a - a n - d u 2 , “for (all) that his mother gave birth to Sulge.”

Text III Building inscription
This short building inscription (YBC 2291) dates to the early Old Babylonian period into the reign of the king ANam (or Diĝiram), who ruled Uruk at the end of the nineteenth century BCE.



Introduction to Sumerian 67
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
Text IV Votive inscription
Another common type of inscription concerns votive gifts. The present text is inscribed on a shallow stone bowl. According to the inscription it was the gift of a certain Iltāni, daughter of Ilumbānī. Occasionally, the inscription refers to the object that is gifted to the deity, in this case a n a 4 d i l i m 2 , “a stone dish.” Frequently, the votive gift is for the life of someone (often the king) and oneself. We find here the construction *namti PN.a(k).še.
The verbal form used in this votive inscription is i n - n a - a n - b a , “he/she gave it to him/her” (*inn.a-n.ba.Ø). The noun n i ĝ 2 - b a , “present, gift,” contains the same verbal base b a . This verb is also used (usually written shorter i n - n a - b a ) in seal legends.
Vocabulary
n a m - t i - l life
l a r s a m Larsa
n a 4 d i l i m 2 dish
b a to give, to allott
z a b a l a m Zabalam



68
Text V Seal inscription
An unusually long inscription can be found on an Ur III cylinder seal from Sulge’s reign, which is currently housed in the British Museum (BM 89131). The seal is dedicated to the netherworld god Meslamta’ea. The seal owner is a certain Kilula (written k i - l u 5 - l a ), a throne-bearer, son of UrBagara (written u r - b a - g a r a 2 ). While the texts so far used the verbs d u 3 , “to build,” and b a , “to present,” this text uses the verb d i m 2 , “to fashion.” The inscription ends with a name this seal was given. The seal name is a complete sentence ending in the verbal form g a - a n - t i - i l , “let me live.”
Vocabulary

8. Enclitic copula and copular clauses
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 82–83; JAGERSMA 2010: 677–714; MICHALOWSKI 2004: 49; THOMSEN 1984: 275–78; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 107–12; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 67–68
A copular clause consists of a subject and a non-verbal predicate. The final part of the predicate is usually a copula, which represents an enclitic form that derives from the Sumerian verb m e , “to be.” Instead of the copula, one can also find the independent finite verbal form of the verb m e , although this usually only happens when the verb expresses modality: e.g., ḫ e 2 - m e - e n , “you are indeed” (*ḫa-î-me.en). The pronominal suffixes follow the intransitive conjugation pattern. Note, however, that the third-person singular enclitic form differs slightly.
Enclitic Independent
1st sg. - m e - e n i 3 - m e - e n
2nd sg. - m e - e n i 3 - m e - e n
3rd sg. - a m 3 (ED: - a m 6 ) i 3 - m e
1st pl. - m e - e n - d e 3 - e n i 3 - m e - e n - d e 3 - e n
2nd pl. - m e - e n - z e 2 - e n i 3 - m e - e n - z e 2 - e n
3rd pl. - m e - e š , - m e - e š 2 , i 3 - m e - e š
Copular clauses do not mark tempus. Therefore, d s u l - g e l u g a l - a m 3 , means “Sulge was/is/will be king.” Nonetheless, the independent form usually refers to the past or the future. The enclitic copula is frequently used in appositions, e.g.: e n s i - k e 4 s i p a z i k a l a m - m a - k a m e 2 m u u n - d u 3 , “the city governor – he is the just shepherd of the land – built the house.”
The first-person and second-person singular forms usually can only be differentiated taking the context into account: e.g., u r - s a ĝ - m e - e n can mean both “I am a hero” and “you are a hero”; u r - s a ĝ - z u - m e - e n should only mean “I am your hero.” Both enclitic forms can be abbreviated to -me. This is particularly well attested in texts of the Lagaš II-dynasty. The form - m e can also be an abbreviation of the third-person plural enclitic copula: e.g., (list of persons) followed by i 3 d u 8 - m e , “they are porters.”
In Emesal texts the first-person and second-person singular forms are usually written with the sign DU read - ĝ e n (m e n 3 in older transliterations).80
In Early Dynastic period texts, the third-person singular copula is written with the sign AN only read -a m 6 ( ). The vowel of the third-person copula usually assimilates to a preceding vowel: e.g., e 2 - ĝ u 1 0 - u m , “it is my house,” u ĝ 3 - b e 2 - e m , “it is its people.” If the copula is preceded by a consonant, it may connect to this consonant by using a CVC-sign such as DAM, KAM, LAM, or
80 Note that the same sign is used to write the verbal base ĝ e n / d u , “to go,” and g u b , “to stand,” but also syllabograms - ř a 2 and - ř e 6 ; for the latter see Table 2-1.



Introduction to Sumerian 71
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
NAM: e.g., s i p a z i k a l a m - m a - k a m , “it is the righteous shepherd of the land” (* sipad zid kalam.ak.am).
- d a m e n 3 - b e 2 t a r - r e - d a m , “it is to be investigated”
- k a m a - r a 2 2 - k a m , “it is the second time”
- l a m not attested! (see, e.g., e 2 - g a l - a m 3 , “it is the palace”)
- n a m u r i 5 k i - m a - g e n 7 - n a m , “it is like that of Ur”
The rarely attested forms of the first-person and second-person plural copula can be abbreviated by dropping the last EN.
The third-person plural form - m e - e š can sometimes be abbreviated to - m e only. Instead of the sign EŠ, occasionally scribes write - m e - e š 2 .
The enclitic copula can follow the genitive case, but also the equative, and even dimensional cases such as the terminative and ablative.

9. Adverbs (of manner) and the adverbiative “case”
Literature: Edzard 2003: 69; Jagersma 2010: 189–191; Zólyomi 2017: 68–70; Colonna d’Istria & Sallaberger 2023: 49 and 78
In a sentence, adverbs qualify a verbal statement. Adverbs usually derive from adjectives (e.g., z i d , “righteous”) or non-finite verbal forms.
Sumerian has in principal two ways to express adverbs.
9.1. Enclitic -be2 or bi
The suffix - b e 2 or - b i can mark adverbs. This suffix is likely identical to the inanimate possessive suffix - b e 2 .
Example 9.1
a2 zi-da-ĝu10 gal-be2 tum2-ma a zid-a-ĝu.Ø gal-be tum-a strength righteous-POSS.ABS great-ADV
he is the one greatly fitting my righteous strength (Ninurta’s Journey to Nippur 147)
9.2. Enclitic /eš/
Some modern grammars (in particular JAGERSMA 2010 and ZÓLYOMI 2017) propose an adverbiative ending -/eš/, which is differentiated from the terminative (see ch. 6.2.3.4). This suffix is usually best seen after words ending in a consonant (e.g., zi-de3-eš2 for *zid-eš). The terminative case marker, however, would usually appear directly after the consonant: lugal-zi-še3, “to the righteous king.” Does the preceding word end in a vowel, a differentiation between these two enclitics is less visible. This enclitic was most probably loaned from the Akkadian –iš adverbialis (e.g., damqiš, “in a good way”).
Example 9.2
an ku3-ge zi-de3-eš2 mu-ĝar An kug.e zid.eš mu-(n.)ĝar. sky pure.ERG righteous-ADV VP-...
Pure An established it righteously.
Occasionally this enclitic may be found with substantives as well: e.g., u4-de3-eš2, “like daylight.”
9.3. Combination of both enclitics
Rare and mostly restricted to texts from the 2nd mill. BC is the combination of both enclitics: e.g., gibil-be2-eš2, “anew.”
9.4. Unmarked
Morphologically, an adjective without ending does not constitute an adverb. However, the adjective may qualify the direct object and as such needs to be translated as adverb:
Example 9.3
den-lil2-le igi zi mu-e-ši-in-bar



78
Enlil.e igi zid.Ø mu-e.ši-n.bar.Ø Enlil.ERG eye righteous.ABS VP-IPP
Pure An established it righteously.
The nominal part in compound verbs (see ch. ###) sometimes is qualified by an adjective and thus translated as adverb.
den-lil2-le igi-zi mu-e-ši-in-bar “Enlil gazed upon you in a just manner.” *Enlil-e igi-zid mu-e.ši-n.bar lit. “he opened a just eye towards you”



Introduction to Sumerian 79
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
10. Numerals
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 61–67; THOMSEN 1984: 82–85; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 70–73
Numerals are usually written with numerical notations or number signs. Their Sumerian pronunciation is therefore known from lexical environments only or from non-orthographic texts. Some numerical notations are written (partially) syllabic in ephemeral texts as well: e.g., š a - n a b e 2 for š a n a b i , “two-thirds”; 1/3-ša for š u š š a n a , “one-third.”
10.1. Cardinal numbers
The earliest syllabic spellings originate from Ebla and date to the 24th century BCE. The tablet in question (TM.75.G.2198), an exercise tablet found in the main archive room in Palace G, contains the cardinal numbers 1–10. Number 1 is only indicated by the conventional spelling with the vertical (here slanted) wedge DIŠ, thus logographically. The remaining numbers are written phonetically. Ebla texts are peripheral texts. Hence syllabic spellings may not reflect the pronunciation of numbers in the Mesopotamian core area accurately.
Fig. 5 Syllabically written Sumerian numerals from Ebla, 24th cent. BC (TM.75.G.2198)
Also the later syllabaries contain information on the pronunciation of numerals. Late canonical Ea II, 53–57 provides readings for number 1; entries 125–45 of multiples of the single horizontal wedge up to 9(aš) = ilimmu2 (see



80
Table 10-1). Entries 146–81 deal with readings and meanings of 10 and multiples of 10 up to 9(u). The same part of Ea also deals with the sign DIŠ and its multiples in entries 232–37 and 245–60.83
Sumerian cardinal numbers are based on the principal of finger-counting. The numbers 6–9 derive from adding 5 and the respective number: e.g., i m i n , “7” < * i a + m i n ; ilimmu, “9” < * i a + limmu

Cardinal numbers can be used as attributes and therefore follow the noun they qualify. In economic texts and metrological expressions this order is reversed. If an animate noun is followed by a numeral, the plural morpheme -/ene/ is never used: e.g., a b g a l i m i n , “seven sages.”
Example 10.1
limmu-ne-ne limmu-anene.(ra)
for the four of them



Introduction to Sumerian 83
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
10.2. Ordinal numbers
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 256–60
Depending on the context cardinal numbers may be used in an ordinal sense: e.g., s a 2 - e š 5 - a m 3 , “the third square (of the ziggurat).” Much more common, however, is adding the suffix /kamma/ after a cardinal number (except for one). In Old Sumerian texts, this suffix is still fully written. This occurs very often in economic records, e.g., when individuals bearing the same name receive rations. The second individual is then followed by 2 84- k a m - m a , “the second one.” By the end of the third millennium, the final /ma/ is dropped, if it occurs in word-final position: e.g., u 4 u s s u - k a m , “it is the eighth day.” However, it still is preserved, if the ordinal number is followed by other case elements: e.g., e.g., a - r a 2 - m i n - k a m - m a - a š , “for the second time,” u 4 - i m i n k a m - m a - k a , “on the seventh day.” In the latter example, the ordinal suffix is followed by a genitive case and a locative case.
10.3. Distributive numbers
For distributive numbers the ablative case follows the cardinal number: e.g., l i m m u - t a , “per four.” This construction may also be followed by the enclitic copula.
10.4. Multiplying numbers
To multiply Sumerian uses the noun a - r a 2 , “times”: e.g., a - r a 2 4 - k a m , “the fourth time.”
10.5. Fractions
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 260–66
The fractions one-half, one-third, two-thirds, and five-sixths are usually written with specific number signs (see Chapter 21.1.1). From lexical sources, however, we know their pronunciation.
The Sumerian expression for specific fractions is i g i - n - ĝ a l 2 , “one-nth” (n is a cardinal number). This expression is well-known from economic records, but also mathematical texts. Reciprocal lists dating to the Ur III period and later contain reciprocals whose product is sixty: e.g., i g i - 6 ĝ a l 2 - b e 2 1 0 , “its sixth part is 10.” Grammatically this form behaves like a noun and can be followed by case markers: e.g., i g i - 6 0 - ĝ a l 2 - š e 3 i r i - t a d e 6 - a , “(barley) was taken out from the city as one-sixtieth.” The expression can also qualify a noun. In these instances it follows the noun it qualifies.
The word for the fraction one-half is š u - r u - a , later also š u - r i - a , whose etymology is uncertain except for the word š u , “hand.” It is uncertain whether the numerical notation 1⁄2 represents this word or rather m a š .
The words for one-third and two-thirds are /šuššana/ and /šanabe/ respectively. Both words are loans from Akkadian: šuššān and šinipu. Texts dating to the Old Sumerian and Old Akkadian periods originating from Nippur contain phonetic spellings for both. The word /šuššana/ is written šu2+1 ša-na; the word /šanabe/ is written together with the numerical notation 2: e.g., 2 e 2 ša-nabe2 s a r , “two-thirds plot of house.”
84 Usually written with a slanted version of the vertical wedge: DIŠtenû.



84
Excursus A: Accounting and Bureaucracy
Sumerian is not only the language of scholarship, of myths, hymns to the gods, proverbs, and so forth, but also the language of bureaucracy. One of the most stereotypic genres of Sumerian texts are texts of the state bureaucracy. The following remarks in this chapter will deal, in particular, with the rich documentation of the last century of the third millennium, the so-called Ur III period, with some additional glimpses to earlier and later periods. In general terms, the corpus of more than 100,000 tablets currently known can be divided into a range of sub-categories based on the texts’ content and technical terminology. The subcategories follow DAHL 2020.
10.6. The Grammar of Administrative Texts
The language of economic records is usually rather terse and formulized. Non-finite verbal forms prevail in general.
10.7. Dates
The date formula is usually placed towards the end of a document, often clearly separated from the main body of a text. Texts of the Ur III period use year names that are introduced by the noun m u , “year” ( ). The year name is preceded by the month and the day. The system of month names is rather complex for the Ur III period, but also a help to identify the provenience of a given text.
The month names are introduced by the noun i t u - d , “month” ( ) (see Chapter 22.2.14).
The day can be referred to either by an ordinal number: e.g., u 4 8 - k a m ( n ) or by the expression Month-t a u 4 8 - a m 3 b a - r a - z a l , “from month so-and-so 8 days have passed.” The latter formula can also be used to refer to time ranges: e.g., i t i š u - n u ĝ u n - t a / u 4 1 0 - a m 3 b a - r a - z a l - l a - t a / i t i m i n - e š 3 u 4 1 0 - š e 3 , “from (the time) ” (SAT 3, 1447 obv. 2–4).
10.8. Sealings
There are different types of seal legends attested. For the Ur III period two types stand out. One type are simple name inscriptions, in which the seal owner’s name is followed by his or her patronym or another kinship relation and the title or occupation. Very common is the title d u b s a r , “scribe.”
Servant seals mention the king or a member of the elite and his or her titulature, followed by the seal owner’s name, his or her titulature, and ending in the phrase u r d u 2 - z u (also read a r a d 2
z u and i r 1 1 - z u ), “your servant” ( ).



Introduction to Sumerian 85
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
Example 10.2
i,1 di-bi2-/dzuen Ibbi-Sîn, 2 lugal kala-ga strong king, 3 lugal uri5/ki-ma king of Ur, 4 lugal an-ub-/da limmu2-ba king of the four world quarters: ii,1 gu-du-du Gududu, 2 dub-sar scribe, 3 dumu da-da-ga son of Dadaga, 4 ensi2 the city governor 5 ummaki of Umma, 6 urdu2-zu (is) your servant.
Example 10.3
i,1 di-bi2-/dzuen Ibbi-Sîn, 2 lugal kala-ga strong king, 3 lugal uri5/ki-ma king of Ur, ii,1 dnin-[lil2-ama-ĝu10] to Ninlil-amaĝu, 2 sugal7 ša3 [e2-duru5-ka] messenger of the Eduru, 3 niĝir [gal] chief herald, 4 urdu2-da-ne2-[er] his servant, 5 in-na-[ba] he gave it.
10.9. Archival tags
Tablets were stored on shelves, in baskets, leather sacks, and in jars. Archival texts stored in these places can be associated with archival tags.



86
10.9.1. Tablet container tags
This type of tablet-shaped tag is fairly wide-spread in the Ur III documentation. Tags of this type were formed around a string, whose impression is sometimes still visible on broken examples. One or two holes on the left edge are characteristic for these tags. The text on such tags refers to the types of tablets that were stored in the baskets or containers or gives a rough summary of the contents. In literature they are referred to as p i s a n - d u b - b a -tablets (updated reading b e š e ĝ
d u b - b a ; ), a term that always occurs in the first line. The designation refers predominantly to containers made of reed (g e b e š e ĝ ). Similarly, although much more rare, is the
designation k u š d u 1 0 - g a n , “leather sack” ( ).85
Example 10.4 bešeĝ dub-ba e2-tum saĝ-niĝ2-gur11-ra-kam u3 zi-ga
a-ḫu-we-er
iti maš-da3-gu7-ta iti še-sag11!-ku5-še3 ⸢iti 12⸣-kam [mu ša]-aš-ruki ba-ḫul

Many such tags end in the verbal form ni-ĝal2 (or i3-ĝal2), “is present therein” ( ).86 Like the example above, tags often refer to types of documents such as the following:

The earliest examples of such tags date to the ED IIIb period. Early Dynastic tags are hemispherical lumps of clay with a perforation, which were directly attached to the reed basket holding the tablets. The imprint of the reed basket is still visible on the backside of such tags. Similar to the later examples, these tags start with the term for tablet container or tablet basket (b e š e ĝ d u b ):
Example 10.5 bešeĝ dub siki-ba lu2 dba-ba6-ke4-ne 1 ba 2 ba 3 ba e-ĝal2 AŠ×5
Tablet basket: Wool rations of the people of Baba — one ration, two rations, three rations — are her. Year 5. (DP 29; ED IIIb; Girsu)
[beš]eĝ dub [a]ša5 še mu2-a apin la2 ⸢gid2⸣-da (break) mu aša5 uš gal uru4-a-kam para10-nam-tar-ra [dam lugal-an-da] [ensi2]



88
[lagaški-ka AŠ×n]
Tablet basket: Wool rations of the people of Baba — one ration, two rations, three rations — are her. Year 5. (BIN 8, 389; ED IIIb; Girsu)
10.9.2. Other archival tags
A particular type of label, shaped like a triangular pyramid, was formed around a knot, whose imprint is visible on broken examples. Only the tip of the pyramid bears a perforation. Such labels are only known from Umma and were used for two purposes: (1) to summarize “regular deliveries
for the messengers” (sa2-du11 kas4; ) and (2) to summarize “regular deliveries for the
gods” (sa2-du11 diĝir-re-ne; ) (LAURITO, MEZZASALMA & VERDERAME 2008).
The labels of the first group were attached to containers holding messenger texts (see Chapter 10.12). The text inscribed on them lists the amounts of different commodities that can also be found in the messenger texts. The commodities are characterized as sa2-du11 kas4. The second part contains the administrative details and follows a certain sequence: (a) the person in charge of
escorting the goods (ĝiri3; ), (b) the person responsible for the sealing (kišeb3; ), (c) the date (month name, day, and year name), and (d) one or two officials who “confirm” the
administrative act (ge-n; ).
Example 10.6
A,1 4.1.3 la2-1 sila3 ⸢kaš⸣ / du gur 2 4.1.1 5 sila3 inda3 du gur 3 2 dug dida ⸢saga⸣ 0.0.1 / 5 sila3 4 27 dug ⸢dida du⸣ / 0.0.1 5 sila3 5 1.4.0 dabin gur B,1 4 udu u2 2 0.0.2 5 1⁄3 sila3 1 giĝ4 / i3 3 484 sa šum2 4 8 sila3 8 giĝ4 naga kum 5 3.0.0 gu2 ge 6 sa2!-du11 kas4 C,1 ša3 an-za-gar3 i7/ĝir2-suki 2 ĝiri3 a-du 3 kišeb3 ensi2 4 iti še-sag11-ku5 5 u4.30.kam 6 mu en eriduki / ba-ḫuĝ D,1 lu2-dsu4-an-ka-/ke4 mu-gi-in
Since the labels are dated to either the 29th or 30th day of a given month, they appear to represent the monthly expenditures for the messengers. Only a few officials sealed the tags: Ur-e11-e (
), Lu2-kal-la ( ), and Ur-dnun-gal ( ).
The second group of labels is summarized as “regular deliveries for the gods.” The commodities listed are only animals (sheep and goats). The list of animals is subdivided into groups of animals offered to specific deities and deified kings. Another difference to the labels pertaining to deliveries



Introduction to Sumerian 89
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
for the messengers is the information from whom the animals were delivered. This individual is
always a certain Alulu (ki a-lu5-lu5-ta; ).
The text inscribed on these sealed tags summarized monthly expenditures that are listed on the tablets. It could be shown that after having served as tags, they were fired and eventually archived.
10.10. Receipts
Receipts are the backbone of Ur III bureaucracy. They can be characterized as primary documents, which funnel into the accounts as debits or credits. In fact, for several larger accounts (monthly or annually) primary documents can still be identified (see, e.g., ENGLUND 2003). Primary documents usually bear seal impressions.
10.10.1. Sealed receipts
Receipts that are impressed with the seal of the recipient of a commodity contain the expression
PN š u b a - t i or š u b a - a n - t i , “PN received it” ( ( ) ). It can also be found as a
passive form: (commodity) š u b a - a b - t i , “it was received” ( ). Receipts of the Old Babylonian period often use here the participal form š u t i - a or the Akkadian equivalent namḫarti. The documents follow a strict formulary: (1) commodity; (2) k i PN-t a , “from PN”; (3) PN (i.e., individual who receives the commodity); (4) š u b a - t i , “he received” (or similar), (5) date.



90
Example 10.7 Obverse 1 70 ad6 udu Seventy sheep carcasses, 2 120 ad6 sila4 120 (or 2) lamb carcasses: 3 ĝiri3 sipa-e-ne Via the shepherds, 4 ki du11-ga-ta from Duga, Reverse 1 ur-ku3-nun-na Ur-kununa 2 šu ba-ti received. (blank) 3 mu ša-aš-ruki ba-ḫul Year ‘Šašru was destroyed’.
Seal 1 ur-ku3-nun-na Ur-kununa, 2 dub-sar scribe, 3 dumu lu2-dnin-ĝir2-su son of Lu-Ninĝirsu, 4 sipa na-gab2-tum shepherd of the place for fattening livestock.
(BPOA 7, 2240; Drehem; Ur III)
A similar formulary is also used in loan contracts (see Chapter 19.7). Many receipts of this type also include the expression k i š e b 3 PN, “sealed document of PN” ( ). Documents containing this information may also lack a form of š u — t i . Although rarely attested in Ur III, some documents may also explicitly refer to the sealing act: kišeb3 PN ib2-ra, “PN’s was rolled/impressed on it” ( (PN) ).87
Occasionally, a person different than the recipient of the commodity seals the document. This can be remarked in the text: e.g., kišeb3 i-di3-diškur-še3 / kišeb3 ur-dšara2 dumu-na ib2-ra, “instead of the seal of Iddin-Adad the seal of Ur-Šara, his son, of impressed” (SAT 2, 198 r 1–2).
In addition, there may be other information present such as the origin or destination of the commodities, or their purpose: e.g., še ur5-ra-še3, “as barley of the interest-bearing loan” (see also Chapter 19.7).
Occasionally, another individual is introduced, who is referred to as ĝiri3, (lit.) “foot” ( ). This term is often understood as “conveyor.”
10.10.2. Unsealed receipts
Receipts may also remain unsealed. Occasionally such documents add the remark kišeb3 nu-ub-ra,
“the seal was not rolled on it” ( ), or kišeb3 nu-ra-a, “unsealed document” (
).
10.10.3. Copies of receipts
Copies are often flagged by the expression gaba-ri kišeb3-ba, “copy of a sealed document” (
) or gaba-ri on its own, which may occur in the middle of the text, but often also towards
87 In Old Babylonian legal texts after the list of witnesses we often find expressions such as the following (YOS 14, 343 rev. 6): kišeb3 lu2 ki enim-ma ib2-ra-ra-aš, “the witnesses impressed (their) seals.” ### Ana ittišu



Introduction to Sumerian 91
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
the end or written on the left edge, particularly in Early Old Babylonian archival texts.88 Alternatively, texts also attest to the expression nu-ĝal2 gaba-ri or gaba-ri nu-ĝal2, “un-existing copy (of a sealed document).”89 As the above example shows, the recipient of a commodity impresses his or her seal on the sealed receipt. Copies suggest that they were kept for internal book keeping purposes.
10.10.4. Deliveries
The operational term for deliveries into an institution is m u - k u x (DU) ( ). The NeoSumerian text database BDTNS currently lists almost 8,500 attestations for this term. The term may appear on its own, often written on a separate line, or may be qualified: e.g., mu-kux lugal, “royal delivery,” mu-kux zabar-dab5, “delivery of the cup-bearer,” mu-kux DN, “delivery for DN.” Occasionally we find also the expression mu-kux nu-ub-tuku, “not having a delivery.”
For Ur III documents the reading mu-kux is well established. Editions of Old Babylonian archival texts prefer to read mu-tum2 or just mu-du.
10.10.5. Withdrawals
The technical term used in documents that deal with withdrawals of commodities is either the finite
verbal form b a - z i , “it was withdrawn/booked out” ( ), or the perfective participle of the
same verbal base z i - g written z i - g a , “withdrawn/booked out” ( ). The latter may also
be qualified: z i - g a l u g a l , “royal withdrawal” ( ).
88 Frequent attestations derive from the so-called Isin Craft Archive dating to the Early Old Babylonian period. See, for instance, BIN 9, 40. 89 The Old Babylonian document JCS 34, 170, No. 28 reads on the left edge: GABA.RI ka-ni-ik ba-ab-bi-li / ša dIŠKURna-ṣi-ir il-qu2-u2, “Copy of a delivered sealed document, which Adad-nāṣir took.”



92
Example 10.8 Obverse 1 30 udu 30 sheep 2 sa2-du11 dgu-la — regular offerings of Gula 3 ša3 uri5ki-ma in Ur, 4 20-la2-1 udu 19 sheep, 5 6 u8 6 ewes, 6 5 ud5 5 goats, Reverse 1 ba-ug7 they are dead: 2 mu ur-gi7-ra-še3 Because of the dogs, 3 diĝir-ba-ni sipa ur-gi7-ra Ilum-bānī, the dog herder, 4 šu ba-ti received. (blank) 5 ki na-sa6-ta ba-zi From Nasa it was booked out. 6 iti maš-da3-gu7 Month ‘Gazelle-eating’, 7 mu damar-dzuen lugal year ‘Amar-Suena, the king’.
(NCBT 1368; Ur III; Umma)
10.10.6. i3-dab5 documents
Many texts from Drehem use the expression PN i 3 - d a b 5 , “PN seized” ( ). Texts of this type are usually concerned with livestock. The logogram I3.DAB5 is also used in Old Babylonian administrative texts, e.g. I3.DAB5 i-ba-aš-ši-DINGIR, “seized by Ibašši-ilum” (YBC 4822 obv. 5).

10.10.7. in-la2 documents
A much smaller group of primary documents in the Ur III period is concerned with metals and textiles. The operational term used in these documents for receiving the goods is PN i n - l a 2 , “PN
weighed it” ( ). The same verb (l a 2 ) is also used in sale contracts (see Chapter 19.8). An additional recipient may be introduced with the expression PN š u b a - t i , “PN received.” Occasionally, the person weighing the goods also seals the document, even though there is good evidence for unsealed documents of this type (Fig. ###). The weight is given for each commodity
using the term k i - l a 2 - b e 2 , “its weight” ( ), followed by the weight (see Chapter 21.1.2).

Tablet basket: ‘House’, (tablets pertaining to) debits and credits (concerning) Aḫu-wēr, from the month ‘Eatingthe-gazelle’ to the month ‘Harvest’ — it is two months —, [year: ‘Ša]šru was destroyed’. (NBC 9968; Ur III)
10.11. Accounts
The first example is a balanced account of barley. The first section (lines o,1–5) provides the credits
summarized as saĝ-niĝ2-gur11-ra-kam ( ). Even though no values are added here,
the amount is totaled (šu-niĝin2; ###): or 1697.2.5 5 sila3 gur. Lines
o,6–r,4 contain the debits section, which is framed by the expressions ša3-be2-ta ( ) and
zi-ga ( ), (lit.) “from its middle so-and-so booked out.” The entries in between are the



96
different debits, which are again totaled. In this text they amount to or 1528.0.4 5 sila3 gur.
The debits are substracted from the credits. Since the debits are larger than the credits, the result ( or 169.2.1 gur) is a negative operating balance or deficit referred to as la2-ia3 ine
r,5 ( ). Such deficits or arrears could be transferred to the debits section of the following
account. Arrears were also repaid: la2-ia3 su-ga ( ).
The next section of the text (lines r,6–7) initiates the colophon, which describes the type of text:
niĝ2-ka9 ak / i3-kal-la šabra, “account of Ikala, the overseer” ( ). The colophon is followed by the date.
The second example of an account is similar to the latter. The debits in the first line is
or 114.3.3 2 sila3 še gur. The totaled credits result in
If the value of the credits section is higher than the value of the debits section, then the account
results in a positive operating balance or surplus, which is referred to as diri ( ). A surplus could be entered into the credits section of the following account.
The first information present in the debits section may be the remainder from a previous account,
which is known by the Akkadian loanword si-i3-tum ( ). This term may be also
specified as the remainder of a particular year or the remainder of the accounts (niĝ2-ka9; ). On the well-preserved account NBC 11448 the first line contains the silver amount 1 ma-na 18 1⁄2
