Introduction to Sumerian 99
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
After a short blank space the debits are totaled, šu-niĝin2 4 1⁄3 ma-na 5 1⁄2 giĝ4 la2-2 še ku3-babbar, and qualified as saĝ-niĝ2-gur11-ra-kam.
The individual entries in the debits section cover obv. i,1-11. The commodities listed in the debits and credits sections of the accounts are added silver equivalencies, which are introduced by ku3
be2, “its silver (value)” ( ).
The first section in balanced accounts inform about the debits, which are summarized as saĝ-niĝ2
gur11-ra-kam ( ). This term also occurs on tablet tags (see Chapter 10.9.1).
A deficit or arrears is referred to as la-ia3 ( ).
Primary documents use the expression ugu2 PN ba-a-ĝar, “(commodity) is transferred to the debits
of PN” ( ), to refer to debits that would be included in the first section of the accounts.
The second section of an account is framed by the terms ša3-be2-ta, “from its middle” (
), and zi-ga-am3, “it is withdrawn/torn out” ( ).
The third and final section contains the operating balance.



100
Accounts end in a colophon.
The well-preserved tablet NBC 11448 is a good example for an account of this type. The colophon in the left lower corner of the reverse informs: niĝ2-ka9 a5 ur-ddumu-zi-da dam-gara3 / iti min3-eš3 / mu hu-uḫ2-nu-riki ba-ḫul, “Accounting of Ur-Dumuzida, the exchange agent; Month ‘Doublesanctuary’, year Ḫuḫnuri was destroyed.”
Thereafter follows the credits section, which is framed by the terms ša3-be2-ta, “from its middle” (obv. i,15), and zi-ga-am3, “it is withdrawn” (rev. iii,2). The total that appears before the latter is the total of all commodities listed in this section. Each entry consists of an amount of a commodity (here given with weight units) followed by the silver equivalent introduced by ku3-be2, “its silver
value” ( ). The total of the credits are given in the silver value as well.
The operating balance in this account is a deficit (la2-ia3)
10.12. Messenger texts
Messenger texts list expenditures for the use of messengers or envoys, who appear with different titles and functions:
s u g a l 7 messenger, envoy
l u 2 k a s 4 runner
l u 2 k i ĝ 2 - g i 4 - a – man of the message
ka-us2-sa
l u 2 - ĝ e š t u k u l soldier
r a 2 - g a b a rider
a g a 3 - u s 2 soldier
u3-kul
The texts come from the sites Umma, Girsu, and Irisaĝrig, but not from the capital city Ur. The different origins also result in different text formats and information contained on them. The messenger texts from Umma are very small inscribed with tiny script and list rations for several messengers each. The messenger texts from Girsu are oblong and list rations for different people



Introduction to Sumerian 101
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
who travel from Girsu to the eastern provinces. The Irisaĝrig texts show a higher complexity, often referring to the purpose of the trips: e.g., u 4 BAD3.ANki-t a / k i l u g a l - š e 3 b a - ĝ e n - n a - a , “when he went from Dēr to the royal court” (CUSAS 40, 1240 obv. 3–4), or u 4 BAD3.ANki-š e 3 b a - e - r e - š a - a , “when they went to Dēr” (CUSAS 40, 104 obv. 9).
The messenger texts from Umma are probably the most stereotypical among the known examples. According to the organization of their texts, they can be categorized into eight subgroups. The following rations can be found in messenger texts:
i n d a 3 / n i n d a bread
i n d a 3 d u regular-quality bread
k a š beer
k a š s a g a good-quality beer
k a š d u regular-quality beer
d u g d i d a jars of dida-beer
d a b i n flour
š e barley
i 3 - ĝ e š sesame oil
n a g a potash
š u m 2 onion
s a š u m 2 bundle of onions
k u 6 fish
Beer and bread are counted with capacity units (see Chapter 21.1.3); oil and potash with weight units (see Chapter 21.1.2); fish, onions, etc. are counted discretely.
10.13. Accounts concerning workers
The administrative term for worker is ĝ u r u š ( ); female workers are geme2 ( ). Worker accounts dating to the Ur III period often inform about the amount of workers needed for specific tasks. We find here expression such as 1 2 ĝ u r u š u 4 6 - š e 3 , “twelve workers for six days,” followed by the task. Examples for tasks are:



102
k a š m a 2 - š e 3 g a 6 - ĝ a 2 beer carried to the boat
The accounts also mention the overseers (ugula; ).
10.14. Letters
See Excursus “Letters”
10.15. Secondary Literature



Introduction to Sumerian 103
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
11. Verb I: Introduction
This and the subsequent chapters discuss the finite verbal form or verbal template. Like the noun, each verbal template consists of elements that perform a function in the sentence and which appear at specific positions within the template. This chapter contains introductory notes and a discussion of the different verb classes in Sumerian. Chapter 12 is concerned with the form and function of the marker of the finite verbal form. Chapter 13 discusses the verbal markers for transitive and intranstive finite forms. In Chapter 0 the different verbal infixes are described. Finally, Chapter 14 deals with modality and modal prefixes.
11.1. Tense or aspect ?
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 359; MICHALOWSKI 2004: 39–40; RUBIO 2007: 1334–37
It is not entirely clear whether Sumerian verbs express tense or aspect. A tense locates the verbal action in time (past, present, or future). An aspect expresses an extension of the verbal action and addresses whether the action is completed or is still ongoing. The latter seems preferable since some verbs reduplicate the stem to create one of the two forms. “Present-future” (imperfective or incompletive) forms denote actions that are simultaneous or posterior in relation to a given reference point. “Preterite” (perfective or completive) denotes actions that are anterior relative to a reference point. Cuneiform grammatical texts – most notably the Neo Babylonian Grammatical Texts (NBGT) (published in MSL 4) – differentiate between the Akkadian technical terms ḫamṭu (“quick, swift”) and marû (“slow, fat”), as shown in the following entries (NBGT II, 9–12):
du-ud u 1 1 qa-bu-u2 ḫa-am-ṭu2 Sum. d u 1 1 means Akk. qabû, ḫamṭu-aspect. e MIN ma-ru-u2 Sum. e means Akk. (qabû), marû-aspect. tu-ušt u š a-šab DIŠ ḫa-am-ṭu2 Sum. t u š means Akk. ašābu, Sg. ḫamṭu-aspect. d u r 2 - r u - u n MIN ḫa-am-ṭu2 u ma-ru-u2 Sum. d u r u n means Akk. (ašābu), Pl. ḫamṭu and marû.
It needs to be emphasized that the categories ḫamṭu and marû are based on the understanding of the Sumerian verb by Akkadian speakers.90 Despite different arguments, both ḫamṭu and marû appear to describe not necessarily features of Sumerian, but rather Akkadian verbal forms:
ḫamṭu: iprus Pret. 3.sg. “he cut” (i.e., a short form)
marû: iparras Pres. 3.sg. “he cuts, will cut” (reduplicated middle radical)
These two forms are used in order to refer to the different conjugation patterns in Sumerian; ḫamṭu for perfective and marû for imperfective. The perfective represents an unmarked aspect. The perfective stem is used as the citation form: e.g., ĝ a r , “to place” (not ĝ a 2 - ĝ a 2 ). Since most verbs are regular in terms of their perfective and imperfective stems, there are other means to differentiate the two aspects. Both aspects use different agreement patterns to mark ergative and absolutive for transitive, and absolutive for intransitive sentences: e.g., m u - u n - š u m 2 - m u - u n , “he gave me” (*mu-n.šum.en) vs. m u - u n - š u m 2 - m u , “he will give him” (*mu-n.šum.e). The verbal base is not affected by vowel harmony, but it may affect surrounding vowels as the examples show.
90 RUBIO 2007: 1334 refers to the Sumerian terms l u g u d , “short,” and g i d 2 , “long,” in an Old Babylonian grammatical text.



104
11.2. The Verbal Template
As the noun phrase has a Head noun (see Chapter 5) the verbal phrase has a Verbal base, which is appended by affixes to create a finite verbal form. The following figure intends to demonstrate the basic structure in a simplified configuration.
Modal prefixes (see Chapter 14) may take the first position in the verbal phrase. If the verbal phrase does not express modality (e.g., a wish), a “conjugation prefix” comes first (see Chapter 12). The verbal base is preceded by a pronominal prefix that co-references Agent or Patient depending on the aspect (ḫamṭu or marû). The base is usually succeeded – intransitive verbs may have here a separate marker for present-future – by a pronominal suffix referring to Agent, Patient or Subject (again) depending on the verb’s aspect. Adverbial infixes appear between the conjugation prefix and the pronominal prefix. They refer to adverbial case makers on the noun phrase (see Chapter 6.2.3).
11.3. Verb base and verbal classes
Traditionally Sumerian verbs can be classified into several distinct groups depending on the shape and form of the imperfective base compared to the perfective or primary base. If both are the same in singular and plural, then we are dealing with a regular verb (see Chapter 11.3.1). Most imperfective bases are derived from the perfective base, but there are also a few cases of irregular configurations discussed below (see Chapter 0).
11.3.1. Regular stems (or Affixation stems) (A)
Most Sumerian verbs belong to the group of regular verbs. There are two schools of thought regarding this group of verbs. One school of thought regards these verbs as regular, i.e., the base remains unchanged irrespective of perfective singular and plural or imperfective singular and plural. The only difference between perfective and imperfective forms is expressed by the agreement through pre- and suffixes, and a decision can usually only be made based on the context. Sometimes, verbal forms may be both: e.g., i n - d a b 5 - b e 2 - e n , “I/you seize him/her” or “he/she seized me/you” (*i-n.dab.en).
The second school of thought going back to studies by Yoshikawa argues that any verb has two different stems, one for the perfective, the other for the imperfective aspect. Similar to reduplication of the perfective stem (see Chapter 11.3.2)

Introduction to Sumerian 105
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
Thus, the so-called regular stems are considered by those as affixed stems, i.e., the imperfective or marû stem is followed by a suffix -e.91 Consider the following example:
Example 11.1
m a - r a - a b - š u m 2 - m u “he will give it to you.” mu-r.a-b.šum.e
In this example, an imperfective verbal form, the subject appears as suffix after the verb base š u m 2 , “to give.” Followers of the PERF = IMPERF theory analyse the vowel after the base (written -mu, i.e., vowel harmony) as the third person singular marker for the subject of the transitive sentence (.e). Followers of the affixation theory, however, analyze the imperfective stem as šume (< šum2 + e), and the subject marker as Ø.92
In summary, neither of the two theories can be disproven. What would be needed is a grammatical text or commentary, which clearly refers to a citation form d a b 5 - b e 2 as marû vs. d a b 5 as ḫamṭu. In lack of such evidence, this overview follows the former theory.
Regular or not, this class also contains verb bases that derive from adjectives (see Chapter 5.3): d a ĝ a l , “broad” and “to be(come)/make broad,” g a l , “to be(come)/make big,” s i k i l , “to be(come)/make pure,” m a ḫ , “to be(come)/make august,” g i b i l , “to be(come)/make new,” etc. The pattern for the verb s a r , “to write,” can be mapped as follows:

11.3.2. Reduplicating stems (B)
While both singular and plural stay the same, the imperfective marû base is a (partial) reduplication of the perfective ḫamṭu base. If the ḫamṭu base ends in a consonant, this consonant is omitted in the marû base: perfective n a ĝ > imperfective n a 8 - n a 8 (written NAG.NAG), “to drink,” perfective t a k a 4 > imperfective d a 1 3 - d a 1 3 , “to leave.” The cuneiform signs used for the reduplicated stem
hardly ever differ from the ḫamṭu stem: perfective ĝ a r ( ) > imperfective ĝ a 2 - ĝ a 2 ( ), “to put, place.” It may also be preserved in the first syllable only: e.g., perfective t e - e n > imperfective t e - e n - t e , “to cool off.” If the perfective stem ends in a vowel, then the whole syllable is reduplicated. If the perfective stem has two syllables, then the first syllable is reduplicated and voiced: e.g., perfective t u k u > d u 1 2 - d u 1 2 , “to have.”
sg. pl. sg. pl.
perf. a a k u 4 97- r k u 4 - r
imperf. a-a a-a k u 4 - k u 4 k u 4 - k u 4
Note that can also be found. This reduplication happens for different reasons: the ergative or absolutive express plurality or need to be emphasized in terms of their size, strength, or importance. The reduplication may also reflect a durative or iterative character of the verbal action (compare Akkadian tan-stems).

This sign is not used in Early Old Babylonian and later texts any longer to write the verb “to enter.” It does not, however, become obsolete and can be used, e.g., as syllabic sign lil in Akkadian texts of much later periods. For the verb “to enter” texts use KU4 (= KWU 636; ), which is still differentiated from the sign TU (= KWU 635; ). Later on, both signs converge. The sign inventory YBC 4615 clearly differentiates between KWU 635 and KWU 636. There is yet another sign that indicates the verb “to enter”: KWU 634 ( ). This sign is used, for instance, in stone inscriptions such as Gudea’s statue inscriptions ( ). Gudea’s Cylinder inscription, however, use in the same context KWU 147.

11.3.3. Alternating or extending stems (C)
Apart from the regular verbs, whose imperfective stem may or may not have an affixed -/e/, only a few verbs have an extended imperfective stem. The imperfective stem of the verb e 3 , “to come/bring forth,” is e 3 - d . Another common verb is t e or t i , “to come close,” whose imperfective stem is t e - ĝ or t i - ĝ : e.g., ḫ u - m u - r a - a b - t e - ĝ e 2 6 , “may he/she bring it close to you” (* ḫa-mu-r.a-b.teĝ.e). As the example shows, the appended consonant is only visible, if an affix is added after the stem.
sg. pl. sg. pl.

imperf. a+ a+ t e - ĝ t e - ĝ

11.3.4. Suppletive stems (D)
Sumerian irregular verbs, i.e., verbs which do not fall into any of the former categories, are few, but frequent verbs. They may have a different imperfective stem and/or different plural stems. Among the most frequent verbs is the verb with the perfective stem d u 1 1 - g , “to speak, to say.” Only the perfective singular stem is d u 1 1 - g written with the sign KA. The imperfective stem and both plural stems are /e/. As a purely vocalic stem, /e/ often merges with preceding morphemes and appears as - n e or - b e 2 : e.g., m u - n a - b e 2 , “he/she will speak to him/her” (*mu-nn.a-(b.)e.Ø). The verb also has a separate stem d i when it is used for an imperfective participle.
sg. pl. sg. pl.
perf. a b d u 1 1 - g e
imperf. b b e e d i
Example 11.2
enim zi mu-na-an-du11 enim zid.Ø mu-nn.a-n.dug.Ø word righteous.ABS VP-3sgIO-3sgA.to say.3sgP
She spoke a righteous word to him.
šum2-me-eb in-na-ne-eš šum-me-b i-nn.a-n.e.eš to give-1plIO-3sgP VP-3sgIO-3sgA.to say.3sgA
They said to him, “give it to us!”
Like the verb a k , “to do,” the verb d u 1 1 - g is often used as auxiliary verb: e.g., š u d a ĝ a l d u 1 1 - g , “to multiply, to enlarge” (lit. “to say/make a wide hand”).99
Somewhat like the pattern of d u 1 1 - g is the verb u š 2 , “to die; to kill.” However, on a graphemic level, the stem is usually not differentiated. The imperfective stem and the plural stems can also be written with the sign BAD3 ( ) read u g 5 or with a reduplicated UŠ2.UŠ2 (compare the verb
t u š , below). In Old Babylonian Sumerian there seems to be rather a semantic differentiation between the singular base u š 2 , “to die,” and a singular(!) base u g 5 , “to kill.”

Among the irregular Sumerian verbs are three, whose perfective stem is written with the sign DU. They show different degrees of irregularity: The intransitive verb ĝ e n , “to go,” has four different stems. On the graphemic level, however, the differences are minimal. The perfective and imperfective plural bases both use a reduplicated sign DU written on top of each other: e.g., m u u n - š i - r e 7 - e š , “they came to him” (*mu-n.ši-ere.eš); i 3 - s u 8 - b e 2 - e n - d e 3 - e n , “we will go” (*i-sub.enden).

perf. a c ĝ e n e - r e 7
imperf. b d d u s u 8 - b
The sign DU is also used for the verb g u b , “to stand; to put” Here, the stem is only altered in the plural of both perfective and imperfective. Note that the sign used is identical to the perfective and imperfective plural stems of the verb ĝ e n , “to go”: i 3 - s u 8 - g e - e š , “they stood” (*i-sug.eš), vs. i 3 - s u 8 - g e - n e , “they stand” (*i-sug.ene).
sg. pl. sg. pl.

imperf. a b g u b s u 8 - g
Slightly more complicated is the situation with the verb d e 6 / t u m 2 , “to bring.” Here, in fact, we can differentiate between two verbs (see SALLABERGER 2005). The verb with the perfective stem t u m 2 means “to bring” when the object can move on its own. Examples concern people, animals, and boats: e.g., d a m - n e 2 b a - t u m 2 - m a - a , “when he brought his spouse” (ba-n.tum.Ø-a.a).

11.3.5. Reduplicated verb bases
Both the perfective base as well as the imperfective base can be reduplicated. Reduplicating the verbal base may indicate that the direct object is considered in the plural (unmarked or marked) or the verbal action is repeated. This can be compared to the meaning of the Akkadian tan-stem. Reduplicated perfective verbs of the reduplicating class, however, are difficult to spot except in the case of the verb ĝ a r , whose imperfective base is ĝ a 2 - ĝ a 2 , but reduplicated perfective base is ĝar-ĝar.

Introduction to Sumerian 115
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner
Text VI Votive inscription
A verb that is frequently found in votive inscriptions is the compound verb a—ru, “to dedicate.” Dedicated to the protective deity Lama is this small bead with a vertical perforation by a certain Hala-Baba.



116
Text VII Votive inscription
With only 28 mm in height the stone carver incised eleven lines on this agate bead. The bead is dedicated to goddess Ninlil by the Isin ruler Lipit-Eštar. The inscription is composed in the firstperson singular. The main verb of the inscription does look like a third-person singular form, but the context implies that it must be a first-person singular form: *a.Ø mu-nn.a-V.ru.Ø.
The epithets for Ninlil contain two phonetic variants: saĝ—du occurs here instead of saĝ—du3, “to create,” and tu2 is used instead of tu-d, “to give birth.”
The inscription contains a temporal clause consisting of u 4 - d , “day,” a subordinated sentence, and the locative. In Sumerian, any finite sentence can be made a noun by adding a nominalizing morpheme - a . The verbal phrase in line 9, i - n i - i n - ĝ a r - r a - a , is thus analyzed *i-ni-(e)n.ĝar.Øa.a (CONJ-LOCV-ERG.BASE.ABS-NOM.LOCN), “on (the day) that he (or I) established it.”



Introduction to Sumerian 117
Version 2023/24 Klaus Wagensonner

12. Verb II: Marking the Finite Verbal Form
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: ###; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 145–162;
A finite verbal chain is usually marked by one of several prefixes, which were traditionally referred to as “conjugation prefixes.” Texts 1–5, above, contained already finite verbal forms. Texts 1 and 2 are building inscriptions ending in the verb m u - n a - d u 3 , “he/she built it for him/her” (*munn.a-(n.)du.Ø); Text 3 contains the verb i n - n a - a n - b a , “he/she presented it to him/her” (*i-nn.an.ba.Ø); and the verb in Text 4 is m u - n a - d i m 2 , “he/she fashioned for him/her” (*mu-nn.a(n.)dim.Ø). In these examples /mu/ and /i/ appear first in the verbal chain, but note that these prefixes may be preceded by another set of prefixes, the so-called modal prefixes (see Chapter 14).
Recent grammatical overviews (JAGERSMA 2010 and ZÓLYOMI 2017) locate the prefixes discussed here at different positions between the slots taken by the modal prefixes and those by the adverbial infixes: – finite-marker prefixes /i/, and /a/ or /al/; – coordinator prefix /nga/ – ventive prefix /m/ – middle voice prefix /ba/ As a matter of clarity, these different prefixes are discussed here together.
This chapter and the examples presented herein anticipate the marking of Agent and Patient on the transitive verb as well as the Subject on the intransitive verb (see Chapter 13). All these functions are expressed by the slot before and after the verbal base. For transitive verbs the position of the switches depending on the aspect of the verb (perfective or imperfective). To demonstrate the structure, it suffices here to only indicate the pre- and suffixes of the third person singular animate. In the intranstive verb the subject is indicated by the pronominal suffix after the base. The slot before the base remains empty. For the third person the suffix is .Ø: e.g., i 3 - ĝ e n , “he went” (*iĝen. Ø), i 3 - d u , “he goes” (*i-du.Ø).
The structure of the transitive verb is summarized in the following graphic:
If the verb is perfective, the agent (A) is marked by the Final Pronominal Prefix (FPP) before the base. The patient or direct object (P) is indicated by the pronominal suffix (PS). For the imperfective, these positions are switched. The third person animate FPP is *n. or *b. if it agrees with inanimate noun. The PS is .Ø, if it plays the role of patient (i.e., in the perfective aspect), but
BASE Perfective
BASE Imperfective
FPP PS
A
P
P

120 CHAPTER 12. VERB II: MARKING THE FINITE VERBAL FORM
.e, if it is an imperfective verb. Note, that proponents for Affixation class verbs (see Chapter 11.3.1) argue for the PS .Ø in both perfective and imperfective forms.
12.1.1. Finite-marker prefix /i/
The prefix /i/ as a marker for finite verbal forms has a rather neutral function and does not add to the meaning of a sentence. If occurring without any other prefixes, it is usually written with the sign NI read i 3 : e.g., i 3 - d u , “he walks.” The writing with I ( ) is rare. Old Sumerian texts, e.g., those from ED IIIb Girsu, use the spelling E, if followed by a syllable that contains the vowel /e/ or /a/: e.g., e - n e - š u m 2 - m u , “he will give them” (DP 581 r. iii,4).102 It seems that not all modal prefixes precede the finite marker (see Chapter 14).
If the prefix is followed by other prefixes, we often find the spelling i m - , i n - , or i b 2 - (also i b : ). For these also exist plene writings such as i 3 - i m - , i 3 - i n - , and i 3 - i b 2 - (also i - i b (2)):
Example 12.1
i 3 - i n - d a b 5 he/she seized it. i-n.dab.Ø
Examples
i 3 - i 3 - d u - u n I/you walk.
i n - i n - š i - d u he/she walks to him/her.
i 3 - i n - i 3 - i n - n a ĝ he drank it.
i b 2 - i b 2 - d a b 5 they (collective) seized it.
i 3 - i b 2 - i 3 - i b 2 - ĝ a l 2 they are present.
i m - i m - d a b 5 he is seized.
i 3 - i m - i 3 - i m - ĝ e n he/she walked there.
i m - m i - i m - m i - d u 8 he/she held.
i m - m a - i m - m a - ĝ e n he/she went there.
The prefix does not appear, if it is followed by a prefix of the type CV- such as m u - , m a - , b a - , or b i 2 - . However, it is required if it is followed by a consonantal cluster such as /mma/, /mmi/, /nna/, /nni/, etc.
102 i is used, if it is followed by syllables containing vowels /i/ or /u/.



CHAPTER 12. VERB II: MARKING THE FINITE VERBAL FORM 121
12.1.2. Marking a state – the prefix /al/
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 111–112; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 96–97; ZOLYOMI 2017: ###.
Some grammatical overviews discuss the prefix /a/ or /al/ as allomorphs of the finite marker /i/ (see previous subchapter). This prefix expresses (1) a state, (2) a state that results from a previous action, or (3) present-future verbal forms expressing an action. In bilingual environments Sumerian verbal forms starting with this prefix frequently correspond to Akkadian stative forms.
The form /al/ (often written with the sign AL) is used when there is no other prefix before the stem: e.g., a l - d u 1 0 , “it is sweet/good” (*al-dug.Ø). A form AL.TIL, “it is complete/finished,” for instance, is found in colophons up to the first millennium BCE. Although the prefix is not negated in the earlier evidence, the artificial environment of colophons allows for a spelling NU AL.TIL for Akkadian ul qati, “(the Vorlage) is not complete(ly copied).”
If between the prefix and the verbal base other prefixes appear, we find a number of additional spellings in writing. The spellings a n - and a b - are the most common. In Example 12.2, a n precedes a dimensional infix. If a n - appears directly before the verbal base, /n/ represents a syncopated locative infix (Example 12.3; see Chapter 14.1.7.1). If the spelling a b - appears directly before the verbal base, /b/ represents a syncopated locative infix (Example 12.4).

Example 12.3
e2 niĝ2-gur11-ra an-ku4 e niĝ-gur.a a-n(i)-ku.Ø
magazine-LOC VP-LOC1-to enter.ABS
It is entered in the magazine. (CUSAS 20, 190 r 2–3; oAkk)
Example 12.4
para10 ... nam-nun-da-ki-ĝar-ra ab-du3-a parag.Ø ... Namnundakiĝar.a a-b(i)-du.Ø-a magazine-LOC VP-LOC2-to enter.ABS
The pedestals, which had been erected there in Namnundakiĝara. (Enmetena 1, ii:39–41; ED IIIb)



122 CHAPTER 12. VERB II: MARKING THE FINITE VERBAL FORM
Note: Example 12.2 is an advanced example! The verbal form k i a n - n a - a ĝ 2 , “he loves him,” qualifies the noun l u g a l and is thus subordinated by adding a suffix - a after the finite verbal form. This suffix nominalizes the verbal form. As newly created noun the subordinated clause can take case markers, the plural suffix, a possessive suffix, or the enclitic copula.
12.1.3. Coordinator prefix /nga/
The next position in the Sumerian verbal template may be used by the coordinator prefix /nga/, which connects two clauses by adding this prefix to the last verb in the chain. In doing so, it renders a meaning “A, and also B,” or negated “neither A, nor B.” The coordinator prefix only follows the finite-marker prefix. Spellings like i n - g a - or i 3 - g a - are the most common. They may also be preceded by some modal prefixes: e.g., a l n a - a n - g a - a m 3 - m i - i n - d u 1 1 , “he too desired it” (al.Ø na-î-nga-m-bi-n.dug.Ø).
Example 12.5
g a l m u - z u g a l i 3 - g a - t u m 2 - m u He knows big (things) and can bring forth big (things) gal.Ø mu-b.zu.e gal.Ø i-nga-b.tum.e as well
a l a n - e u 3 k u 3 n u z a - g i n 3 n u - g a - a m 3 This statue is of neither silver nor lapis lazuli. alan-e.Ø u kug.Ø nu zagin.Ø nu-i-nga-me.Ø
12.1.4. Ventive prefix /m/
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 497–511; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 151–156 (slot 4);
The ventive prefix /m/ (or /mu/; see JAGERSMA 2010) occurs in third position in the relative order of the verbal prefixes. It appears before any dimensional infixes (including the middle prefix /ba/). The ventive expresses proximity to the speaker. Like the Akkadian ventive it can denote a movement towards the speaker with respective verbs: e.g., i m - ĝ e n , “he came here”; i m - t a e 1 1 , “he brought it down from there.” Its presence can also mark proximity of the verbal action: e.g., m u - n a - d u 3 , “he built it (here) for him/her.”
The ventive prefix can be combined with preceding prefixes and has the following forms:
Examples
i m - i m - d a b 5 *i-m-b.dab.Ø
i 3 - i m - i 3 - i m - ĝ e n he/she walked here.
i m - m i - i m - m i - d u 8 he/she held.
i m - m a - i m - m a - ĝ e n he/she went there.
The form /m/ with preceding finite marker, written i m - , i 3 - i m - , and rarely a m 3 - , can occur directly before the intransitive stem: e.g., i m - e 3 , “he came forth” (*i-m-e.Ø or *i-m(u)-e.Ø), i 3 i m - ĝ e n , “he came” (*i-m-ĝen.Ø).



CHAPTER 12. VERB II: MARKING THE FINITE VERBAL FORM 123
If this form occurs directly before a transitive (imperfective) stem, the inanimate absolutive marker does not appear in writing: e.g., i m - ĝ a 2 - ĝ a 2 , “he is placing it” (*i-m-(b.)ĝaĝa.e), i m - l a 2 - n e , “they are hanging it” (*i-m-(b.)la.ene).103
The same spelling also occurs before the inanimate pronominal prefix (/b/), which forms part of some dimensional infixes. We find forms starting i m - d a - , i m - š i - , or i m - t a - :
Example 12.6
i m - d a - t u š he sat together with them (collective) *i-m-(b.)da-tuš.Ø
i m - d a - m u 2 he let it grow together with it *i-m-(b.)da-(n.)mu.Ø
i m - š i - ĝ e n he came for it *i-m-(b.)ši-ĝen.Ø
i m - t a - s i k i l - e - n e they are cleaning (the house) with it *i-m-(b.)ta-(b.)sikil.ene
The form /mu/ written with the sign MU appears before a cluster of two consonants. This includes defective spellings that drop the syllable-final consonant (/mu/ instead of /mun/) in the following two pairs of examples:
Example 12.7
m u - u n - ĝ a r he placed it *mu-n.ĝar.Ø
m u - ĝ a r he placed it *mu-(n.)ĝar.Ø
m u - u n - n a - n i - i b 2 - g e 4 - g e 4 he/she answers him/her *mu-nn.a-ni-b.gege.e
m u - n a - n i - i b 2 - g e 4 - g e 4 he/she answers him/her *mu-nn.a-ni-b.gege.e
The form /mu/ of the ventive is also attested before the first-person (/’/) and second-person personal pronouns – as part of a dimensional infix and before the base –, and the local infix /e/. It is assumed that /mu/ becomes /mū/ in these environments:
Example 12.8
ĝ e 2 6 a - n a m u - u 3 - d a - z u what have I learned from you? *ĝe ana.Ø mu-e-da-’.zu.Ø
Plene spellings may be written either m u - u 3 - ( ) or m u - u 8 - ( ):
Example 12.9
ḫ u - m u - u 8 - d a - a n - t i may he/she dwell with you!
103 Please note that JAGERSMA 2010 does not reconstruct the pronominal prefix in this case or in the combination i m d a - , i m - š i - , or i m - t a - .



124 CHAPTER 12. VERB II: MARKING THE FINITE VERBAL FORM
*ḫa-mu-e.da-n.til.Ø
Additional two environments of the ventive form /m/ are the spellings i m - m a - ( ) and
i m - m i - ( ). Both configurations start with the above-mentioned finite marker and the ventive prefix /m/. These two prefixes are followed by /ba/ and /bi/ respectively, whose initial consonant assimilates to the ventive. The morpheme /ba/ is used as middle prefix (see Chapter 12.1.5), which is identical in form and position to the third-person inanimate dative infix (*b.a):
Example 12.10
i m - m a - š u m 2 - m u he gives it to it (i.e., statue) *i-m-b.a-(b.)šum.e
i m - m a - ĝ e n he went there *i-m-ba-ĝen.Ø
The morpheme /bi/ is the third-person inanimate oblique object. While /a/ in /ba/ is the dative case, /i/ in /bi/ is the directive (also referred to as locative 3). /bi/ is used for both animate and inanimate nouns, which are marked with the dative case marker /ra/ and the directive case marker /e/ (or locative case /a/) respectively. In the verbal phrase, the oblique object refers to animate (marked with the dative) and inanimate nouns (marked with the locative or directive):
Example 12.11
l u 2 - r a m u - n a - a n - š u m 2 he gave it to the man *lu.ra mu-nn.a-n.šum.Ø
l u 2 - r a m u - n i - u s 2 he seated (lit. made follow) him/her next to the man *lu.ra mu-nn.i-(n.)us.Ø
When the oblique object is inanimate, then the noun phrase is either marked with the locative case /a/ or the directive case /e/:
Example 12.12
ĝ e š m a 2 b i 2 - i n - d u 8 he caulked the boat *ma.e bi-n.du.Ø
m a 2 d e n - k i - k a b i 2 - i n - d u 8 he caulked the Enki’s boat *ma Enki.ak.a bi-n.du.Ø
The difference to the indirect object is that the oblique object is not the recipient of a verb, but rather the verbal action is performed on it. In the first sentence in Example 9.8, the man is the recipient of the object that is given to him and hence the dative is used in the verbal phrase. In the second example the man is not the recipient, but the action is performed on him.
This prefix /m/ also occurs before the locative infix /ni/ in two different spellings: m u - n i - and m i - n i - , the latter being the result of vowel harmony and probably only an orthographical variant.



CHAPTER 12. VERB II: MARKING THE FINITE VERBAL FORM 125
12.1.5. Middle Prefix /ba/
Literature: ZÓLYOMI 2017: 156–162 (slot 5); SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 97–98
The prefix /ba/ can occur at the beginning of a verbal chain but can be preceded by modal prefixes (see Chapter 1), by the finite marker /i/ (or its allomorph /a/), and the ventive prefix /m/. If occurring on its own, the prefix is written with the sign BA. After the ventive we find the spellings i m - m a or a m 3 - m a - (*î-m-ba- or *â-m-ba-). It shares these spellings and the position within the verbal template with the indirect object infix for inanimate nouns (see Chapter 14.1.3).
Examples
b a - b a - a - ĝ a r it was placed on it.
i m - m a - i m - m a - ĝ e n he/she went away.
am3-ma
e - m a - 104 e - m a - ř e 6 it was brought there.
The prefix /ba/ marks the middle voice. It is used when the verbal action or verbal state affects the grammatical subject of a sentence. This particular function can be demonstrated in the following example:
Example 12.13
l u g a l b a - u g 5 - g e - e š The kings have died. lugal.Ø ba-ug.eš
m u - u g 5 - g e - e n I (or he) have killed you.105 mu-V.ug.en
Typical middle verbs are (compound) verbs that express motion without a change of location, but also words of grooming:
Example 12.14
s a ĝ s i g b a - š i - ĝ a r He lowered the head towards it (i.e., word). saĝ sig.Ø ba-b.ši-(n.)ĝar.Ø
š u - n e 2 b a - n i - i n - z i He raised his hands. šu.ane.Ø ba-ni-n.zig.Ø
In intransitive sentences, if the grammatical subject is affected by the verbal action, the prefix /ba/ can express a passive meaning. This can be observed, for instance, in year names:
Example 12.15
m u m a 2 - g u r 8 d n i n - l i l 2 - l a 2 b a - a b - d u 8 Year: the barge of Ninlil was caulked.
104 This spelling occurs in Old Sumerian texts. 105 In texts of the third millennium, one would expect here the base u š 2 . There seems to be a semantic shift in the Old Babylonian period that differentiates between an intransitive base u š 2 , “to die,” and a transitive base u g 5 , “to kill.” See ATTINGER 2011.



126 CHAPTER 12. VERB II: MARKING THE FINITE VERBAL FORM
mu.Ø magur Ninlil.a(k).Ø ba-b(i).du.Ø106
m u ( ... ) l u g a l - e b a d 3 m a - d a m u - d u 3 Year: the king built the wall of the land. mu.Ø lugal.e bad mada.a(k).Ø mu-(n.)du.Ø
The middle prefix /ba/ has also an indirect reflexive meaning. A single participant can function as grammatical subject and either as indirect object, beneficiary, or as goal in the case of motion verbs. The compound verb š u — t i , “to receive,” is another example of a middle verb.
Example 12.16
g u 3 - d e 2 - a - a š 2 š u b a - š i - t i Gudea accepted it from him. Gudea.še šu.e ba-n.ši-(n.)ti.Ø
The subject of the sentence can also serve as beneficiary in the meaning “for himself, for herself” (JAGERSMA 2010: 490–93). For the reflexive meaning of the Middle prefix the verbal template does not use the Indirect Object (IO). If in the second sentence in Example 12.17 someone else were be beneficiary, the verbal template would be i n - n a - d e 6 (or similar), (*i-nn.a-(n.)de.Ø). Hence, the Middle prefix used in this function can refer to both animate and inanimate subjects:
Example 12.17
ĝ e š g u - z a b a - g u b He set up for himself the chair. guza.Ø ba-(n.)gub.Ø
k u 3 e n s i 2 - k e 4 b a - d e 6 The ruler took the silver for himself. kug.Ø ensik.e ba-(n.)de.Ø
A further function of the Middle prefix occurs with intransitive verbs of motion. Here, it expresses a separative meaning:
Example 12.18
b a - ĝ e n he went forth *ba-ĝen.Ø
i m - ĝ e n he came *i-m-ĝen.Ø
Verbal forms may also be marked with the Middle prefix to indicate that the subject is changing the status to what the verb expresses (JAGERSMA 2010: 493–94).
Example 12.19
m a 2 - b e 2 b a - s u This boat sank. *ba-su.Ø
The morpheme /ba/ is also used as the inanimate indirect object (analyzed *b.a): “for it.” It agrees with a directive case marker on the noun. The following two examples show the agreement with inanimate and animate nouns:
106 Note, that the /b/ before the base is a syncopated Oblique Object (OO) marker: b i 2 > b . Finite forms of the verb d u 8 are always constructed with the inanimate OO marker. The active statement “he caulked (a boat)” is expressed bi2-in-du8.



CHAPTER 12. VERB II: MARKING THE FINITE VERBAL FORM 127
Example 12.20
a l a n - e e n i m i m - m a - š u m 2 - m u He gives the message (lit. word) to the statue. alan.e enim.Ø i-m-b.a-(b.)šum.e
l u 2 - r a d u b i 3 - n a - š u m 2 - m u He gives the tablet to the man. lu.ra dub.Ø i-nn.a-(b.)šum.e
If /ba/ is used as middle prefix, it cannot be followed by the inanimate third-person dative infix. Spellings such as **b a - b a - š u m 2 - m u or **i m - m a - b a - š u m 2 - m u are impossible.



128 CHAPTER 13. VERB III: TYPES OF “CONJUGATIONS”
13. Verb III: Types of “Conjugations”
Literature: SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 101–07
Sumerian finite verbs have four conjugation patterns, two for the intransitive and two for the transitive verb. Important for all patterns is the status of the final pronominal prefix (FPP) and the pronominal suffix (PS) in the verbal phrase. The following table contains an overview of the four different patterns:
Perfective Imperfective
Intransitive
i3-dib *i-dib.Ø
“He passed by”
i3-dib-be2 *i-dib.e(d).Ø
“He (will) pass(es) by”
Transitive
in-dib *i-n.dib.Ø
“He made pass by”
ib2-dib-be2 *i-b.dib.e
“He make pass by”
All forms take a third person singular as subject or agent. The full form of the imperfective intransitive suffix is only revealed if followed by a vowel: e.g., i 3 - d i b - b e 2 - d e 3 - e n , “I/you will pass by” (*i-dib.ed.en).
BASE Perfective
BASE Imperfective
FPP PS
A
P
P
A
VP
BASE Perfective
BASE Imperfective
PS
S
ed
INTRANSITIVE
TRANSITIVE



CHAPTER 13. VERB III: TYPES OF “CONJUGATIONS” 129
In the transitive forms, the prefix directly preceding the base is referred to as Final Pronominal Prefix (FPP). It needs to be contrasted from the Initial Pronominal Prefix (IPP), which introduces the dimensional infixes (see Chapter 0). For the pronominal suffix there are two sets to distinguish, which only differ in the third-person singular and plural:
Set A Set B 1st sg. -/en/ -/en/ 2nd sg. -/en/ -/en/ 3rd sg. -/Ø/ -/e/107 1st pl. -/enden/ -/enden/ 2nd pl. -/enzen/ -/enzen/ 3rd pl. -/eš/ -/ene/
The suffixes in Set A agree with the Subject of the intransitive verb (see Chapter 13.1.2), and the Patient of the transitive perfective conjugation (see Chapter 0). Hence these suffixes agree with the absolutive case.
Example 13.1
mu-un-na-gub-be2-eš mu-nn.a-gub.eš VP-DAT3sg-to stand.S3pl
They stood there for him.
The suffixes in Set B agree with the Agent of the transitive imperfective conjugation (see Chapter 13.1.3).
107 Note that in case an affixation class of verbs is preferable over a regular class of verbs (see Chapter 11.3.1), then the Pronominal Suffix for the Agent in the imperfective form is .Ø.



130 CHAPTER 13. VERB III: TYPES OF “CONJUGATIONS”
13.1.2. Intransitive (and passive) pattern
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 81–83; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 126–31; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 103–04
We have seen this pattern already in the discussion of the enclitic copula and the independent use of the verb m e , “to be” (see Chapter 8). In an intransitive sentence the subject of the verb is unmarked (i.e., absolutive). In this pattern, the subject is co-referenced by a pronominal suffix after the verb base. The slot for the pronominal prefix remains empty. For the verb d i b , “to pass by,” this pattern looks as follows:
Form Analysis (*) Meaning
1st sg. i 3 - d i b - b e 2 - e n i-dib.en I pass(ed) by 2nd sg. i 3 - d i b - b e 2 - e n i-dib.en you pass(ed) by 3rd sg. i 3 - d i b i-dib.Ø he/she passes/d by 1st pl. i 3 - d i b - b e 2 - e n - d e 3 - e n i-dib.enden we pass(ed) by 2nd pl. i 3 - d i b - b e 2 - e n - z e 2 - e n i-dib.enzen you (pl.) pass(ed) by 3rd pl. i 3 - d i b - b e 2 - e š i-dib.eš they pass(ed) by
Since intransitive sentences only have one participant, the difference between imperfective and perfective forms is not always easy to spot. However, imperfective forms often add a suffix /ed/, which appears directly after the imperfective base and before the Pronominal Suffix. Some verbs, however, which have an explicit imperfective stem such as the suppletive verb ĝ e n / d u , “to go,” have no attestation for this suffix. The forms of the explicit marking of the imperfective are given in the following table:108
Form Analysis (*) Meaning
1st sg. i 3 - d i b - b e 2 - d e 3 - e n i-dib.ed.en I (will) pass by 2nd sg. i 3 - d i b - b e 2 - d e 3 - e n i-dib.ed.en you (will) pass by 3rd sg. i 3 - d i b - b e 2 i-dib.e(d).Ø he/she (will) pass(es) by 1st pl. i 3 - d i b - b e 2 - d e 3 - e n - d e 3 - e n i-dib.ed.enden we (will) pass by 2nd pl. i 3 - d i b - b e 2 - d e 3 - e n - z e 2 - e n i-dib.ed.enzen you (will) pass by 3rd pl. i 3 - d i b - b e 2 - d e 3 - e š i-dib.ed.eš they (will) pass by
The following notes on the orthography of the pronominal suffixes also apply to the transitive conjugation patterns, except for the third-person singular suffix.
The first-person and second-person suffixes are often written with the sign EN ( ), rarely also
written -e n 3 ( ) and -e n 6 ( ). If the verbal base ends in a consonant, then a syllable sign frequently takes up the final consonant: e.g., n u - u n - k u 4 - r e - e n 6 , “I did not enter there” (*nui-n(i)-kur.en); m u - u n - p a 3 - d e 3 - e n , “he/she chose me/you” (*mu-n.pad.en); i 3 - g i 4 - i n , “I returned” (*i-gi.en). It is also common that the vowel of the suffix assimilates to the vowel of the verb base (-a n and -u n ): e.g., m u - n a - ĝ a 2 - ĝ a 2 - a n , “I place it for him” (*mu-nn.a(b.)ĝaĝa.en). In texts of the Gudea period, but also later, the suffix may be shortened to /e/, since syllable-final consonants tend to be omitted in writing: m a - r a - d u 3 - e , “he will (or: I will) build it for you” (mu-r.a-(b.)du.e(n)).
108 Or a suffix /d/ in case verbs of the regular class have an affix /e/ in their imperfective stem; see Chapter 11.3.1.



CHAPTER 13. VERB III: TYPES OF “CONJUGATIONS” 131
The third-person suffix in the intransitive verb is Ø.
The plural forms are identical to the transitive perfective suffixes. The first-person and secondperson suffixes are rarely attested outside the lexical corpus. Both contain the person marker /en/: e.g., i m - š i - r e 7 - e n - d e 3 - e n , “we went to him” (*i-m-(n.)ši-re.enden); i m - m a - n i - i b - k u 4 e n - d e 3 - e n , “we brought it in” (*i-m-ba-ni-b.ku(r).enden). The suffix may also be shortened to d e 3 - e n . After verbs containing the vowel /u/ the initial vowel of the suffix may be adjusted: e.g., i b 2 - š u m 2 - m u - u n - d e 3 - e n , “we will give it” (*i-b.šum.enden).
The second-person plural suffix is /enzen/. Before a nominalizing -/a/ it is sometimes written -e n z a - n a (see Chapter 18).
The third-person suffix is /eš/ is usually written with the sign EŠ ( ), but often also -e š 2 ( ): e.g., i n - š i - r e 7 - e š , “they went to him” (*i-n.ši-ere.eš). After verbs that contain /u/, the vowel of the suffix assimilates:; m u - n a - a n - š u m 2 - m u - u š , “they gave him to her” (mu-nn.a-n.šum.eš). It is important to note that in earlier texts, in particular, the final /š/ may drop.
The intransitive pattern can be summarized as follows:
13.1.3. Transitive imperfective pattern Literature: EDZARD 2003: 83–87;
Sumerian uses two different configurations to mark the perfective or imperfective in the transitive sentence. The main difference to the intransitive pattern is the use of pronominal prefixes and suffixes to agree with the agent and the patient respectively. In terms of suffixes, the differences to the intransitive pattern are minimal and only concern the third-person singular and plural suffixes. In the transitive imperfective pattern the pronominal suffix after the imperfective stem agrees with the sentence’s agent or ergative: e.g., i b 2 - ĝ a 2 - ĝ a 2 - a n , “I/you place it” (*i-b.ĝaĝa.en). The pronominal prefix directly before the base agrees with the patient.
VP
BASE Perfective
BASE Imperfective

enzen

1 pl

3 pl



132 CHAPTER 13. VERB III: TYPES OF “CONJUGATIONS”
The following table focuses on the singular and plural forms of the pronominal suffixes agreeing with the ergative. The absolutive is in all instances a third-person inanimate singular (/b/):
Form Analysis (*) Meaning 1st sg. i b 2 - d a b 5 - b e 2 - e n i-b.dab.en I seize it 2nd sg. i b 2 - d a b 5 - b e 2 - e n i-b.dab.en you seize it 3rd sg. i b 2 - d a b 5 - b e 2 i-b.dab.e he/she seizes it109 1st pl. i b 2 - d a b 5 - b e 2 - e n - d e 3 - e n i-b.dab.enden we seize it 2nd pl. i b 2 - d a b 5 - b e 2 - e n - z e 2 - e n i-b.dab.enzen you (pl.) seize it 3rd pl. i b 2 - d a b 5 - b e 2 - n e i-b.dab.ene they seize it
For the spelling and examples of the various suffixes see the preceding section on the intransitive verb.
The third-person singular suffix is /e/. In writing, the suffix is often written with the syllable sign that incorporates the preceding consonant. It is usually not written after a verb base ending in a vowel: e.g., i b - b e 2 , “he says it” (*i-b.e.e); š u b i 2 - i b - u r 3 - a , “whoever effaces (the inscription)” (*šu.Ø b.i-b.ur.e-a).110
The third-person plural suffix is /ene/. If the verb base ends in a consonant, it is often written -Ce n e , otherwise only -n e . It may also be influenced by vowel harmony: e.g., i n - š u m 2 - m u - n e , “they give him” (*i-n.šum.ene).
In the transitive imperfective pattern, the FPP agrees with the sentence’s patient. The prefix is not always clearly written and may even be optional, and hence there is not a full consensus on some of the forms. The singular forms are identical to the transitive perfective pattern (see Chapter 0). The plural forms, however, are only rarely attested:
Form Analysis (*) Meaning
1st sg. m u - d a b 5 - b e 2 mu-’.dab.e111 he seizes me 2nd sg. mu-e.dab.e he seizes you 3rd sg. anim. mu-n.dab.e he seizes him/her 3rd sg. inan. mu-b.dab.e he seizes it 1st pl. mu-me.dab.e he seizes us 2nd pl. ? he seized you (pl.) 3rd pl. mu-nne.dab.e he seizes them
The first-person plural prefix is /me/, which is used both as initial pronominal prefix before the dimensional infix as well as (very rare) as final pronominal prefix before the base.
The third-person plural prefix is /nne/.112 In contrast to the other final pronominal prefixes, this prefix is not used to mark the agent of a transitive perfective form or an oblique object: e.g., ḫ a
109 Note that in case the regular verbs are in fact perfective stems followed by /e/ (see Chapter 11.3.1), the imperfective base is /dabe/ and thus the pronominal suffix is .Ø. 110 For subordinated clauses, see Chapter 18. 111 Or *mu-’.dabe.Ø; see footnote 109. 112 The /nn/ is considered parallel to the Initial Pronominal Prefix; see JAGERSMA 2010: 339–41. SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 106 suggest /ne/.



CHAPTER 13. VERB III: TYPES OF “CONJUGATIONS” 133
m u - r a - n e - š u m 2 - m u , “he must give them to you” (*ḫa-mu-ra-nne.šum.e).113 In this example, the prefix /nne/ occurs after the indirect object prefix /ra/. Compare a form such as m u - n e š u m 2 - m u , “he gives it to them” (*mu-nne-b.šum.e). After the Old Akkadian period the prefix of choice is /b/: ḫ a - m u - r a - a b - š u m 2 - m u , “he must give them to you” (*ḫa-mu-ra-b.šum.e).
The imperfective is used for any continuing action or state in the present and future. However, incomplete verbal actions or states can also happen in the past and may, for instance, describe circumstances happening.

134 CHAPTER 13. VERB III: TYPES OF “CONJUGATIONS”
13.1.4. Transitive perfective pattern
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 87–9; ZOLYÓMI 2017: 126–7
In general, in a transitive perfective form, the pronominal prefix directly before the base (also referred to as Final Pronominal Prefix) agrees with the agent or ergative and the pronominal suffix after the base agrees with the patient or absolutive of the sentence.114 This agreement pattern works well with the singular forms. In the plural forms, however, the ergative is referenced both before and after the base. Since the slot after the base is taken by the pronominal suffix that agrees with the ergative, the absolutive cannot be cross-referenced in the perfective plural forms.
The following table focuses on the singular and plural forms of the pronominal affixes agreeing with the ergative. The absolutive is in all instances a third-person singular, thus only marked by a Ø-morpheme.
Form Analysis (*) Meaning 1st sg. m u - d a b 5 mu-’.dab.Ø I seized it 2nd sg. m u - e - d a b 5 mu-e.dab.Ø you seized it 3rd sg. anim. m u - u n - d a b 5 mu-n.dab.Ø he/she seized it 3rd sg. inan. m u - u b - d a b 5 mu-b.dab.Ø it seized it/him 1st pl. m u - d a b 5 - b e 2 - e n - d e 3 - e n mu-’.dab.enden we seized it 2nd pl. m u - e - d a b 5 - b e 2 - e n - z e 2 - e n mu-e.dab.enzen you (pl.) seized it 3rd pl. m u - u n - d a b 5 - b e 2 - e š mu-n.dab.eš they seized it
The first-person pronominal prefix was probably a glottal stop, which is not reflected in writing, but its presence can be deduced from plene writings and the full writing of the ventive prefix /mu/ (instead of /m/), the oblique-object prefix /bi/ (instead of /b/), or the locative infix /ni/ (instead of /n/). Furthermore, the context is an important indicator as well. Text 7, for instance, ends in the verb a m u - n a - r u , which usually would be translated “he dedicated for him/her,” but the context of the inscription makes a first person plausible (*a.Ø mu-nn.a-’.ru.Ø). Up to the Gudea period, the first-person prefix is ignored entirely. Later texts sometimes use plene writings to indicate the first person. Scribes insert vowel signs ( - a - , - i 3 - , and - u 3 - ) corresponding to the vowel in the preceding syllabic sign:
Example 13.2
š u z i m a - r a - a - ĝ a r I faithfully executed it. šu zid.Ø mu-e.ra-’.ĝar.Ø
š a 3 k u 3 - ĝ u 1 0 b i 2 - i 3 - p a 3 I have chosen him in my pure heart. šag kug-ĝu.e bi-’.pad.Ø
The plene writings indicate that the first-person pronominal prefix lengthens the preceding vowel. Probably by the time of Gudea, the original glottal stop contracted to the preceding vowel.
114 Please note that the final pronominal prefix may alternatively agree with the oblique object (see Chapter 14.1.4): e.g., n a m - e n m u - a k - k e 4 , “he let him exercise the lordship” (*nam-en.Ø mu-n.ak.e). Here, the /n/ before the verb base agrees with the oblique object. The non-causative construction would assume a /b/ before the base agreeing with the absolutive.



CHAPTER 13. VERB III: TYPES OF “CONJUGATIONS” 135
The second-person pronominal prefix was /e/. It contracts with a preceding vowel, which leads to a lengthening of that vowel. This contraction is sometimes indicated in plene writing. Scribes used vowel signs that correspond to the preceding vowel ( - a - , - i 3 - , and - u 3 - ). Later texts tend to write an explicit /e/. This may indicate that there was a change in pronunciation of this prefix. (Jagersma 2010: 335–336):
Example 13.3
m u - e - ĝ a r you placed it. mu-e.ĝar.Ø
m u - u 3 - ĝ a r you placed it. mu-e.ĝar.Ø
The third-person animate prefix is /n/.
The third-person inanimate prefix is /b/.
The patient is only reflected in the singular forms, since the slot after the verbal stem is already taken by a pronominal suffix that agrees to the ergative. The following table contains the pronominal suffixes, which agree with the patient. The plural suffixes are identical to the suffixes used for the ergative. In forms use a third-person singular ergative:
Form Analysis (*) Meaning
1st sg. m u - u n - d a b 5 - b e 2 - e n mu-n.dab.en he seized me 2nd sg. m u - u n - d a b 5 - b e 2 - e n mu-n.dab.en he seized you 3rd sg. anim. m u - u n - d a b 5 mu-n.dab.Ø he seized him/her 3rd sg. inan. m u - u n - d a b 5 mu-n.dab.Ø he seized it 1st pl. m u - u n - d a b 5 - b e 2 - e n - d e 3 - e n mu-n.dab.enden he seized us 2nd pl. m u - u n - d a b 5 - b e 2 - e n - z e 2 - e n mu-n.dab.enzen he seized you (pl.) 3rd pl. m u - u n - d a b 5 - b e 2 - e š mu-n.dab.eš he seized them
Both the first-person and second-person singular suffix have the form /en/. In writing, the sign EN is usually not just added after the base. Very often a syllabogram takes up the final consonant of the preceding verb base. The suffix is also influenced by vowel harmony.

13.1.5. Imperfective passive forms Literature:
In theory, a passive statement puts focus onto the direct object and demotes the original agent. Therefore, the passive is derived from a transitive form. The verbal action is viewed from the viewpoint of the object or the affected entity. WILCKE (1990: 488–98) proposed that Sumerian has a passive voice. Such passive forms would resemble imperfective forms, which omit the agent, and replace the imperfective base by a perfective one. ATTINGER (1993: 195–7) suggested that the ###
Several Sumerian verbs are ambitransitive, i.e., they have both an intransitive and transitive meaning. A verb, whose basic meaning is intransitive. KARAHASHI (2009) differentiates between two groups of ambitransitive verbs. One group concerns verbs that express a change of location, and where the intransitive subject becomes the transitive object in a causative construction. The other group are those verbs
e.g., g u b , “to stand” (intransitive) and “to make stand” (transitive); k u 4 , “to enter” (intransitive” and “to make enter” (transitive). We will revisit the transitive usage in the discussion of causative constructions derived from intransitive sentences (see Chapter 14.1.7.4).
13.1.5.1. The Middle Prefix /ba/ as passive marker
We have seen in Chapter 12.1.5 that the Middle Prefix b a - (MED) can render passive voice. This function of the prefix likely developed from its use as IO of inanimate nouns, via reflexive use, and middle voice. It does, however, only render the passive of a verbal action, and not of a verbal state. The following three pairs of examples derive from year names and technical expressions in Ur III ephemeral texts:
Example 13.4
mu ma2-gur8 maḫ ba-ab-du8 mu.Ø magur maḫ.Ø ba-b(i).du.Ø115 year.ABS barge august.ABS MED-OO.to caulk.3sgS
Year: the august processional boat was caulked.
In this example, a year name of the Ur III period (i.e., Šū-Sîn 8), the verb has to be understood as a passive form. The /b/ before the verb base does not refer to the subject of the passive, but is a syncopated OO prefix referring to an imaginary quay. Some year names, such as this one, are attested both in passive and active voice:
115 Note, that the /b/ before the base is a syncopated Oblique Object (OO) marker: b i 2 > b . Finite forms of the verb d u 8 are always constructed with the inanimate OO marker. The active statement “he caulked (a boat)” is expressed bi2-in-du8.



CHAPTER 13. VERB III: TYPES OF “CONJUGATIONS” 137
Example 13.5
mu dšu-dzuen lugal-e ma2-gur8 maḫ bi2-in-du8 mu.Ø Šū-Sîn lugal.e magur maḫ.Ø b.i-n.du.Ø
year.ABS PN king.ERG barge august.ABS OO-3sgA.to caulk.3sgP
Year: Šū-Sîn the king caulked the august processional boat.
The following pair contains a LOC2 infix (see Chapter 14.1.7.2) referring to the locative on m a 2 , “boat.” The first example is again rendered in passive voice.
Example 13.6
(commodity) m a 2 - a b a - a - s i ma.a ba-e-si.Ø boat.LOC MED-LOC2-to fill.3sgS
(Commodity) was filled on the boat.
Example 13.7
(commodity) m a 2 - a b i 2 - s i ma.a b.i-(n.)si.Ø boat.LOC LOC2-3sgA.to fill.3sgP
He filled (commodity) on the boat.
The third pair, another year name (Amar-Suen 2), does not use any locative infix:
Example 13.8
mu ur-bi2-lumki ba-ḫulu mu Urbilum.Ø ba-ḫulu.Ø
year GN.ABS MED-to destroy.3sgS
Year: Urbilum was destroyed.
Example 13.9
mu damar-dzuen lugal-e ur-bi2-lumki mu-ḫulu mu Amar-Suen.a(k) lugal.e Urbilum.Ø mu-(n.)ḫulu.Ø
year PN king.ERG GN.ABS VEN-3sgA.to destroy.3sgP
Year: Amar-Suen the king destroyed Urbilum.
The passive form using the Middle Prefix may already be attested as early as the ED IIIa period.
13.1.5.2. /b/ as passive marker ?
A second form of passive derives from a syncopated form of the prefix /b/ referring to the directive.
There is also good evidence for modal forms that are best translated as as passive statements. Indicative examples come from curse formulae:
Example 13.10
gu4-gen7 u4 ne-na ḫe2-gaz gud.gen ud nen.a ḫe-i-(n-)gaz.Ø bull.EQU day that.LOC MOD-VP-LOC1-to smash.3sgS
May he be slaughtered like a bull on that very day!



138 CHAPTER 13. VERB III: TYPES OF “CONJUGATIONS”
am-gen7 a2 ḫuš-na ḫe2-dab5 am.gen a ḫuš-ane.a ḫe-i-(n-)dab.Ø ram.EQU arm terrible.POSS.LOC MOD-VP-LOC1-to seize.3sgS
May he be seized like a ram by his terrible arms!
The context of these two parallel lines suggest precative statements.
Example 13.11
mu-ne2 kišeb3-ta ḫe2-em-ta-ĝar mu-ane.Ø kišeb.ta ḫe-i-m-b.ta-ĝar.Ø name-POSS.ABS seal.ABL MOD-VP-VEN-IPP.ABL-to place.3sgS
May his name be removed from (his) seal!
13.1.5.3. /a/ as passive marker
The prefix /a/ (or a l - directly before the verb base) expresses a state (see Chapter 12.1.2). It has been argued that this prefix can mark passives in Sumerian texts from the north such as Nippur and Isin. This does not necessarily mean that the prefix is only used for passive statements:
Example 13.12
ur-dnuska-ra an-na-ab-šum2-mu Ur-Nuska.a(k).ra a-nn.a-b.šum.e PN.DAT VP-IPP.IO-3sgP.to give.3sgA
He will give it to PN (NRVN 1, 259; Ur III; Nippur)
KEETMAN (2017: 120) warns that the attestations, which come mainly from terse economic contexts, may not suffice to provide a definite conclusion whether or not the prefix marks passives.
The passive of a verbal state is expressed by the prefix /b/ before the verb base.
Instructive is the following example, which contains a passive form that is negated: nu-um-šum2, “it is not given on there.” The spelling nu-um- before the base shows that
Example 13.13
tukum-be2 a-ša3 šu-na nu-um-šum2 tukum-be ašag.Ø šu-ane.a nu-i-m-b(.i)-šum.Ø if field.ABS hand-POSS.LOC NEG-VP-VEN-LOC2-to give.3sgS
ki na-me-a apin-la2 ga-ba-ab-dab5 ki name.a apin-la.Ø ga-ba-b.dab
place INDEF.LOC rented field.ABS COH-MED-3sgP.to seize
If the field is not given into his hand, I shall seize a rented field anywhere. (MVN 11, 168; letter; Umma; Ur III)



CHAPTER 13. VERB III: TYPES OF “CONJUGATIONS” 139
The passive is derived from a transitive verb, which refers to the direct object.
Another interpretation
Consider the following two parallel statements, which include reason clauses with the head nouns b a r and m u (see Chapter 18.2.5):
Example 13.14
bar še-be2 nu-da-su3-su3-da-ka bar še-be.Ø nu-i-(n.)da-susu-ed.Ø-a.ak.a outside barley-POSS.ABS NEG-VP-IPP.COM-to repay-IMPF.3sgS-NOM.GEN.LOC
Because he would not repay that barley (lit. that barley will not be repaid with him).
Example 13.15
mu nu-da-su-su-da-še3 mu nu-i-(n.)da-susu-ed.Ø-a.ak.še
name NEG-VP-IPP.COM-to repay-IMPF.3sgS-NOM.GEN.TERM
Because he would not repay it.
In both examples the imperfective base s u 3 - s u 3 or s u - s u is followed by the imperfective particle /ed/ and the NOM /a/. The translation favors an active voice. If interpreted active in a grammatical sense, the agent should be marked: **-(b.)susu-ed.e-a. This interpretation is not impossible (KEETMAN 2017: ###). However, it appears that the subordinated clause is a passive voice and še, “barley,” is the subject.
This and similar examples can be interpreted differently. One interpretation assumes the morpheme before the base as Oblique Object (syncopated /bi/). The base would be followed by the Pronominal Suffix .Ø, which refers to the subject m a 2 , “boat.” This is the interpretation followed by JAGERSMA 2010: 418–19.
13.1.5.4. /i/ as passive marker ?
JAGERSMA (2010: ###) refers to this type of passive as dynamic passive. Its stative version uses the general verbal prefix /i/ and the intransitive verb pattern (see Chapter 13.1.2). Such forms can be



140 CHAPTER 13. VERB III: TYPES OF “CONJUGATIONS”
differentiated, however, by the use of a transitive verb base (e.g., š u m 2 , “to give”). It has been observed that Old Akkadian texts from Adab show parallel statements, either with the prefix /i/ (e.g., e - n a - š u m 2 , “it is given”) or the prefix /a/ (e.g., a n - n a - š u m 2 , “it is given”; see below), in contexts where they appear to be understood as passive statements.116 There has been criticism on this theory, e.g., by KEETMAN 2017, arguing that a prefix marking the passive should only be used for a passive form or other uses should be clearly marked. Thus, a form e - n a - š u m 2 should not mean both, “he has given you him” or “it was given to him.”
13.1.6. Summary of agreement patterns for the intransitive and transitive verbal forms

CHAPTER 13. VERB III: TYPES OF “CONJUGATIONS” 145
Text VIII Building inscription
The inscription on this Early Dynastic cone dating to the reign of the Lagaš ruler Enmetena can be divided into three sections. The first part (i 1–8) follows the formulary of building inscriptions. Lines i 9–ii 3 contain a statement that establishes the personal god of Enmetena, Šulutul. Of historical importance is the remainder of the text, which helps as a synchronism between the royal houses of Lagaš and Uruk.



146
14. Adverbial infixes
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 92–111; JAGERSMA 2010: 381–486; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 112–24
14.1.1. Introduction
The previous chapter dealt with the agreement patterns for the Subject in the intransitive verb as well as the Agent and Patient in the transitive verb. These participants of the Sumerian sentence are agreed upon through the Final Pronominal Prefix (directly before the base; FPP) and the Pronominal Suffix (PS) after the base. The FPP can agree with the Agent of the transitive perfective verb and the Patient of the transitive imperfective verb.
Elements of the finite verbal form can also agree with adverbial case markers on the noun phrase (see Chapter 6.2.3). In the verbal chain these can be referred to as adverbial infixes. In the texts treated so far, we already encountered the dative infix, which marks the indirect object. We saw the forms m u - n a - d u 3 , “he/she built for him/her” (*mu-nn.a-(n.)du.Ø), in building inscriptions, m u - n a - d i m 2 , “he/she fashioned for him/her” (*mu-nn.a-(n.)dim.Ø), as well as i n - n a - b a , “he/she presented to him/her” (*i-nn.a-(n.)ba.Ø), and a m u - n a - r u , “he/she dedicated for him/her” (*a.Ø mu-nn.a-(n.)ru.Ø), in votive inscriptions. In all these forms the element - n a (analyzed *-nn.a-) is an adverbial infix.
Adverbial infixes appear in a certain sequence after the “conjugation prefix(es)” (see Chapter 12), but before the Agent/Patient marker in the transitive verbal chain. In case the verbal prefix chain contains various adverbial infixes, the following graphic shows their sequence:
Four positions can be differentiated:
• indirect object / dative prefix (see Chapter 14.1.3) or oblique object (see Chapter 14.1.7.3)
• comitative prefix (see Chapter 14.1.4)
• ablative prefix (see Chapter 14.1.6) or terminative prefix (see Chapter 14.1.5)
• local prefixes (see Chapter 14.1.7)
The first prefix is preceded by the Initial Pronominal Prefix (IPP). The following example demonstrates the agreements between the prefixes in the verb and nominal case markers:
Example 14.1
ensi2-ke4 dinana-ra kisal-a para10 mu-na-ni-in-ĝar ensik.e Inanak.ra kisal.a parag.Ø mu-nn.a-ni-n.ĝar.Ø
city governor.ERG DN.DAT courtyard.LOC dais.ABS VP-DAT-LOC-ERG.to place.ABS
The city governor founded the dais in the courtyard for Inana.
Indirect Object
IPP FPP
Comitative
Terminative
Ablative
Locative 1
Locative 2



CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES 147
The verbal template in this example uses two adverbial prefixes: the dative prefix and the locative prefix. The dative prefix is here introduced by the third-person animate IPP. It agrees with the dative postposition after the divine name Inana. The locative 1 infix agrees with the locative postposition after the noun k i s a l . It should be noted, however, that co-referencing case-markers in the verbal prefix chain is not obligatory. In theory, a finite verbal chain may contain up to four adverbial infixes in a row. Although there are examples with three infixes, forms with one or two infixes are common.
14.1.2. Initial Pronominal Prefix (IPP)
The set of infixes is preceded by a pronominal prefix, the so-called Initial Pronominal Prefix (IPP). Only the first adverbial prefix is preceded by the pronominal prefix. The prefix specifies the person, gender, and number of the case the infix is referring to. Morphologically, the following forms can be differentiated:
Analysis 1st sg. /’/ 2nd sg. /e/ 3rd sg. anim. /n/ 3rd sg. inan. /b/ 1st pl. /me/ 2nd pl. (/ene/) 3rd pl. /nne/
The first-person prefix is /’/. In the third millennium, it only occurs after the ventive prefix /mu/.117 Plene writings after the texts dating to Gudea’s reign show that the vowel of /mu/ was lengthened when the glottal stop was lost: e.g., k u 3 n u - m u - d a - a - t u k u , “I do not owe you silver” (lit. “you did not have silver with me”) (*kug.Ø nu-mu-’.da-e.tuku.Ø); m u - u 3 - d a - g u b , “he stands with me” (*mu-’.da-gub.Ø).
The second-person prefix is /e/. Apart from the indirect object (/ra/) and oblique object (/ri/), the prefix usually contracts with the preceding vowel. Up to the Gudea texts the contraction is usually ignored. Thereafter, plene spellings are attested: e.g., š a 3 - b e 2 n u - m u - u 3 - d a - z u , “I have not learned its meaning from you” (*šag-be.Ø nu-mu-e.da-’.zu.Ø).
The third-person animate prefix is /n/. Examples are i g i z i m u - u n - š i - i n - b a r - r e - e š , “they looked favorably towards him” (*igi zid.Ø mu-n.ši-n.bar.eš); i n - d a - ĝ e n , “he came with him” (*i-n.da-ĝen.Ø); a g a 3 - u s 2 - d a e - d a - ĝ a l 2 , “it is with the soldier” (*aga-us.da i-n.da-ĝal.Ø). As the last example shows, /n/ may not be written explicitly.
117 See JAGERSMA 2010.

148 CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES
The third-person inanimate prefix is /b/, which occurs before all adverbial prefixes: e.g., e n i m b i 2 - i n - ĝ a r , “he laid a claim” (*enim.Ø b.i-n.ĝar.Ø). The verb enim—ĝar literally means to “place a word on something” and hence is constructed with the locative 2 (see Chapter 14.1.7.2).
The first-person plural prefix is /me/, which is always written with the sign ME: e.g., m e - d a t u k u - a , “which we owe him” (lit. “which he has with us”) (*me.da-n.tuku.Ø-a). It can also be used on its own without following adverbial prefix. In such a case, it serves as oblique object (see Chapter 14.1.4): e.g., i g i m e - e b - d u 8 - n e , “they will see us” (*igi.Ø me-b.du.ene). It can also be the writing for an indirect object (see Chapter 14.1.3).
The second-person plural is possibly /ene/, but forms are only attested from the Old Babylonian period onwards (ATTINGER 1993: 210): e.g., m u - e - n e - š i - ĝ e n - e n , “I went to you” (*muene.ši-ĝen.en).
The third-person plural prefix is /nne/ (the vowel is likely long): i n - n e - š i - s a 1 0 , “he bought her from them” (*i-nne.ši-(n.)sa.Ø); i n - n e d a - ĝ a l 2 , “it is with them” (*i-nne.da-ĝal.Ø).
Indirect Object and Oblique Object share the same slot in the verbal chain. In Chapter 12.1.4 we already encountered the prefix combinations i m - m a - and i m - m i - , which contain the morphemes /ba/ and /bi/ respectively.
14.1.3. Indirect Object (-/a/-)
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 399–414; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 167–172;
In the verbal chain, the indirect object agrees with the dative postposition (-/ra/) for animate nouns (see Chapter 6.2.3.1) or the directive postposition (-/e/) for inanimate nouns (see Chapter 6.2.3.6). Consider the following two example sentences:
Example 14.2
den-lil2 lugal-ĝu10-ra ḫu-mu-na-gub Enlil lugal-ĝu.ra ḫa-mu-nn.a-’.ĝar.Ø
DN king-POSS.DAT MOD-VP-DAT-ERG.to place.ABS
I indeed erected (the throne) for Enlil, my king.
lugal-ĝu10 bir3-e ḫa-ba-ab-šum2-mu lugal-ĝu.e bir.e ḫa-(i).b.a-b.šum.e
king-POSS.ERG team.DIR MOD-VP-IO-ABS.to give.ERG
May my king give it to the team.
Both sentences contain a benefactor or recipient of the verbal action, thus an indirect object (IO). In the first sentence it is Enlil, for whom the throne is erected. Since Enlil is animate, the nominal phrase the divine name belongs to is marked with the dative postposition (-/ra/); the verb contains the animate form of the indirect object (*-nn.a-). In the second sentence the team is the recipient. The word “team” (b i r 3 ) is grammatically inanimate. Hence it is marked with the directive postposition (-/e/); the verb contains the inanimate form of the indirect object (*-b.a-).



CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES 149
With verbs of giving (e.g., š u m 2 ) it marks the recipient of the verbal action. With verbs of speaking (e.g., d u 1 1 - g ; g u 3 — d e 2 ) it marks the addressee of the verb. With verbs of motion (e.g., ĝ e n ) the dative marks the goal of the motion. A further nuance of the dative case is to mark the participant that experiences a sensation or feeling: e.g., s e s - ĝ u 1 0 a - n a - z u a - r a - g i g / k a - ĝ u 1 0 m a - g i g , “‘My brother, what part of you hurts?’ – ‘My mouth hurts.’” (*ses-ĝu.Ø anazu.Ø a-ra-gig.Ø / kag-ĝu.Ø ma-gig.Ø).
Finally, the semantic object of the compound verb k i — a ĝ 2 , “to love,” is in the dative. If an inanimate noun is liked or loved, then that noun is followed by a directive postposition.
The adverbial element for the indirect object is /a/. It follows the IPP.118 The forms of the indirect object are best seen in combination with a preceding ventive prefix (see Chapter 12.1.4).
Form Ventive Meaning
1st sg. *-’.a- m a - a n - d u 3 – he built for me 2nd sg. *-era- m a - r a - a n - d u 3 r a - a n - d u 3 he built for you 3rd sg. anim. -nn.a- m u - n a - a n - d u 3 i n - n a - a n - d u 3 he built for her 3rd sg. inan. -b.a- i m - m a - a n - d u 3 b a - a n - d u 3 he built for it 1st pl. -me(.a)- he built for us 2nd pl. -ene(.a)- he built for you (pl.) 3rd pl. -nne(.a) m u - u n - n e - e n - d u 3 i n - n e - e n - d u 3 he built for them
The first-person singular indirect object is only visible when used with a ventive, which is always spelled with the sign MA ( ): e.g., m a - a n - d u 1 1 , “he said it to me” (*mu-’.a-n.dug.Ø or *man.dug.Ø);119 m a - a n - ĝ a l 2 , “he made it available to me” (*mu-’.a-n.ĝal.Ø or *ma-n.ĝal.Ø). Since the form is otherwise not visible, the form is usually considered to be *ma-. If it is followed by another vowel, we find often a plene writing: e.g., m a - a - d u 1 1 , “you said it to me” (*mu-’.ae.dug.Ø) vs. m u - e - d u 1 1 , “you said it” (*mu-e.dug.Ø).
The second-person singular form is characterized by /r/, which appears before the vowel. The full reconstructed form should be *-era. For the forms with preceding ventive both the spellings m u r a - and m a - r a - are attested: e.g., m u - r a - b e 2 - e n , “I say it to you” (*mu-ra-b.e.en); m a r a - a b - b e 2 - e n , “I say it to you” (*mu-ra-b.e.en). In the lexical environment of the Old Babylonian Grammatical Texts (OBGT), a form i 3 - r a - n i - i n - g u b , “he erected (it) for you” (Akk. ušzizkum), shows that in the Old Babylonian period a finite marker could be used before the second-person singular indirect object. In the third millennium the morpheme /ra/ can be at the first position of the verb, similar to the middle prefix (see Chapter 12.1.5): e.g., r a - a b - š u m 2 - m u , “he will give it to you” (*ra-b.šum.e). It should be noted that a spelling b a - r a - can either be the model prefix /bara/-, expressing a vetitive (see Chapter 15.1.5), or a spelling for *ba-ta-, which contains the ablative infix (see Chapter 0).
The third-person singular forms differentiate between animate and inanimate. With the ventive preceding the animate form we usually find the spellings m u - n a - and m u - u n - n a - ; the latter is particularly well attested in the Old Babylonian period and later. Without the ventive forms begin with the spellings i 3 - n a - and i n - n a - . The full form is /nna/ and not /na/. Early Dynastic texts
118 It is plausible that the infix is etymologically related to the nominal locative case; see Chapter 6.2.3.5. 119 Note that some grammatical overviews suggest *ma- as basic form based on the preceding ventive,



150 CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES
from the south (e.g., Girsu) attest to the spelling e - n a - : e.g., e - n a - š u m 2 , “he gave it to him/her” (*i-nn.a-(n.)šum.Ø).120
The inanimate form is *b.a-, which can appear at the beginning of the finite verb, but often follows the finite marker and the ventive in the spelling i m - m a - : e.g., i m - m a - ĝ e n , “he came there (to the temple)” (*i-m-ba-ĝen.Ø).
Example 14.3
n a m - l u g a l - z u u ĝ 3 - e b a - s a 6 Your kingship is good for the people. nam-lugal-zu.Ø uĝ.e b.a-sag.Ø
It should be noted that b a - used for the inanimate indirect object (IO) is identical in form to the Middle Voice prefix (MED; see Chapter 12.1.5).121
The first-person plural form is /me/, but a few examples also indicate a form *me.a: e.g., ḫ e 2 m e - š u m 2 - m u , “he may give it to us” (*ḫa-i-me-(b.)šum.e). For the latter see, e.g., š u m 2 - m e a b , “give it to us!” (*šum-me.a-b).122 The spelling suggests that the vowel /e/ was long: *mē.a.
The third-person plural form is /nne/, although a few texts at the end of the third millennium attest to a form *nne.a. A few documents from Girsu dating to the Ur III period contain a form ḫ e 2 - n e a b - š u m 2 - m u , “may he give it to them” (*ḫa-i-nne.a-b.šum.e). Compare the contemporary form ḫ e 2 - n e - e b - š u m 2 - m u (*ḫa-i-nne-b.šum.e) with the same meaning. The spelling suggests that the vowel /e/ was long: *nnē.a.123
The following table contains most attested spellings for the IO.
Analysis
m a - m a - for me
m a - r a - m u - r a - for you
m u - r a - m u - r a - for you
r a - r a - for you
m u - n a - m u - n n . a - for him/her
m u - u n - n a - m u - n n . a - for him/her
i 3 - n a - i - n n . a - for him/her
120 It has been argued that such a form can also be understood as a stative passive; see Chapter 13.1.5. 121 The Middle prefix can be analyzed *ba-, whereas the IO is analyzed *b.a-. 122 For the imperative, see Chapter 15.2. 123 Compare also the letter MICHALOWSKI 1993, No. 138: (1) l u 2 - d u t u (2) u3 u r - d n a n š e - r a (3) u 3 - n e - a du11.

i n - n a - i - n n . a - for him/her
n a - 124
mu-un-ne

in-ne
in-ne-a
me
b a - b a - a - ĝ a r it was placed on it.
im-ma
124 The form n a - a b - b e 2 - a (Old Sumerian n a - e - a ) attested in the letter formulary was interpreted in different ways: EDZARD 2003: 94 takes n a - as indirect object: “that which he (Edzard: you) will say to him/her.” JAGERSMA 2010: 229 understands it as a fusion of the interrogative pronoun a - n a , “what,” and a form of the verb d u 1 1 - g : *(a)na.Ø a-b.e.e-a.Ø, “what he says.” An indirect object is not impossible considering form such as r a - a n - e š , “they said it to you” (*ra-n.e.eš). Initial n a - is otherwise also known as modal prefix; see Chapters 15.1.6 and 15.1.8.
