154 CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES
14.1.4. Comitative (-da-)
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 448–54; FOXVOG 2014: 78–9; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 172–80
The comitative case with the basic meaning “(together) with” is nearly always written with the grapheme DA: e.g., PN- d a m u - d a - ĝ e n , “he came with PN” (*mu-(n.)da-ĝen.Ø). In late Early Dynastic texts /da/ with following inanimate FPP /b/ is rendered with the sign URUDA read d a b 6 ( ): e.g., a n - n e 2 k i - d a e n i m a n - d a b 6 - e , “Heaven was talking with Earth” (*an.e ki.da enim.Ø a-n.da-b.e.e). When followed by the locative infix - n i - , the comitative case occasionally is written - d i - or - d i 3 - : e.g., u 4 m u - d i 3 - n i - i b 2 - z a l - e , “he was passing the day with him in ...” (*mu-(n.)da-ni-b.zal.e). The spelling - d e 3 - is also possible in the context of a secondperson singular pronoun /e/ or before a verb base containing /e/: ḫ u - m u - u 8 - d e 3 - ĝ a l 2 , “it is indeed there with you” (*ḫa-mu-e.da-ĝal.Ø).
The following table contains the forms with preceding IPP and forms with preceding IO:
Form Comitative Ind. Object + Com. Meaning
1st sg. *-’.da- m u - d a - g u b he stands by me 2nd sg. -e.da- m u - e - d a - g u b m a - r a - d a - g u b he stands by you 3rd sg. anim. -n.da- m u - u n - d a - g u b m u - n a - d a - g u b he stands by her 3rd sg. inan. -b.da- i b 2 - d a - g u b b a - d a - g u b he stands by it 1st pl. -me.da2nd pl. ?
3rd pl. -nne.da m u - n e - d a - g u b m u - n e - d a - g u b he stands by them
The first-person plural form contains the initial pronominal prefix /me/. It is always written - m e d a - : e.g., ḫ e 2 - m e - d a - a n - z u , “he indeed should know about it from us” (*ḫa-i-me.dan.zu.Ø)125; m e - d a - t u k u - a , “what he has with us” (*me.da-(n.)tuku.Ø). The latter example shows that it can occur at the beginning of the verbal chain.
The third-person plural form is /nnē.da/, which is usually written - n e - d a - . Texts of the second half of the third millennium use the sign PI ( ) which is read - n e d a - in this context: e.g., b a n e d a - l a 2 , “(the debt) was attached with them” (*ba-nne.da-la.Ø), s i m u g - n e i n - n e d a ĝ a l 2 , “it is with the smiths” (*simug-ene.(da) i-nne.da-ĝal.Ø).
As the nominal comitative postposition, the dimensional infix has the basic meaning “(together) with,” and therefore marks a participant in whose presence or company the action or state takes place. The participant can be both animate or inanimate:
Example 14.4
l u 2 d e l i l u 2 2 - d a k i ĝ 2 m u - d a - a k - k e 4 A solitary man will work with another. lu dili.e lu min.da kiĝ.Ø mu-(n.)da-(b.)ak.e
k u 3 - d i m 2 i m - d a - t u š A silversmith sat there with it. kug-dim.Ø i-m-b.da-tuš.Ø
l u 2 - d a n u - m u - u n - d a - a b - b e 2 He will not discuss it with anyone. lu.da nu-mu-n.da-b.e.e
125 Bilgames and Huwawa A, 9. For the use of the COM with this verb compare Gudea Cylinder ###.



CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES 155
The comitative has also a nuanced meaning “near to, at.”
When used with the verb t u k u , “to have,” the comitative can have a nuanced meaning “near to, at” in expressions that someone owes something. In such expressions, the debtor is marked with the comitative:
Example 14.5
s e s - t u r - d a e - d a - t u k u Sestur owes it to him. Ses-tur.da i-(n.)da-(n.)tuku.Ø
k u 3 n u - m u - d a - a - t u k u I do not owe you silver. kug.Ø nu-mu-’.da-e.tuku.Ø
A similar nuanced meaning of the comitative occurs with the verb ĝ a l 2 , “to be present.”
Example 14.6
l u 2 m a 2 - g a n k i - d a i n - d a - ĝ a l 2 The men from Magan have it. lu Magan.a(k).da i-n.da-ĝal.Ø
š e e 2 - a n u - m u - d a - ĝ a l 2 I have no barley in the house. še.Ø e.a nu-mu-’.da-(n.)ĝal.Ø
The comitative can also be used in the meaning “to be able to.” The grammatical subject is also cross-referenced in the initial pronominal prefix:
Example 14.7
l u 2 e n i m - m a - b e 2 n u - m u - d a - d e 6 He was not able to bring a witness for this. lu enim.ak-be.Ø nu-mu-(n.)da-n.de.Ø
k a b 2 n u - u b - d a - a b - d u 1 1 - g a - a š As something that could not be estimated. kab.Ø nu-i-b.da-b.dug.Ø-a.še
The comitative also denotes a participant with whom the subject is involved in a mutual or reciprocal activity:
Example 14.8
u m m a k i - d a d a m - ḫ a - r a e - d a - a k He did battle with Umma. Umma.da damḫara.Ø i-(b.)da-(n.)ak.Ø
Verbs that express emotion use the comitative to mark the animate or inanimate participant that causes the emotion. A frequent verb used here is ḫ u l 2 , “to rejoice.”
Example 14.9
e 2 - d a l u g a l i m - d a - ḫ u l 2 The owner was happy with the house. e.da lugal.Ø i-m-(b.)da-ḫul.Ø
d e n - k i - d a d e n - l i l 2 m u - u n - d a - ḫ u l 2 Enlil rejoiced over Enki. Enki.da Enlil.Ø mu-n.da-ḫul.Ø
Finally, some verbs tend to be constructed with the comitative. The compound verb a 2 — a ĝ 2 , “to instruct,” for instance, uses the comitative to mark the instructed individual(s):



156 CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES
Example 14.10
d n i n - z a 3 - g a - d a a 2 m u - d a - a ĝ 2 He instructed Ninzaga. Nin-zaga.da a.Ø mu-(n.)da-(n.).aĝ.Ø
Other verbs frequently used with the comitative are s a 2 , “to be equal to,” and z u , “to learn (from someone).” In the Old Babylonian Grammatical Texts (OBGT), the comitative occasionally expresses the grammatical object of the transitive verb: e.g., m u - u n - d a - ĝ a r (iškunšu), “he placed it/him.”
Analysis
in-da- i-n.da
mu-da
mu-u8-da- mu-e.da
mu-e-da- mu-e.da
mu-un-da- mu-n.da
mu-ub-da- mu-b.da



CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES 157
14.1.5. Terminative infix (-ši-)
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 459–464; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 191–198
The ablative and terminative prefixes occupy the same slot in the verbal prefix chain, as the directional meaning of both (“from” vs. “to”) exclude each other. The latter is usually written with
the sign ŠI ( ),126 in early Sumerian texts also written ŠE3 ( ) before syllables containing vowels /a/ and /e/, but occasionally also /u/.127 It can be preceded by the IO and rarely the comitative infix. The following table shows the forms in which the terminative infix appears first:
Analysis 1st sg. -V/’.ši2nd sg. -e.ši3rd sg. anim. -n.ši3rd sg. inan. -b.ši
1st pl. -me.ši- (?) 2nd pl. ?
3rd pl. -nne.ši3rd pl. inan. -b.ši
Similar to the comitative infix the first person singular is usually not explicitly written. For the second person singular forms the spelling mu-e-ši- (and rarely mu-u8-ši-) is well attested.
The third person singular inanimate form may be preceded by a ventive:128
Example 14.11
iri-ne2-še3 igi zi im-ši-bar-ra iri-ane.še igi zid.Ø i-m-b.ši-n.bar.Ø-a.a city-POSS.TERM eye just.ABS VP-VENT-3sg.TERM-3sgA.to split.3sgP-NOM.LOC
When he looked favorably at his city.
The terminative prefix indicates the destination or purpose of the verbal action. Its meanings range from “to, towards” to “for the sake of, because of.”
Example 14.12
den-lil2-le igi zi mu-e-ši-in-bar Enlil.e igi zid.Ø mu-e.ši-n.bar.Ø DN.ERG eye just.ABS VENT-2sg.TERM-3sgA.to split.3sgP
Enlil gazed favorably upon you.
The terminative also expresses an abstract destination in three fashions: result, purpose, and reason:
Example 14.13
a l a n - n a - n e 2 - š e 3 m u - t u He made it into his own statue. alan-ane.še mu-(n.)tud.Ø
126 A few instances from Nippur dating to the Ur III period attest to a spelling with the sign SI; see JAGERSMA 2010: 460. 127 Examples are e - š e 3 - ĝ a r , “he established it regarding it” (*i-b.ši-(n.)ĝar.Ø); a m u - n a - š e 3 - r u , “he dedicated it for him for (the life of ...)” (a.Ø mu-nn.a-ši-(n.)ru.Ø) (Ean I 19, 11). 128 Note that JAGERSMA 2010 analyzes such forms *i-m(u)-ši-...



158 CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES
14.1.6. Ablative infix (-ta-)
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 454–9; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 116
The dimensional infix /ta/ is usually written with the sign TA ( ). However, from the Ur III period onwards /ra/ appears instead after the vowels /a/ and /e/: e.g., m u - n e - r a - e 3 , “he came forth for them” (*mu-nne-ta-e.Ø). In this example, /ra/ can only be the ablative infix as it follows the third-person plural dative infix. If the ablative infix is preceded by the middle prefix /ba/, the inanimate pronominal prefix /b/ does not appear. Consider also the two parallel sentences in the following example taken from date formulas in economic texts:
Example 14.14
i t i - t a u 4 2 4 b a - t a - z a l 24 days have passed of the month. itid.ta ud 24.Ø ba-ta-zal.Ø month.ABL day NUM.ABS MED-ABL-to pass.3sgS
i t i - t a u 4 2 4 b a - r a - z a l 24 days have passed of the month. itid.ta ud 24.Ø ba-ta-zal.Ø
As this example shows, the ablative has a separative meaning, which is opposite to the terminative prefix /ši/ (see Chapter 14.1.5). While here the separative meaning is temporal, the following two examples show separative meaning in terms of locality:
Example 14.15
kur ma2-ganki-ta na4esi im-ta-e11 kur Magan.a(k).ta esi.Ø i-m-b.ta-(n.)e.Ø land GN.GEN.ABL diorite.ABS VP-VEN-ABL-3sgA.to go down.3sgP
He brought down diorite from the mountain of Magan.
Example 14.16
e2-gal-ta e-ta-e3-de3 egal.ta i-(b.)ta-(b.)ed.e palace.ABL VP-ABL-3sgP.to go out.3sgA
He will bring forth from the palace.
Verbs with an inherently separative meaning are usually construed with the ablative. This, in particular, applies to verbs such the following:
+OO Meaning
b a l a nabalkutu to cross over
e 3 waṣû; šūṣû to go out; to bring out
e 1 1 warādu; šūrudu to go down; to bring down
z a l šutebrû to pass
z i - g našû to take from, out; withdraw



CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES 159
As its nominal counterpart, the ablative can have instrumental meaning as well:
Example 14.17
ĝeššinig-ta e2-ninnu im-ta-sikil-e-ne šinig.ta Eninnu.Ø i-m-b.ta-(b.)sikil.ene tamarisk.ABL TN.ABS VP-VEN-ABL-3sgP.to clean.3plA
They were cleaning the Eninnu with tamarisk. (Gudea Cylinder B iv 10–1)
The ablative predominantly occurs with inanimate nouns. There are, however, a few examples with animate nouns:
Example 14.18
lu2 erim2-ra guruš3uš mu-e-ta-ab-e3-de3-en lu erim.ra guruš.Ø mu-e.ta-b.ed.en evildoer.DAT cut.ABS VEN-ABL-3sgP.to go forth.3plA
You rage against the evildoer. (Lipit-Eštar B 33)

b a - r a - ba-ra

162 CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES
14.1.7. The local prefixes and their functions
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 465–85; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 201–21; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 117–22
We have already seen in the discussion of verbal or conjugation prefixes that the verbal prefix m u (or short m) has a local connotation (see Chapter 12.1.4). The verbal prefix does, however, not agree with any dimensional case on the nominal template. Within the verbal prefix chain two infixes can be considered local prefixes. One has a basic form /ni/ with the meaning “in” or “into” (Locative 1), the other a basic form /e/ with the meaning “on” or “onto” (Locative 2). Both of these verbal infixes are agreed upon by a locative case on the nominal template. A third prefix
Nominal case marker Verbal prefix Meaning
animate inanimate basic form Location Locative 1 – /a/ /ni/ in(to) Locative 2 /ra/ /a/ /e/ on(to) Locative 3 /ra/ /e/ /e/ next to, at
14.1.7.1. The local prefix /ni/ (Locative 1)
The local prefix /ni/, “in, there, therein, into,” agrees with the locative case on the (inanimate) noun phrase, and is not used with animate nouns. It may also refer to a previously mentioned location. The prefix is usually written with the sign NI ( ) when it is followed by the final pronominal prefix before the verb base (even if not fully reflected in writing!; see Example 14.21). When the local prefix is the last element before the verbal base, it is shortened to /n/ (usually written with the
syllabograms , , or ). This happens in intransitive verbal forms, as the following two examples demonstrate:
Example 14.19
ša3-be2-a mu-un-da-an-ti šag-be.a mu-n.da-n(i)-til.Ø heart-POSS.LOC VP-3sg.COM-LOC1-to live.3sgS
He lives with him/her in it.
Example 14.20
e2-kišeb3-ba-ka-na nu-un-ku4-re-en6 e-kišeb.ak-ane.a nu-(i)-n(i)-kur.en house seal.GEN-POSS.LOC NEG-(VP)-LOC1-to enter.1sgS
I did not enter his storeroom.
In the first example, the /n/ before the base is a syncopated form of the locative 1 infix /ni/, since the verb t i - l , “to live,” is intranstive, and thus the slot of the FPP remains empty. The local prefix agrees with the locative case on š a 3 - g , “heart, midst.” The same applies to the second example.
Example 14.21
unugki-ge giri17-zal-a u4 mu-da-zal-zal-le Unug.e giri-zal.a ud.Ø mu-n.da-(n.)zalzal.e
GN.ERG joy.LOC day.ABS VP-3sg.COM-LOC1-to pass.3sgS
Uruk spends the day in joy with (or because of) him.



CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES 163
This example uses the verb u 4 — z a l , “to spend the day.” Note that the FPP agreeing with the patient (u 4 ) is optional, but is often written in later sources as in the third example: e.g., u 4 a s i l a l a 2 m i - n i - i b - z a l - z a l - e - n e , “they spend the day in feasts.” Although nothing directly before the verb base appears in writing, a locative 1 prefix needs to be reconstructed agreeing with the locative postposition on g i r i 1 7 - z a l , “joy.”
The locative 1 prefix is never preceded by an IPP. It differs in meaning and form from the oblique object prefix /nni/ (see Chapter 14.1.7.3), which in contrast always refers to an animate noun. Since local /ni/ has the form /n/ directly before the base, it does not differ from the FPP /n/ that marks either agent (for perfective) or patient (for imperfective). Both morphemes are usually distinguishable based on the context.
While usually it refers to a specific location, in which the verbal action happens (Example 14.22), it can also modify the basic meaning of a verb adding the notion of locality (Example 14.23):
Example 14.22
ensi2-ke4 kalam-ma-na zi-ga ba-ni-ĝar ensik.e kalam-ane.a ziga.Ø ba-ni-(n.)ĝar.Ø city governor.ERG land-POSS.LOC levy.ABS MED-LOC-3sgA.to place.3sgP
The ruler established a levy in his land.
Example 14.23
du6-du6-ra2 mi-ni-in-ĝar du-dur.a mu-ni-n.ĝar.Ø mound.REDUPL.LOC VEN-LOC-3sgA.to place.3sgP
He made (the settlements) into ruined mounds.
Another example of this latter use occurs with the compound or idiomatic verb e n i m — g i 4 , “to answer,” which often omits the nominal part. Its literal meaning is “to turn (the word) in”:
Example 14.24
dinana-ke4 mu-na-ni-ib2-gi4-gi4 Inanak.e mu-nn.a-ni-b.gigi.e DN.ERG VEN-IO3sg-LOC-3sgP.to return.REDUPL.3sgA
Inana answers.

i - n i - i-ni-(b/n.)VB
m u - n i - mu-ni-(b/n.)VB

b a - a n - ba-n(i)-VB

In third millennium sources, the locative 1 prefix /ni/ can begin a verbal form: e.g., t e m e n - b e 2 k i - a n i - s i - s i , “he put its foundation pegs into the ground” (*temen-be.Ø ki.a ni-(n.)sisi.Ø); g u 3 - d e 2 - a i m u 3 - š u b - b a n i - ĝ a r , “Gudea put clay into the mould” (*ni-(n.)ĝar.Ø). Traditionally, such forms were read with initial i 3 - and analyzed *i-n.VB.Ø. Due to vowel harmony, however, a spelling NI-ĝ a r can only be read n e 2 - ĝ a r in an Old Sumerian text, and not i 3 - ĝ a r , since a writing e - ĝ a r is to be expected.129
129 See ZOLYOMI 2017: 204–5.



CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES 165
Obverse 1 bešeĝ dub-ba 2 kišeb3 dab-ba 3 a-du ugula uš-bar 4 mu ma2 den-ki 5 u3 mu si-ma-num2ki Reverse 1 ni-ĝal2
Tablet basket: the sealed documents of conveyance of Adu, the overseer of the weavers (for) the year “Boat of Enki” (= Šū-Suen 3) and the year “Simanum” (= Šū-Suen 4) are present in it.
Fig. 8 Tablet tag NBC 581 (BPOA 6, 1162) from Umma



166 CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES
Labels attached to tablet baskets, the so-called b e š e ĝ d u b - b a (pisan-dub-ba) show that the shortened locative 1 prefix can also disappear before the base, like the final pronominal prefix. In texts from Ur it sometimes is written:
Example 14.25
b e š e ĝ d u b - b a d i - t i l - l a n i - ĝ a l 2 Tablet basket: the completed legal cases are in it. bešeĝ-dub.ak.Ø di-til-a.Ø ni-(n.)ĝal.Ø
bešeĝ dub-ba niĝ2-ka9-ak i 3 - i n - ĝ a l 2 Tablet basket: the accounts (...) are in it. bešeĝ-dub.ak.Ø niĝ-kas-ak.Ø i-n.ĝal.Ø
Note: If, however, the locative in the verb expresses the meaning “on, upon,” then a form NI- ĝ a l 2 should be read i 3 - ĝ a l 2 , which is analyzed as *i-b(i)-ĝal.Ø. Here, the Locative 2 prefix is used (see Chapter 14.1.7.2, below).
The prefix /ni/ co-references a locative and therefore refers to a location of a verbal action. Sometimes, however, it does not refer to a specific location and only adds to the basic meaning of the verb: e.g., PN-e n u - u n - n a - n i - k u 4 , “PN did not bring it in for him.”
Furthermore, the local prefix /ni/ occasionally also is co-referenced with a terminative in the noun phrase and expresses a meaning “into”: e.g., e 2 - k i š e b 3 - b a - š e 3 b a - a n - k u 4 , “it was brought into the storehouse” (*e-kišeb-ak-še ba-n(i)-kuř.Ø).
A further function of the locative 1 is to refer to the material that went into the production of something: e.g., k u 3 - s i 2 2 k u 3 - b a b b a r 2 - r a š u m u - n a - n i - t a g , “he decorated it in gold and silver for her.” Finally, /ni/ has sometimes an instrumental meaning: e.g., PN-e k a - g a - n a b a - n i - g e - e n 6 , “PN confirmed it with his own mouth.”
14.1.7.2. The local prefix /e/ (Locative 2)
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 476–85; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 206–21; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 117–8
The basic form of the locative 2 meaning “on, upon” is /e/. In third millennium evidence it is usually
written as a plene spelling with the sign A ( ) based on vowel harmony with the preceding sign (ba-, da-, ma-, na-, ra-, and ta-).130 The form appears identical to the second person singular FPP. Thus a form b a - a - d e 2 can mean by itself both, “it is poured on it” and “you poured it for something.”
Example 14.26
1 dug 1(ban2) ma2-a ba-a-ĝar diš dug ban.Ø ma.a ba-e-ĝar.Ø
NUM jar MET.ABS boat.LOC MED-LOC2-to place.3sgS
One jar (with a capacity of) one seah was placed on the boat.
130 Hence the earliest thorough study of the locative 2 assumes /a/ being the basic form; see YOSHIKAWA 1977. JAGERSMA 2010: 489 refers to several instances of “non-standard spellings” b a - a - in texts from Lagaš, where only b a - is expected. These spellings may indicate a local dialect that assumes a vowel length of the prefix b a - .



CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES 167
The form /e/ only appears directly before the verbal base. The following table contains common spellings of the local prefix /e/.

i 3 - i b 2 - i-(b).i-VB
a - a b - a-b).i-VB
It should be noted that the local prefix /e/ (LOC2) has been dealt with differently in grammatical overviews. ZOLYOMI 2017 assumes that it can be used for both animate and inanimate nouns. Animate nouns are therefore marked with the dative case. JAGERSMA 2010 differentiates between the local prefix /e/ used to refer to inanimate nouns and the animate oblique object prefix used for the animate opposite of the local prefix /e/ (see Chapter 14.1.7.3). As an example to demonstrate
131 It appears that the local prefix is not written after a long /ē/ as in the third person plural IO prefix: e.g., m a 2 - a b a n e - g u b vs. m a 2 - a b a - n a - a - g u b ; see JAGERSMA 2010: 480.



168 CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES
this differentiation, Jagersma compares examples of the compound verb e n i m — ĝ a r , “to lay claim on something/someone”:
Example 14.27
lu2-ge-na ab2-ba enim bi2-ĝar Lugena.e ab.a enim.Ø b.i-(n.)ĝar.Ø
PN.ERG cow.LOC word.ABS 3sgLOC2-3sgA.to place.3sgP
Lugena claimed the cow. (< “he placed a word on it”)
Example 14.28
ur-dba-ba6-ke4 lu2-nam2-maḫ-ra enim in-ni-ĝa2ĝarar Ur-Baba.ak.e Lu-nammaḫ.ra enim.Ø i-nn.i-(n.)ĝar.Ø
PN.ERG PN.DAT word.ABS VP-3sgOO-3sgA.to place.3sgP
Ur-Baba claimed Lunammah. (< “he placed a word on him”)
In Example 14.27, a b 2 , “cow,” is semantic object of the compound verb. As an inanimate noun it is marked with the prefix b i 2 - (*b.i-). In Example 14.28, the semantic object is a proper name or animate noun and thus is marked with the dative case (DAT). ZOLYOMI 2017 interprets the prefixes in both verbal forms (b i 2 - ĝ a r and i n - n i - ĝ a r ) as LOC2.132 In his view, therefore, the local prefix /e/ has both inanimate and animate forms. JAGERSMA 2010, on the other hand, separates between inanimate and animate forms. In his view, the sentences with inanimate noun as semantic object are expressed with the LOC2 in the verbal prefix chain; in the cases an animate noun is used as semantic object, he analyzes the infix as an OO. SALLABERGER & D’ISTRIA 2023 interpret both as OO. In their analysis, the inanimate noun can be marked both with LOC and DIR.
The idiom e n i m — ĝ a r is also found in passive constructions:
Example 14.29
ku3-ba enim ba-a-ĝa2-ar kug-be.a enim.Ø ba-e-ĝar.Ø silver-DEM.LOC word.ABS MED-LOC2-to place.3sgS
This silver was claimed. (< “a word was placed on it”)
While the previous examples
The basic meaning of the locative 2 can also be found in a more metaphorical sense:
Example 14.30
a - g u 3 133 u r - b a - g a r a 2 - k a b a - a - ĝ a r agu Ur-bagara.ak.a ba-e-ĝar.Ø
skull PN.GEN.LOC MED-LOC2-to place.3sgS
It was placed on Ur-Bagara’s account (lit. skull).
132 See ZOLYOMI 2017: 210, examples 401 and 402. 133 Alternative reading of a - g u 3 is u g u 2 .



CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES 169
As the examples above show, the local prefix /e/ is only used with inanimate nouns. The corresponding noun phrase is marked with the locative case (see Example 14.30).
Example 14.31
a g a ( ... ) s a ĝ - ĝ a 2 i m - m i - ĝ a l 2 He had placed a crown on my head. aga.Ø saĝ-ĝu.a i-m-b.i-(n.)ĝal.Ø
ĝ e š m a 2 - a b i 2 - i n - g u b He put it onto the boat. ma.a b.i-n.gub.Ø
š e - n u m u n a - g u 3 - a b a - a - ĝ a r The seeds were put on the account. še-numun.Ø ugu.a ba-e-ĝar.Ø
m u - s a r - r a - b a š u b i 2 - i b 2 - u r 3 - a Whoever effaces this inscription. musara-be.a šu.Ø b.i-b.ur.e-a
Expressions such as the last of these examples appear in curse formulae of (royal) inscriptions. This example shows nicely the use of the locative 2. The noun phrase m u - s a r - r a , “inscription”
( ), is marked with the locative postposition. It is agreed upon by the prefix /bi/ in the
verbal chain, which contains the locative 2 prefix. The compound verb š u — u r 3 ( ) means literally “to rub the hand (on); to flatten with the hand.”
14.1.7.3. Oblique Object or Locative 3 (-/i/-)134
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 415–44; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 215–21; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 118–22
While the IO (see Chapter 14.1.3) indicates the recipient of a verbal action, the Oblique Object (OO) refers to the animate or inanimate noun, which is affected by the verbal action. Like the IO, the OO agrees with a dative postposition for animate nouns or a directive postposition for inanimate nouns. It is used, in particular, as a semantic object in environments, where the absolutive already marks another noun:
Example 14.32
an-ra den-lil2 im-ma-ni-us2 An.ra Enlil.Ø i-m-ba-nni-(n.).us.Ø DN.DAT DN.ABS VP-VEN-MED-3sgOO-3sgA.to follow.3sgP
He had Enlil sit next to An.
Example 14.33
mu-zu pa bi2-in-e3 mu-zu.e pa.Ø b.i-n.e.Ø name-POSS.DIR twig.ABS 3sgOO-3sgA.to go out.3sgP
He made your fame appear.
In the first sentence, the sky god is not the recipient of the verbal action u s 2 , “to be next to,” but the verbal action is performed on him. Hence, the verbal phrase contains the oblique object infix /nni/. The second example uses the compound verb p a — e 3 , “to make appear” (lit. “to let twigs come forth”). The noun p a is the direct object of the verbal base e 3 , but the semantic object is
134 “Locative 3” is a designation used in ZÓLYOMI 2017.



170 CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES
m u , “name, fame.” Hence, it is marked with a directive case. In the verbal chain the prefix /bi/ agrees with the oblique object.
The following table provides an overview of the different forms of the oblique object marker in the verb. As a matter of clarity, the ergative is a third-person animate noun and the verb is in the perfective aspect. The following table thus only takes forms into account, in which the OO is followed by a FPP:
Form Ventive Meaning
1st sg. *-’.e- m u - u n - u s 2 – he let me follow 2nd sg. *-(e)ri- m u - r i - i n - u s 2 135 i - r i - i n - u s 2 he let you follow 3rd sg. anim. -nn.i- m u - n i - i n - u s 2 i n - n i - i n - u s 2 he let her follow 3rd sg. inan. -b.i- i m - m i - i n - u s 2 b i 2 - i n - u s 2 he let it follow 1st pl. -mē2nd pl. -enē
3rd pl. -nnē- m u - u n - n e - e n - u s 2 i n - n e - e n - u s 2 he let them follow
The oblique object is used in three different ways: (1) a dimensional meaning “in(to) contact with,” (2) to mark the causee in a causative construction, (3) to mark the animate opposite for the local prefix /e/.

i 3 - i b 2 - i-b-VB ×

Forms that contain /bi/, /immi/, /nni/, and also /ri/ (see below) are always transitive. The table also shows that the OO prefix is not combined with other prefixes such as the IO, and the dimensional prefixes /da/, /ta/, /ši/, /ni/ and /e/. For example, in several instances, the IO, if expressed explicitly, is written without an OO prefix, that the verbal form usually requires without the IO: e.g., k i - b e 2 b i 2 - g i 4 , “he restored it” (lit. “he returned it to its place”; *ki-be.e bi-(n.)gi.Ø) vs. k i - b e 2 m u n a - g i 4 , “he restored it for him/her” (*ki-be.e mu-nn.a-(n.)gi4.Ø).
The inanimate forms with b i - are identical to the corresponding Locative 2 forms. However, they can be differentiated by the different connotation of the OO compared to the local prefix /e/. Whereas the former has a meaning “next to; in(to) contact with,” the latter means “on, upon.” There is also a morphological difference. The inanimate OO agrees with a directive on the noun phrase, while the locative 2 agrees with a locative case. The following two examples show the difference:
Example 14.34
dub du10-ga im-mi-ĝal2 dub.Ø dug.a i-m-bi-(n.)ĝal.Ø tablet.ABS knee.LOC VP-VEN-LOC2-3sgA.to be there.3sgP
She had a tablet on (her) knee.
Example 14.35
e2-e im-mi-dab6 e.e i-m-bi-(n.)dab.Ø house.DIR VP-VEN-OO-3sgA.to surround.3sgP
He had them surround the temple.
The forms /bi/ and /nni/ always occur before a FPP. Since both forms contain an IPP (*b and *n respectively), they cannot occur together with the IO prefix, /da/, /ta/, /ši/, or /ni/. As a major difference to the local prefix /e/ the OO prefix can take the slot of the FPP, if it is not already taken. In such a case, the inanimate OO prefix appears as /b/ before the base; the animate OO prefix appears as /n/. The FPP slot only stays empty in intransitive forms (see Chapter 13.1.2) and in transitive imperfective forms, which do not explicitly mark the direct object.138
The second person singular prefix of the OO behaves similar to the third person singular prefixes. Before a FPP it has the form /ri/. If the slot of the FPP is not taken by the subject or object of the transitive verb, or in an intransitive form, then the OO form is /e/. Most attestations date to the Old
138 Additionally this applies to the forms of the cohortative (see Chapter 15.1.7) and the imperative (see Chapter 15.2).



172 CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES
Babylonian period, when we find, e.g., m i m g a - e - d u 1 1 , “let me treat you with care” (*mim.Ø ga-e-dug).139
Table 14-2 Common spellings of the second person singular oblique object prefix
Analysis
i - r i - i-(e.)ri-(n/b.)VB
m u - r i - mu-(e.)ri-(n/b.)VB
m u - e - r i mu-e.ri-(n./b.)VB
m i - r i - mu-(e.)ri-(n/b.)VB
i m - m a - r i - i-m-ba-(e.)ri-(n/b.)VB
r e 2 - ri-(n/b.)VB140
Somewhat similar to the form m a - of the IO, the first person singular form of the OO is m u - , if it is followed by a FPP. If there is no FPP marking the agent or patient of the transitive sentence, then the form is represented by the FPP of the first person singular /’/.
The plural forms are a little bit less clear as evidence is lacking. It seems clear, however, that the plural OO prefixes before a FPP are represented by the IPP only without additional marking of the OO case. Since the plural prefixes are reconstructed to have long vowels, it seems plausible that a case marker /i/ contracted to those vowels:141
Example 14.36 in-ne-gu7 i-nne-(n.)gu.Ø VP-3plOO-3sgA.to eat.3sgP
He let them eat.
Returning to the functions of the OO, its dimensional meaning “in(to) contact with” should be addressed first. Several intransitive and transitive verbs use this dimensional meaning of the OO.

dnin-ĝeš-zi-da eĝer-be2 ib2-us2 Nin-ĝeš-zid-a(k).Ø eĝer-be.e i-b-us.Ø
DN.ABS back-POSS.DIR VP-OO-to be next to.3sgS

e2-an-na-tum2-ra lu2 ti mu-ne2-ra E’anatum.ra lu.e ti.Ø mu-nn.i-(n.)ra.Ø PN.DAT man.ERG arrow.ABS VEN-OO-3sgA.to hit.3sgP

The second main function of the OO, to mark the causee in a causative construction, will be discussed below in Chapter 14.1.7.4. Thirdly, the animate OO forms are used to mark the local prefix /e/, when an animate noun is the goal of the verbal action.142 The following verbs are commonly attested with animate OO vs. inanimate local prefix /e/:

DAT LOC š u — b a r to release (lit. “to open the hand upon”)

Example 14.39
nam mu-ni-in-tar nam.Ø mu-nni-n.tar.Ø fate.ABS VEN-OO-3sgA.to decide.3sgP
He decided a fate for him.
nam mu-ni-ib-tar-re nam.Ø mu-nni-b.tar.e fate.ABS VEN-OO-3sgP.to decide.3sgA
He will decide a fate for him.
The compound verb n a m — t a r , “to decide a fate,” is constructed with the oblique object. The absolutive marks already the direct object n a m . The semantic object, however, the animate or inanimate noun that is affected by the verbal action, is marked with the OO. Compare, however, the verb n a m — k u 5 - ř , “to curse (lit. cut a fate),” written with the same signs, which appears not to be constructed with the OO: e.g., PN-r a n a m m u - n a - k u 5 - r e 6 , “he cursed PN.”
Example 14.40
šu mu-na-an-bar šu.Ø mu-nn.a-n.bar.Ø hand.ABS VEN-IO-3sgA.to remove.3sgP
He released someone/something for him/her.
šu mu-ni-in-bar šu.Ø mu-nn.i-n.bar.Ø hand.ABS VEN-OO-3sgA.to remove.3sgP
He released him/her.



CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES 175
The OO prefix /bi/ is identical to the local prefix /e/ while joined with the initial person prefix /b/: /bi/. The only difference is in their function: While the local /bi/ has a co-reference to the locative case, the oblique object prefix /bi/ co-references the directive.
Nominal case marker Verbal prefix Meaning
animate inanimate basic form Location Locative 1 – /a/ /ni/ in(to) Locative 2 /ra/ /a/ /e/ on(to) Locative 3 /ra/ /e/ /e/ next to, at
The third-person singular infix for an animate oblique object is /nni/. However, it can also be expressed by the final pronominal prefix /n/ before the verb base. The latter is only possible, if the final pronominal prefix does not already agree with an agent or a patient of a transitive sentence:
Example 14.41
ḫa-mu-ne-gi4-ge4 ḫa-mu-nne.gege.e
Let him send them here.
enim bi2-ib2-gi4-gi4-a enim.Ø b.i-b.gege.e-a
He who answers.
14.1.7.4. Causative constructions
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 429–34; ZOLYOMI 2017: 223–26; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 122
An important function of the oblique object is to mark the causee in a causative construction. Other agglutinating languages such as Turkish have similar features. In Turkish, a verbal stem can be followed by a suffix -tır (and similar) to produce the causative meaning: e.g., öl-mek, “to die” (mek is the infinitive suffix), öl-dür-mek, “to kill.” Sumerian is not entirely comparable to Turkish, since in Sumerian a causative construction is syntactical only.
A causative construction always has one more participant than the number of participants in either an intransitive or transitive sentence. The additional participant is called the causer. Adding an additional participant changes the syntactical function of the original subject or agent. In an intransitive sentence (see the following diagram) the original subject (absolutive) becomes the direct object (absolutive) of the verb and the causer the agent (ergative). The causative of an intransitive sentence is therefore morphologically identical to a transitive sentence.



176 CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES
The simple example sentence in the diagram shows that adding an additional participant to an intransitive sentence results in a transitive sentence.
Example 14.42
e2-a mu-un-ku4 e.a mu-n(i)-kur.Ø house.LOC VEN-LOC1-to enter.3sgS
He/it entered the house.
Example 14.43
e2-a mu-na-ni-ku4 e.a mu-nn.a-ni-(n.)kur.Ø house.LOC VEN-DAT-LOC1-3sgA.to enter.3sgP
He brought it into the house for him/her. (< “he let it enter in the house”)
The first sentence in the above example is intransitive. The /n/ before the base is here not the FPP, but the locative 1 infix /ni/ (see Chapter 14.1.7.1) agreeing with the locative postposition /a/. The infix is shortened to /n/, since in an intransitive sentence there is no FPP following it. The second sentence is transitive; another participant was added (“causer”), which is now expressed as the ergative. Since the verbal form is perfective, the ergative is agreed upon by the FPP. The full spelling of the locative 1 infix /ni/ shows that the ergative is present, but not realized in writing. The causative of an intransitive verb can also be expressed in passive form:
Example 14.44
e2-gal-la ba-an-ku4 egal.a ba-n(i)-kur.Ø palace.LOC MED-LOC1-to enter.3sgS
It was brought into the palace. (< it was being let enter in the palace”)
A simple non-causative transitive sentence contains two participants, an agent and a patient. Adding another participant transforms the agent into the causee, which is expressed as an oblique object, (1) dative for animate and (2) directive for inanimate causees. In addition, the causative of a transitive verb is expressed by the OO prefix in the verbal chain.
Subject
(absolutive) Verb
Causer

intranstive

munus.Ø
ensik.e
i-tuš.Ø
munus.Ø i-n.tuš.Ø



CHAPTER 14. ADVERBIAL INFIXES 177
Example 14.45
ninda mu-un-gu7 ninda.Ø mu-n.gu.Ø bread.ABS VEN-3sgA.to eat.3sgP
He ate bread.
Example 14.46
ninda mu-un-gu7-e ninda.Ø mu-n.gu.e bread.ABS VEN-3sgOO-to eat.3sgA
He has him eat bread.
Example 14.47
ninda mi-ni-in-gu7 ninda.Ø mu-nni-n.gu.Ø bread.ABS VEN-3sgOO-3sgA.to eat.3sgP
He had him eat bread.
The first example is a common transitive sentence. The sentence’s ergative is agreed upon by the FPP /n/; the absolutive is unmarked after the verb base. Although almost the same, the second sentence is a causative construction. The verbal form is imperfective: the ergative is agreed upon by the pronominal suffix /e/. The /n/ before the base cannot agree with the absolutive (n i n d a ). This /n/ is the OO, which agrees with a dative on an animate noun in the sentence. The third sentence, here a perfective form, contains the full form of the OO /nni/. The ergative is not optional and takes precedence before the base.

15. Verb V: Modal prefixes and negation
The previous chapters discussed the various components of the Sumerian finite verbal template. However, Sumerian can also express modality and negation by adding affixes. These affixes occur at the first position of the verbal template and therefore precede finite markers or conjugation prefixes (see Chapter 12). Most of these prefixes have specific meanings depending on whether the verbal form is perfective or imperfective as the following example of the modal prefix /ḫa/ or /ḫe/ shows:
Example 15.1
ḫe2-na-du11 ḫa-(i)-nn.a-(n.)dug.Ø MOD-(VP)-IO-3sgA.to speak.3sgP
He indeed told him.
Example 15.2
saĝĝa ḫe2-na-ab-be2 saĝĝa.ra ḫa-(i)-nn.a-b.e.e administrator.DAT MOD-(VP)-IO-3sgP.to speak.3sgA
May he tell the temple administrator.
Positive Negative statement Indicative /nu/
wish Imperative /na(n)/
assertion /ḫa/ and /ga/ /bara/
For the indicative see the forms discussed in Chapter 13 and 14. Sumerian has three positive modal forms:
• the imperative expresses direct commands and wishes. Due to its different configuration it is treated after the modal prefixes (see Chapter 15.2).
• a proclitic /ḫa/ expresses assertations (counterpart of /bara/) or requests (counterpart of /na(n)/ (see Chapter 15.1.4).
• A wish or promise expressed by the speaker(s) (i.e., cohortative) is expressed by the prefix /ga/ (see Chapter 15.1.7). The negative types are the following:
• an indicative form can be negated by a proclitic element /nu/ (see Chapter 15.1.3)
• the prefix /bara/ negates assertations (see Chapter 15.1.5)
• the prefix /na(n)/ negates wishes, requests, and commands (see Chapter 15.1.6)



CHAPTER 15. VERB V: MODAL PREFIXES AND NEGATION 183
15.1.2. Anteriority (/u/)
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 517–26; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 131–2; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 126–7
Like the finite markers /i/ (see Chapter 12.1.1) and /a/ (see Chapter 12.1.2) the prefix /u/ has also a vocalic shape. It does, however, not occur together with the negative proclitic (see Chapter 15.1.3). Based on its function as prefix of anteriority it only occurs in perfective forms. It is usually written
with the sign U3 ( ). However, the vowel of the prefix assimilates to the following open syllable: /u/ > /i/, if followed by /ni/ or /bi/; /u/ > /a/, if followed by /ba/. A spelling i 3 - m i - cannot be analyzed as **u-m-b.i-; this form would be written u 3 - m i - .145 It should also be noted that a spelling i 3 - n i - is a valid spelling for the prefix of anteriority, if the element - n i - is analyzed as locative 1 prefix (see Chapter 14.1.7.1), but not, if it is analyzed as OO (*i-nn.i-; see Chapter 14.1.7.3). This requires a spelling u 3 - n i - . The following table contains common spellings for the prefix of anteriority:
Analysis
u 3 u- he did not give it to them.
u 3 - n i - u-nn.i-(n./b.)VB
u 3 - m i - u-i-m-b.i
u m - m i - u-im.b.i
u3-un
un
u3-um
um
u3-ma
u3-ul
u3-ub
u3-mu
i - b i 2 - u-b.i
i 3 - n i - u-ni
145 The spelling u m - m i - is attested in Old Babylonian texts.



184 CHAPTER 15. VERB V: MODAL PREFIXES AND NEGATION
a - b a - u-ba
Similar to the prefix /a/ (see Chapter 12.1.2), which appears as /al/ directly before the verbal base, the prefix of anteriority is written (usually as plene spelling) u 3 - u l - :
Example 15.3
u3-ul-pa3 zi-re-dam u-pad.Ø zer.ed-Ø-am ANT-to find.3sgS to destroy.IMPF-NFIN-COP
When it (i.e., the sealed document) is found, it is to be destroyed. (OrSp 47–49, 411; Ur III; Umma)
Forms with the prefix of anteriority are subordinate to the following indicative or modal form. A translation “when” or “after” is most often applicable. A form with this prefix is often part of a complex sentence. The verbal action or state expressed by it is anterior to the one expressed by the following indicative form:
Example 15.4
dnin-ĝir2-su-ke4 sa-šuš gal-ne2 u3-ni-šu4 Nin-Ĝirsu.ak.e sašuš gal-ane.Ø u-nn.i-(n.)šu.Ø DN.ERG battle-net large-POSS.ABS ANT-OO-3sgA.to throw.3sgP
Ninĝirsu, having thrown his big battle-net over him,
šu maḫ ĝiri3 maḫ-ne2 an-ta ḫe2-ĝa2-ĝa2 šu maḫ ĝiri maḫ-ane.Ø an.ta ḫa-(i)-(n-)ĝaĝa.e hand august foot august-POSS.ABS sky.ABL MOD-VP-OO-to place.3sgA
may he put his august hands and feet on him from above. (Ent 28 vi,21–25; ED IIIb; Girsu)
In this example, the clause expressed by the prefix of anteriority functions like a subordinated clause (see Chapter 18). Ninĝirsu is the subject to both, the subordinated and the main clause.
Although very close in meaning, the prefix /u/ meaning “when” (i.e., “under the circumstance that”) usually does not express a meaning “at the time when.” For this type of temporal expression, Sumerian uses a subordinate clause marked by the locative. Such a form is attested in Text 7:146
Example 15.5
u4 niĝ2-si-sa2 ki-en-ge ki-uri-a i-ni-in-ĝar-ra-a ud niĝ-sisa.Ø ki’enge ki’uri.a i-ni-n.ĝar.Ø-a.a
day justice.ABS Sumer Akkad.LOC VP-LOC1-3sgA.to place.3sgP-NOM.LOC
When (lit. on the day) he established justice in Sumer and Akkad.
A form with the prefix of anteriority also is somewhat close to conditional clauses (see Chapter 18.2.6). It is, however, far more common to introduce these clauses with u 4 - d a or t u k u m - b e 2 , “if.” Forms with the prefix of anteriority can also be nominalized:
146 The context there suggests a first person agent. For subordination see Chapter 18. JAGERSMA 2010: 523 notes examples of such constructions that contain a form with the prefix of anteriority.



CHAPTER 15. VERB V: MODAL PREFIXES AND NEGATION 185
Example 15.6
igi uĝ3-še3 u3-ši-bar-ra-zu šeĝ3 ḫe2-ĝal2-la-am3 igi.Ø uĝ.še u-b.ši-(n.)bar.Ø-a-zu šeĝ eye.ABS people.TERM ANT-TERM-3sgA.to open.3sgP-NOM-POSS
Your having looked at the people .
The earliest letters date to the ED IIIb period. In their formulary they use an imperative d u 1 1 - g a n a , “say it to him/her” (*dug-a-nn.a-b), which corresponds to Akkadian qibī-ma in later Akkadian letters. Starting in the Old Akkadian period, letters use the form u 3 - n a - a - d u 1 1 instead. Literally, this form can be translated as “in the circumstance that you have said it to him/her” or “after you have said it to him/her.” A translation as polite imperative is here appropriate: “tell him/her please.”
15.1.3. Negation (/nu/)
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 551–55; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 237–39; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 125–6
The prefix /nu/ (also called negative proclitic) negates both finite and non-finite verbs (for the latter, see Chapter 16). It usually precedes a finite marker or other “conjugation prefix.” Followed by the general finite marker /i/ (see Chapter 12.1.1), the vowel assimilates to the vowel in the negative prefix, which sometimes results in a plene writing n u - u 3 -. Such plene spellings indicate that the contraction resulted in a lengthening of the vowel: /nū/.
The proclitic /nu/ negates indicative forms and does usually not express modality. For this purpose, Sumerian uses the modal prefixes /na(n)/ (see Chapter 15.1.6) or /bara/ (see Chapter 15.1.5). These modal prefixes express negative wishes, commands, and assertions. The following table contains the most common forms of the proclitic /nu/ together with examples:
Examples
n u - n u - n e - š u m 2 he did not give it to them.
n u - u 3 - n u - u 3 - n a - š u m 2 he did not give it to her.
n u - i 3 - n u - i 3 - n a - š u m 2 he did not give it to her.
nu-mu
nu-mi
nu-um-mi
nu-ma
nu-um-ma
nu-ga

nu-un-ga
n u - b a - n u - b a - a b - d u 8 it is not released.
n u - b i 2 - e r 2 n u - b i 2 - d u 1 1 she did not utter laments.
la-ba
li2-bi2

The combinations n u - b a - and n u - b i 2 - are forms that can be found in earlier Sumerian: e.g., n u - b a - š u m 2 - m u , “he will/would not give it to them” (*nu-b.a-(b.)šum.e); l u 2 - u 3 n u - b i 2 n a , “he did not let them do it” (*lu.e nu-b.i-n.ak.Ø).147 From the Ur III period onwards, /nu/ changes to either /la/ before /ba/ or /li/ before /bi/: l a - b a - a n - k u 4 , “it was not brought into it” (*nu-ba-n(i)-kur.Ø); e n i m l i 2 - b i 2 - i n - g e , “he did not corroborate (lit. made firm) the statement about it” (*enim.Ø nu-b.i-n.gen.Ø).148
In contrast to modal prefixes, /nu/ can also negate non-finite verbal forms such as participles: e.g., g a b a - r i n u - t u k u , “the one who has no opponent” (gaba-ri.Ø nu-tuku.Ø); e r e n 2 š e n u t u k u - m e , “they are laborers who have no barley” (*eren še.Ø nu-tuku.Ø-me.eš). It is, however, different to the particle /nu/ in some occupations (e.g., n u - ĝ e š k i r i 6 ; see Chapter 5.2.3).
The element /nu/ can also be used on its own as in the following passage from one of Gudea’s statue inscriptions (Gudea Statue B vii,49–54):
Example 15.7
a l a n - e This statue alan-e.Ø
statue-DEM.ABS
u 3 k u 3 n u z a - g i n 3 n u - g a - a m 3 is neither of silver and also not of lapis lazuli, u kug.Ø nu zagin.Ø nu-i-nga-me.Ø CON silver.ABS NEG lapis.ABS NEG-VP-COO-to be.3sgS
u 3 u r u d a n u u 3 a n - n a n u nor of copper nor tin, u uruda.Ø nu u anna.Ø nu
CON copper.ABS NEG CON tin.ABS NEG
z a b a r n u nor of bronze, zabar.Ø nu bronze.ABS NEG
147 Please not the verb a 5 - k (or a k ), “to do, make,” is frequently written with a syllabogram that combines the base and the preceding FPP. 148 This change has likely phonetic reasons. An influence from Akkadian lā appears not entirely implausible. The proximity of the sounds /n/ and /l/ can also be observed in texts from Ebla, which represent the title l u g a l as n u g a l . Noteworthy is also the occupation n u - b a n d a 3 , “inspector,” which has an Akkadian equivalent laputtû. See more on this in Chapter 3.2.

k i ĝ 2 - ĝ e 2 6 l u 2 n u - b a - ĝ a 2 - ĝ a 2 nobody was supposed to use: kiĝ.e lu.e nu-b.a-ĝaĝa.e work.DIR man.ERG NEG-IO-3sgP.to place.3sgA
n a 4 e s i - a m 3 it is of diorite. esi.Ø-am diorite.ABS-COP

15.1.4. Precative/Affirmative (/ḫa/)
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 558–65; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 130–2
The basic form of this prefix (according to JAGERSMA 2010 a proclitic like /nu/) is /ḫa/.149 Old
Sumerian texts clearly differentiate between the spellings ḫ a - ( ) and ḫ e 2 - ( ), the latter being a contraction of /ḫa/ and the finite marker /i/ or /e/.
Example 15.8 ḫa-ma-ti ḫa-ma-til.Ø MOD-DAT-to live.3sgS
May he live for me.
Example 15.9
ḫe2-na-be2 ḫa-i-nn.a-b.e.e MOD-VP-DAT-3sgP.to say.3sgA
May he say it to him.
The following table contains the most common spellings for this prefix:
Examples
ḫa
ḫa-ba
ḫa-ab
ḫe2
ḫe2-bi2
149 SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023 establish as basic form /ḫe/.



188 CHAPTER 15. VERB V: MODAL PREFIXES AND NEGATION
ḫe2-be2
ḫe2-eb
ḫe2-ab
ḫe2-en
ḫe2-mi
ḫe2-em
ḫe
ḫu-mu
ḫa-mu
Texts of the early second millennium BC show not a straightforward distribution of spellings any longer. Furthermore, /ḫa/ may assimilate to following /mu/: e.g., ḫ u - m u - n a - a b - š u m 2 - m u , “he should give it to him” (*ḫa-mu-nn.a-b.šum.e). The prefix /ḫa/ can occasionally occur with the first person, but has then a slightly different meaning than the prefix /ga/ (see Chapter 15.1.7).
The prefix /ḫa/ expresses assertions, wishes, and commands, but there are differences between the usage of /ḫa/ in imperfective and perfective forms: – /ḫa/ + imperfective: transitive action expressing wish or command – /ḫa/ + perfective: intransitive or stative expressing wish or command, but is also used to put forward a strong and definitive assertion.
Example 15.10
0.2.0 še ur-dnin-mug-ra ḫe2-na-ab-šum2-mu 0.2.0 barley.Ø Ur-Ninmug.(ak).ra ḫa-i-nn.a-b.šum.e NUM barley.ABS PN.DAT MOD-VP-DAT-3sgP.to give.3sgA
He should give 2 barig barley to Ur-Ninmug.
Example 15.11
nuĝun-na-ne2 ḫe2-eb2-til-ne nuĝun-ane.Ø ḫa-i-b.til.ene seed-POSS.ABS MOD-VP-3sgP.to end.3sgA
May (the gods) end his lineage.
Both forms are imperfective and hence express a wish or command. A wish or command can also be expressed in perfective forms. However, the respective forms concern a state or are passive:



CHAPTER 15. VERB V: MODAL PREFIXES AND NEGATION 189
Example 15.12
nuĝun-a-ne2 ḫe2-til nuĝun-ane.Ø ḫa-i-til.Ø seed-POSS.ABS MOD-VP-to end.3sgS
May his lineage come to an end.
Example 15.13
nam-ti-la-ne2-še3 dnin-ĝir2-su2-ra e2-ninnu-a nam-til-ane.še Nin-Ĝirsu.ak.ra E-ninnu.a life-POSS.TERM DN.DAT TN.LOC
giri17 šu ḫe2-na-še3-ĝal2 giri.e šu.Ø ḫa-i-nn.a-ši-(n.)ĝal.Ø nose.DIR hand.ABS MOD-VP-DAT-3sgA.to be present.3sgP
May he (i.e., personal god) pay obeisance to Ninĝirsu in the Eninnu for his life. (lit. “may he let the hand be present at the nose”) (Ent 27; ED IIIb; Girsu)
Specific verbs such as t u k u , “to have,” or z u , “to know,” also use perfective forms:
Example 15.14
lugal-ĝu10 ḫe2-en-zu lugal-ĝu.e ḫa-i-n.zu.Ø king-POSS.ERG MOD-VP-3sgA.to know.3sgP
May my king know it.
Apart from wishes and commands, perfective forms can also express an affirmative sense in the past. Translations with “indeed” or “truly” are most often appropriate:
Example 15.15
šeg12 kul-aba4ki-še3 ḫe2-em-ma-ni-in-ku4-re-en šeg Kulaba.ak.še ḫa-i-m-ba-ni-n.kur.en brick work GN.GEN.TERM MOD-VP-VEN-MED-LOC1-3sgA.to enter.1sgP
He/She truly brought me into the brick work of Kulaba. (Lugalbanda II, 318; OB)
The prefix /ḫa/ always expresses a positive modality. It cannot be negated. If an assertion like the one in Example 15.5 should be expressed in a negative sense, then Sumerian uses the modal prefix /bara/, “I truly did not ...” (see Chapter 15.1.5). The negative counterpart of positive wishes and commands is rendered with the prefix /nan/ (see Chapter 15.1.6).
Apart from wishes and commands (both imperfective and perfective) as well as assertions (only perfective), modal forms with /ḫa/ come also close to a conditional meaning:
Example 15.16
ḫe2-eb2-ta-ku5-e ga-mu-na-ab-silim ḫa-i-b.ta-(b.)kur.e ga-mu-nn.a-b.silim MOD-VP-IPP.ABL-3sgP.to break.3sgA MOD-VEN-DAT-3sgP.to repair
Should he break it, I shall repair it for him.



190 CHAPTER 15. VERB V: MODAL PREFIXES AND NEGATION
Modal forms with /ḫa/ can here also be combined with t u k u m - b e 2 , “if” (see Chapter 18.2.6).
Similar to the negative proclitic /nu/, /ḫa/ or /ḫe/ can also be used on its own:
Example 15.17
eg2 ki-sur-ra dnanše-ka am6-ta-bala-e-da lu2 ummaki ḫe2 eg kisura Nanše.ak.a a-m-b.ta-e-bala-ed.Ø-a lu Umma.ak.Ø ḫe
dyke border DN.GEN.LOC VP-VEN-IPP.ABL-LOC2-to cross-IMPF.3sgS-NOM man GN.GEN.ABS MOD
lu2 kur-ra ḫe2 den-lil2-le ḫe2-ḫa-lam-me lu kur.ak.Ø ḫe Enlil.e ḫa-i-(n.)ḫalam.e
man mountain.GEN.ABS MOD DN.ERG MOD-VP-3sgP.to destroy.3sgA
May Enlil destroy the man who crosses the boundary dyke of Nanše, be it a man of Umma or a foreigner. (Ent 1 vi,12–20; ED IIIb; Girsu)
Such a form can also be followed by the enclitic copula (see Chapter 8): ḫ e 2 - a m 3 or ḫ e 2 - a . Forms such as these are traditionally analyzed *ḫa-i-me.Ø, but it seems more likely that the prefix behaves here similar to the negative proclitic.
15.1.5. Vetitive (/bara/)
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 572–75; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 133
The prefix /bara/ is always written b a - r a - ( ).150 It expresses a firm negation (“certainly not”) and can be used with both perfective and imperfective forms. In its meaning is represents the opposite of the prefixes g a - (see Chapter 15.1.7) and ḫ a - (see Chapter 15.1.4). The prefix /bara/ used with an imperfective denotes a strong denial of a present or future action. Used with a perfective, the form expresses a strong denial of a past, present, or future state. The following examples use the imperfective base and are to be understood as counterpart to the cohortative:
Example 15.18
ki-sur-ra dnin-ĝir2-su2-ka-ke4 ba-ra-mu-bala-e kisura Nin-Ĝirsu.ak.ak.e bara-mu-(b?)-bala.e(n) border DN.DIR MOD-VEN-LOC3151-to cross.1sgS152
I certainly will not cross the border of Ninĝirsu. (Ean 1; ED IIIb; Lagaš)
Example 15.19
ba-ra-zaḫ3-e-de3-en bara-zaḫ.ed.en MOD-to run away.IMPF.2sgS
I certainly will not run away.
150 ZOLYOMI 2017: 246 locates /bara/ at his “slot 2” (S2), because it does not occur together with the finite marker /i/. 151 JAGERSMA 2010: 574 analyzes a syncopated LOC1, but this should not refer to the DIR on the nominal template. The compound verb š u — b a l a , however, agrees with LOC on the nominal template: e.g., š u n a - a n - n i - i b 2 b a l a - e , “may he not change it” (lit. “may he not let his hands go across it”; *šu nan-ni-b.bala.e). 152 It is not uncommon that the first and second person pronominal suffixes are shortened and loose the final /n/.



CHAPTER 15. VERB V: MODAL PREFIXES AND NEGATION 191
Example 15.20
šu-ĝu10 ba-ra-ba-ta-e3 šu-ĝu.ta bara-ba-(b.)ta-’.e.Ø hand-POSS.ABL MOD-MED-ABL-1sgA-to go out.3sgP
I certainly did not let him escape my hand.
Although these examples do not mark the first or second person singular explicitly, their context suggests this interpretation. Second and third person forms are possible as well:
Example 15.21
ba-ra-gi4-gi4-nam bara-gigi.en-a-am MOD-to return-3sgS-NOM-COP
It is so that you will never return!153
Example 15.22
u4-be2-ta im-ma gub-bu ḫe2-ĝal ud-be.ta im.a gub.e(d).Ø ḫa-i-ĝal.Ø bara-gigi.en-a-am
im si-si-ge ba-ra-ĝal2-la-am3 im sisig.e(d).Ø bara-ĝal.Ø-a-am
Since these days writing on tablets certainly existed, but enveloping tablets did certainly not exist. (Sargon Legend 53; OB)
It should be noted that the spelling ba-ra- may also indicate a middle prefix followed by the second person singular IO.
15.1.6. Prohibitive (/na(n)/)
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 565–69; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 132–33
Besides /bara/ (see Chapter 15.1.5) the modal prefix /na(n)/ expresses negative modality. It denotes,
in particular, a negative command or request. The prefix is usually written with the sign NA ( ). It cannot be preceded by any other prefix. Perfective forms starting with /na(n)/ only concern stative verbs. However, the modal prefix is most commonly found with imperfective forms:
Example 15.23
nam-mi-gur-re na-m-b.i-(b.)gur.e MOD-VEN-LOC2-3sgP.to go back.3sgA
He should not return to (this matter).
Example 15.24
lu2 na-ab-dab5-e lu.e na-b.dab.e man.ERG MOD-3sgP.to seize.3sgA
Nobody should seize it.
153 The form without nominalization (see Chapter 18) is b a - r a - g i 4 - g i 4 - i n .



192 CHAPTER 15. VERB V: MODAL PREFIXES AND NEGATION
Examples
na
n a - b a - n a - b a - r a - s a 1 0 he should not sell him.
na-ma
nam-mi
nam-bi2
na-mi- na-mi-gur-re
na-bi2
na-an-ni
na-a-an-ni
nam-mu
na-mu
na-an- nan
Without a context, some forms may be ambivalent. A verb starting with the sequence na-ba- may also contain the negative proclitic /nu/. Thus, a form na-ba-ra-sa10 can either mean “he should not sell him” (*na(n)-ba-ta-(b.)sa.e) or “he did not sell him” (*nu-ba-ta-(n.)sa.Ø).
Due to its different form the imperative will be discussed in ch. 15.2. Its negative counterpart is the prefix /na(n)/, which expresses a negative command or a negative request. In most instances /na(n)/ is used with imperfective forms. Jagersma 2010 differentiates prohibitive /na(n)/ from affirmative /na/ that is usually used with perfective forms. The (full) form /nan/ is used before a single consonant; /na/ before two consonants: e.g., na-na-šum2-mu, “he should not give it to her” (*nann.a-(b.)šum.e), na-an-na-ni-ib-gi4-gi4, “he should not return it for him” (*na-nn.a-ni-b.gigi.e).
If the prefix /na(n)/ is followed by the ventive /m/, it is either written nam- or only na-: e.g., nammi-gur-re and na-mi-gur-re, “he should not refuse this” (< he should not let [the order] go back on this; *na-m.bi-(b.)gur.e). The full form /na(n)/ is occasionally attested in forms such as šu na-anba-ab-ti-ti, “he should not receive it” (*nan-ba-b.titi.e). The prefix /na(n)/ may assimilate to following /mu/ as in nam-mi-ni-in-dib-be2-en, “I should not let him pass there” (*nan-mu-ni



CHAPTER 15. VERB V: MODAL PREFIXES AND NEGATION 193
n.dib.en). Finally /na(n)/ might occur directly before the verb base: e.g., e2-gal-še3 na-an-du-un, “I do not want to go to the palace” (*nan-du.en).
15.1.7. Cohortative (ga-)
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 569–72; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 241–43; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 129–30
The prefix /ga/ expresses an assertion or wish referring to the speaker. It only occurs with the firstperson singular or plural. The first person is not expressed separately, but only by the modal prefix. Texts of the third millennium BCE do not differentiate between first-person singular and plural: g a - š e 3 - s a 1 0 , “I want to buy it from you” (*ga-e.ši-(b.)sa). Only in the Old Babylonian period the person suffix -/enden/ is regularly added to the form: e.g., g a - b a - ḫ u l 2 - ḫ u l 2 - l e - e n - d e 3 e n , “let us be happy” (*ga-ba-ḫulḫul.enden). The prefix /ga/ cannot be negated. In such a case, Sumerian uses the modal prefix /bara/ (see Chapter 15.1.5).
The prefix /ga/ uses the perfective base, but inflects according to the imperfective:
Example 15.25
ĝešgem-be2 ga-ra-ab-šum2 ĝešgem-be.Ø ga-ra-b.šum sign-POSS.ABS COH-DAT-3sgP.to give
I will give you its sign.
Despite using a perfective base, the object or patient can be referred to by a final person prefix before the base. The presence of this prefix is, however, optional:
Example 15.26
ki-ba ga-ra-a-ĝa2-ar ki-be.a ga-ra-e-šum place-POSS.LOC COH-DAT-LOC2-to place
I will put it on its place.
While the prefix /ga/ is usually written with the sign GA, there are conditions in which it the prefix is colored to /gu/ and /gi/: e.g., g i 4 - b i 2 - i b 2 - g u 7 , “I will let them eat it” (*ga-bi-b.gu).
Examples
ga
g a - m u - g a - m u - r a - a b - š u m 2 let me give it to you.
g a - r a - g a - r a - a b - d u 1 1 let me say it to you.
ga-na
ga-an
gu2-mu- gu2-mu-ri2-ib2-tar



194 CHAPTER 15. VERB V: MODAL PREFIXES AND NEGATION
gi4-bi2
gi4-ni
gi4-ri2
As we have seen, the prefix /ga/ can also be found in frozen forms that are used as nouns (see Chapter 5.2.4): e.g., g a b 2 - s a r , “jeweller” (lit. “let me engrave it”); g a - a b - s a 1 0 , “buyer” (lit. “let me buy it”), g a b 2 - i l 2 , “carrier” (< “I want to carry it”), g a b 2 - r a , “herder assistant” (< “I want to beat it”), g a - a b - s a 1 0 , “buyer” (< “I want to buy it”), g a - a n - t u š , “tenant” (< “I want to sit in it.”).154
15.1.8. Non-negative coordination prefix (na-)
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 579–81; ZOLYOMI 2017: 248–9; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 128
We have seen already in Chapter 15.1.6 that Sumerian has a prefix /na/ or /na(n)/ that expresses a negative wish and is usually attested with the imperfective. Sumerian literary texts, but also a few royal inscriptions, in particular, attest to this prefix /na/. While it is clear that it has a non-negative connotation, it appears that the prefix coordinates sentences and as such flags attention for what
follows. The prefix is not affected by vowel harmony and is usually written with the sign NA (
). When followed by a ventive, the spelling with the sign NAM ( ) is common.
Example 15.27
ma2-gur8-ra-na ĝiri3 nam-mi-gub magur-ane.a ĝiri.Ø na-(i)-m-b.i-(n.)gub.Ø processional boat-POSS.LOC foot.ABS MOD-VP-VENT-LOC2-3sgA.to stand.3sgP
He placed the feet on his processional boat.
A somewhat problematic issue is the form n a - a b - b e 2 - a (also n a - b e 2 - a and n a - e - a ) in the letter address. This form was interpreted differently. JAGERSMA (following Krecher and Attinger) wants to analyze the form as a fusion of the interrogative pronoun a - n a , “what,” and the subordinated finite form a b - b e 2 - a (*a-b.e.e-a).155 EDZARD suggests that initial /na/ in this form is the IO (see Chapter 14.1.3). SALLABERGER and ZOLYOMI, however, interpret it as the coordinating modal prefix /na/.
Example 15.28
ur-lugal-ke4 na-be2-a enim-ma-ra u3-na-du11 Ur-lugal.ak.e na-b.e.e-a Enim.ak.ra u-nn.a-e.dug.Ø PN.ERG MOD-3sgP.to say.3sgA-NOM.ABS PN.DAT MOD-DAT-2sgA.to say.3sgP
That which Urlugal says in the following, when you have said (this) to Enima.
154 For a discussion see SELZ 1993. 155 For some forms see ATTINGER 1993: 264. The letter UET 3, 5 contains (not in the address) an expression a - n a a n a - b e 2 - a , “what he had told/promised him.” Compare also YOS 4, 138: (5) a - n a n a - b e 2 - a ḫ e 2 - n a š u m 2 - m u , “may he provide him with what he has told him.”



CHAPTER 15. VERB V: MODAL PREFIXES AND NEGATION 195
15.1.9. The prefix /ši/
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 577–9; THOMSEN 1984: 206–8; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 127–8
This prefix with its basic form /ši/ is attested with both perfective and imperfective. It often appears in narrative text. It precedes the the Coordinator prefix /nga/ (see Chapter 12.1.3), but appears to be on the same position as the general finite marker /i/. The Old Sumerian evidence suggests that originally the basic form of the prefix was /še/. With the Old Babylonian period, spellings with /ša/ and /šu/ are also attested, the latter, for instance, before the ventive prefix m u - .
Examples
ši- ši-in-ga-tum2-mu
še3- še3-mu-ni-in-de5
ša4- ša4-ba-dab5
ša- ša-ra-ni-ĝal2
šu-mu- šu-mu-un-ši-ĝal2
ša-mu- ša-mu-ni-ib2-tar-re
The exact function and meaning of /ši/ are difficult to discern from the evidence at hand. The prefix is rather frequent in the Instructions of Šuruppak. In almost half of the attestations there a form with /ši/ follows a form with negative /nan/ (see Chapter 15.1.6):
Example 15.29
gana2-zu-am3 pu2 na-an-ni-du3-e-en gana-zu-am pu.Ø nan-ni-b.du.en field.POSS-COP well.ABS MOD-LOC1-3sgP.to build.2sgA
uĝ3-e ša-ri-ib-ḫul-ḫul uĝ.e ša-ri-b.ḫulhul.Ø people.ERG MOD-LOC1-3sgP.to build.2sgA
Do not place a well in your own field, as people will become hostile against you. (Instructions of Šuruppak 17)
It appears that the prefix /ši/ has a coordinating function and expresses an action or state that happens as consequence to the verb mentioned before. It is already attested as such in the ED IIIa texts from Tell Abū Ṣalābīkh. Later on, it appears that it also serves a rhetorical function, which has no direct impact on meaning.156 This can, in particular, be seen in hymns. Affirmative meaning has been suggested, but this is difficult to prove.
156 See also CIVIL 2005: 38–9.



196 CHAPTER 15. VERB V: MODAL PREFIXES AND NEGATION
Example 15.30
e2-tar-sir2-sir2 an-ne2 ĝar-ra-za nam kur-kur-ra ša-mu-ni-ib2-tar-re Etarsirsir an.e ĝar-a-zu.a nam kurkur.a(k).Ø ši-mu-nn.i-b.tar.en TN sky.ERG to place-PERF-POSS.LOC fate mountains.GEN.ABS MOD-VEN-OO-3sgP.to decide.2sgA
In your Etarsirsir, founded by An, you decide the fate of all the countries. (Luma A 12–3; early OB)
15.1.10. The prefixes /nuš/ and /iri/
Literature: THOMSEN 1984: 211–3; ATTINGER 1993: 296–7; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 134
The rarely attested prefix /nuš/ is written n u - u š - ; before the coordination prefix /nga/ it can also be written n u - š i - i n - g a - . Other spellings are n e 2 - e š - and n i - i š - . The prefix can appear directly before the base. It only occurs in literary texts such as songs of praise or city laments dating to the Old Babylonian period, but which go back to the Ur III period. It probably expresses a hypothetical wish and therefore a verbal action that is wished for, but does not come into being. This prefix was also referred to as frustrative:
Example 15.31
nu-uš-ma-ab-be2-en nuš-ma-b.e.en MOD-DAT-3sgP.to say.3sgA
If only you could tell me.
Example 15.32
kala-ga-ĝu10 u2-šem-gen7 edin-na nu-uš-ma-da-mu2-am3 kalaga-ĝu.Ø u-šem.gen edin.a nuš-ma-da-mu.Ø-a-am
If only my strong one would grow for me like herbs in the steppe. (Ur-Namma A 214)
A prefix /iri/ is even less well attested. It can be written with the sign URU read i r i - , but also i r i - and i - r i 2 - . The latter spelling could be interpreted as an OO of the second person singular (see Chapter 14.1.7.3). The prefix appears only to occur with the verb d u 1 1 - g , “to say,” in constructions such as m i m - - d u 1 1 - g , “to take good care, to praise.” What exact function it has, remains unclear.
Example 15.33
nin-e ni2-te-a-ne2 mim zi iri-in-ga-am3-me nin.e nite-ane.e mim zid.Ø iri-nga-m-b.e.e
The lady praises herself.
15.2. Imperative
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 556–8; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 240–1; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 129
While the prefix /ḫa/ (see Chapter 15.1.4) expresses a wish or request also to the second person (e.g., š e - ĝ u 1 0 ḫ a - m u - t u m 3 , “you should bring my barley!” [*ḫa-mu-e.tum.Ø]), the imperative expresses a direct command to a person. Morphologically, the imperative functions differently than finite verbal forms. It is not expressed by a separate morpheme (e.g., prefix), but by a re-modelling of the verbal phrase: imperative forms start with the perfective base, which is followed by the prefixes of the finite verbal form:



CHAPTER 15. VERB V: MODAL PREFIXES AND NEGATION 197
Example 15.34
ma-an-šum2 ma-n.šum.Ø DAT-3sgA.to give.3sgP
He gave it to me.
šum2-ma-ab šum-ma-b to give-DAT-3sgP
Give it to me!
Similar to the modal prefix /ga/ the imperative marks the object or patient, where the corresponding finite form would mark the subject or agent. Hence, š u m 2 - m a - a n means “Give him/her to me!” Therefore, despite using the perfective base the final person prefix follows the pattern of the imperfective.
If a finite verbal form starts with the vocalic finite marker /i/ (or /e/), its correspondence in an imperative form would be /a/:
Example 15.35
in-na-an-du11 i-nn.a-n.dug.Ø VP-DAT-3sgA.to say.3sgP
He said it to him.
du11-ga-na dug-a-nn.a-(b) to say-VP-DAT-3sgP
Say it to him!
Plural imperative forms add a suffix /(n)zen/:
Example 15.36
nin9-ĝu10 tum2-mu-un-ze2-en nin-ĝu.Ø tum-mu-n.zen VP-DAT-3sgA.to say.3sgP
Bring my sister!
Imperative forms can often be rather short. A form ĝ e n - n a can mean both “Go!” or (as a nonfinite verbal form) “gone” (see Chapter 16.3).



198 CHAPTER 15. VERB V: MODAL PREFIXES AND NEGATION
Texts 10 and 11 (Two Letters)
The following letters are typical letter orders attested in the Ur III period. Letters follow a certain formulary. Both letters contain a verbal form, which is characteristic for letters: u 3 - n a - a - d u 1 1 , (lit.) “when you have told it to him/her” (*u-nn.a-e.dug.Ø). The “you” refers to the messenger, who delivers the letter. Lexical texts render the form as qibīšum-ma, “speak to him.” This, of course, reminds of Akkadian letters. The form is sometimes translated as a (polite) imperative (see Chapter 15.1.2). This verbal form was also loaned into Akkadian and used there to refer to letters (besides tuppum): unnedukkum.
The first letter (YBC 754) only contains the addressee, who is marked with the dative. The second letter (YBC 1317) also mentions the sender, in this case the king. This part of the letter formulary contains the verbal form n a - a b - b e 2 - a . In Sargonic period texts, it is usually written n a - b e 2 a , and even earlier, n a - e - a . For the different discussions of this form, which translates to something like “that which so-and-so says in the following,” see Chapter 15.1.8.
