16. Non-finite verbal forms
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 130–42; JAGERSMA 2010: 627–76; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 91–4; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 60–3
Sumerian has several non-finite verbal forms, also known as participles. Such non-finite forms have none of the pre- or suffixes used in finite verbal chains. However, a non-finite form can be negated by the prefix /nu/ (see Chapter 15.1.3). The three forms are the following:157
*ĝar-Ø consists of the perfective stem only. The form corresponds in meaning to a present participle.
*ĝar-a consists of the perfective stem with following suffix /a/ as nominalizer. This form corresponds to a past participle.
*ĝaĝa-ed consists of the imperfective stem with following suffix /ed/. This form corresponds to an imperfective participle.
Non-finite verbal forms are widely used in Sumerian texts. By adding additional suffixes, nonfinite forms may express a purpose (e.g., d u 3 - d e 3 , “in order to build”; see Chapter 16.6) or a time (e.g., d u 3 - a - n e 2 , “when he built”; see Chapter 16.7). These particular functions of non-finite verbal forms are discussed below. The stem of such non-finite verbal forms may be reduplicated in order to express verbal number: e.g., ĝ a r - ĝ a r - r e , “one who repeatedly places.” Any of these forms can also be negated using the negation prefix n u - (see Chapter 15.1.3): e.g., n u - š u m 2 m u , “someone who does/will not give” (*nu-šum-ed.Ø).
As non-finite forms they can be followed by the enclitic copula (see Chapter 8) or case markers.
Example 16.1
nam-ti šum2-ma-me-en nam-til.Ø šum-a-men
life.ABS to give-PFTPART-COP
I am/You are the one, to whom life was given.
Example 16.2
gu3-de2-a e2-ĝu10 du3-da ĝesgem-be2 ga-ra-ab-šum2 Gudea.Ø e.ĝu.Ø du-ed.a(k) ĝešgem.be.Ø ga-ra-b.šum PN.ABS house-POSS.ABS to build-PRESPART.GEN sign-POSS.ABS COH-DAT-3sgP.to give
Gudea, let me tell you the ominous sign of building my house.
Non-finite forms can be used as verbal adjectives and as verbal nouns. When used as a verbal adjective, the non-finite form refers to the participant who plays a role in the action or state expressed by the verb (JAGERSMA 2010: 631–4; 643–9; 662–5). Compare the following two examples:
157 Note that JAGERSMA 2010 analyzed non-finite forms with a Ø morpheme (glossed NFIN): e.g., i r i - t a n u - e 3 , “one who does not leave the city” (*nu-e-Ø). In this overview, such a Ø morpheme is avoided.



Example 16.3
DN d i ĝ i r g u 3 - d e 2 - a e n s i 2 l a g a š k i l u 2 e 2 - a n - n a i n - d u 3 - a - k a m DN diĝir Gudea ensik Lagaš.a(k) lu E-an.a(k).Ø i-n.du.Ø-a.ak-am
DN deity Gudea city governor Lagaš.GEN man TN.ABS VP-3sgA.to build.3sgP-NOM.GEN-COP
DN is the (personal) god of Gudea, the ruler of L., the man, who built the Eana.
Example 16.4
gu3-de2-a lu2 e2 du3-a-ra Gu-de-a lu e.Ø du-a.a(k).ra
For Gudea, the man who built the house.
The first example contains a subordinated finite clause (see Chapter 18). It qualifies the nomen rectum of the genitive construction (i.e., Gudea). In order to add the genitive postposition, the clause needs to be nominalized. The second example with a very similar meaning contains a nonfinite statement. Literally it is the “man of having built the house.” The genitive is visible in spellings such as l u 2 e 2 d u 3 - a - k e 4 .158 The head noun is often the transitive subject of the relative clause. In the preceding examples, it is the man (l u 2 ) who built the house. With intransitive verbs, the head noun corresponds to the intransitive subject. However, the head noun can also play the role of an indirect object:
Example 16.5
e n s i 2 ĝ e š - t u 9 ĝ e š t u š u m 2 - m a d e n - k i - k a The ruler who is given wisdom by Enki. ensik ĝeštug.Ø šum-a Enkik.a(k)
u r - s a ĝ p a r a 1 0 k u 3 - g a t u š - a - r a For the warrior sitting on the holy dais. ursaĝ parag kug.a tuš-a.ra
The head noun may of course also be omitted. This happens, in particular, when the context is clear. Furthermore, the head noun can play the role of a direct object of the verbal action, or can also function as the possessor of a non-finite relative clause:
Example 16.6
e n d u 1 1 - g a - n e 2 s a ĝ - b i š e 3 - a Lord whose commands take precedence. en dug-ane.Ø saĝ-be.še e-a
A non-finite verbal form used as verbal noun does not refer to a participant of the verbal action or state, but to the action or state itself (JAGERSMA 2010: 634–6; 650–5; 665–71). The non-finite form functions then as the subject or object of a verb or can be used as adverb.
Example 16.7
m u l u 2 k a s 4 - k e 4 - n e - š e 3 Because159 of the couriers (< “men of running”). mu lu kas-Ø.ak-ene.a(k).še
d u b e 2 s a 1 0 - a - b e 2 Its tablet that a house had been bought. dub e sa-a-a(k)-be
158 See also Example 16.7. 159 The meaning “because” applies to a genitive construction marked with the terminative case with either b a r or m u as head noun; see Chapter 18.2.5.



CHAPTER 16. NON-FINITE VERBAL FORMS 209
š a 3 g u 3 d i e 2 d u 3 - d a - k a - n a In his heart which cries out (because) of šag gu.Ø di-e(d) e.Ø du-ed.ak-ane.a building the temple.
16.2. The present participle (*ĝar)
The present participle solely consists of the perfective verbal base and carries no additional suffixes. It usually expresses a timeless truth and therefore never refers to a specific event that happened at a certain time. As such, these simple forms often occur in occupations such as d u b s a r , “scribe” (< someone who generally writes tablets). It can also often be found in royal and divine epithets:
Example 16.8
ka lal3-gen7 du10 mu ka-ge du7 kag.Ø lal.gen dug mu.Ø kag.e du
mouth.ABS syrup.EQU to be sweet name mouth.DIR to be fitting
(Your) mouth is as sweet as syrup; (your) name suits the mouth.
The present participle can be used both as verbal adjective and as verbal noun. As a verbal adjective the present participle refers to the participant, who has a role in the verbal action: e.g., š e - g a d e n - l i l 2 - l a 2 d n i n - l i l 2 - e k i a ĝ 2 , “favorite of Enlil whom Ninlil loves.” There must not necessarily be a head noun present: e.g., i r i - t a n u - e 3 , “someone who does not leave the city.”
16.3. The perfective participle (*ĝar-a)
Past participles express a specific action or state at a particular time. They can denote a past action, a past state, but also a present state. As the present participle it can be used both as verbal adjective and as verbal noun. As verbal adjective it is used like any adjective and follows the head noun.
The perfective participle or non-finite form consists of the perfective base followed by the postposition /a/. Usually, the postposition is combined with the final consonant of the preceding base in a syllabogram: e.g., ĝ a r - r a , “placed” (*ĝar-a); š u m 2 - m a , “given” (*šum-a); i l 2 - l a , “lifted” (*il-a). If the preceding base ends in a vowel, the suffix is usually written with the sign A: e.g., d u 3 - a , “built” (*du-a); g u 7 - a , “eaten” (*gu-a). If the base ends in the vowel /a/, the suffix may not be written separately.
The perfective participle is particularly well attested in epithets and appositions. The following three statements are attested in an ED IIIb royal inscription and describe E’anatum of Lagash:
Example 16.9
a2 šum2-ma dnin-ĝir2-su2-ka-ke4 a.Ø šum-a Nin-Ĝirsu.ak.ak.e arm.ABS to give-PERFPART DN.GEN.ERG
(The one) given strength by Ninĝirsu.
ga zi gu7-a dnin-ḫur-saĝ-ka-ke4 ga zid.Ø gu-a Nin-ḫursaĝ.ak.ak.e milk just.ABS to eat-PERFPART DN.GEN.ERG
(The one) fed with rich milk by Ninḫursaĝa.



ki aĝ2 ddumu-zi-abzu-ka-ke4 ki.Ø aĝ-a Dumuzid-abzu.ak.ak.e place.ABS to measure-PERFPART DN.GEN.ERG
(The one) beloved of Dumuzi’abzu.
All statements are genitive constructions: the subject is expressed by the genitive case. An alternative construction was named after the Early Dynastic ruler Mes’anepada (written m e š 3 a n - n e 2 - p a 3 - d a ), whose name is analyzed as *meš an.e pad-a, “the young man, chosen by An.”
16.4. The imperfective participle (*ĝa2-ĝa2-ed)
The imperfective participle is formed with the imperfective base followed by the particle -/ed/. This particle is identical to the suffix -/ed/ in the finite verbal form expressing a future event. The non-finite form also expresses a future event. Together with the enclitic copula it may also denote necessity and obligation (see subsequent chapter).
Example 16.10
d i ĝ i r n a m t a r - r e - d e 3 The god who decrees a fate. diĝir nam.Ø tar-ed.e
ḫ e 2 - ĝ a l 2 n u - t i l - l e - d e 3 Who does not put an end to abundance. ḫeĝal.Ø nu-til-ed.e
The latter example allows for another interpretation, which is preferable in the text this example originates from (Rīm-Sîn G, line 18): “not to put an end to abundance.” For this construction see Chapter 16.6, below.
Examples
-de3
16.5. Expressing necessity or obligation (*ĝa2-ĝa2-ed.Ø-am) Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 698–701
Frequently, the non-finite imperfective form may also be followed by an enclitic copula. Very often such forms end in the sign DAM ( ). This construction has a slightly different meaning and expresses necessity and obligation:
Example 16.11
k i š e b 3 - b e 2 g u 2 - r u - d a m These sealed documents are to be brought back. kišeb-be.Ø gur-ed-Ø-am
n a m - e r i m 2 - b e 2 k u 5 - r u - d a m This oath is to be taken. nam-erim-be.Ø kuř-ed-Ø-am
u d u - b e 2 s u - s u - d a m These sheep are to be repaid. udu-be.Ø susu-ed-Ø-am
Such non-finite statements occur frequently in economic texts.



CHAPTER 16. NON-FINITE VERBAL FORMS 211
16.6. Expressing purpose (*ĝa2-ĝa2-ed.e)
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 135–6; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 100–2;
The non-finite imperfective verbal form can also be used in a clause expressing the goal or the purpose of a verbal action. Here, the non-finite form is followed by the directive case, which has the function of the inanimate dative case marker. In Akkadian, these forms correspond to an infinitive construction preceded by the preposition ana: e.g., ana šakānim, “to place.” The vowel of the imperfective particle -/ed/ often disappears in writing.
Example 16.12
š e g u 4 - r e 6 g u 7 - d e 3 Barley to be eaten by the oxen. še guř.e gu-ed-Ø.e
e 2 d u 3 - d e 3 i r i - n e 2 - š e 3 To build the temple, they gathered in his city. gu2 mu-na-si-si
e.Ø du-ed.e eri-ane.še gu.Ø mu-nn.a-(b.)sisi.e
d a - n u n - n a u 3 d i - d e 3 The Anuna gods stood there in awe. im-ma-su8-su8-ge-eš2 A-nun.a(k) u di-ed.e i-m-b.a-susug.eš
l u 2 - z a ḫ 3 d a b 5 - d a b 5 - d e 3 ĝ e n - n a Who went to capture fugitives. lu-zah.Ø dabdab-ed.e ĝen-a
The ending may not only(!) indicate a directive case. Depending on the context of a clause, it may also be analyzed as ergative:
Example 16.13
d e n - l i l 2 d u 1 1 - g a - n e 2 Enlil, who does not alter his utterance.
šu nu-bala-e-de3
Enlil dug-ane.e šu.Ø nu-bala-ed.e
In this example, the non-finite expression *dug-ane.e šu.Ø nu-bala-ed qualifies Enlil. Without the presence of Enlil in this example a translation “not to alter his utterance” is perfectly correct.
As some of the examples above show, the vowel of the suffix -/ed/ may change to /u/ after verb bases containing /u/ (e.g., š u m 2 - m u - d e 3 , “in order to give”) or rarely to /a/ (e.g., d a b 5 - b a d e 3 , “in order to seize”).
16.7. Non-finite verbal forms as adverbial clauses of time
Literature: EDZARD 2003: 137–41; FOXVOG 2010: 146–48; JAGERSMA 2010: 672–4; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 102–4; SALLABERGER & COLONNA D’ISTRIA 2023: 83–4
The perfective and imperfective non-finite forms (see Chapters 16.3 and 16.4) can also be used to express a temporal clause. The non-finite forms are followed by possessive suffixes, which indicate the subject of the verbal action, and a locative case. Since possessive suffixes are added to the nonfinite forms, the construction was also referred to as pronominal conjugation. In the imperfective set of forms the possessive suffix is preceded by the subordinator, whose presence may be explained by analogy to the perfective forms.



The third person forms end in the directive or locative 3 case /e/. Noteworthy is the ending - n e of the first and second person singular. This ending may represent an old form of the locative, which is partially still visible in the locative 1 dimensional prefix of the finite verbal chain (see Chapter 14.1.7.1).
First and second plural forms are not yet attested. A form such as **ĝar-ra-me-ne or **ĝa2-ĝa2-dazu-ne-ne could be expected. The following table contains attested forms:
Perfective
1st sg. ĝ a r - r a - ĝ u 1 0 - n e *ĝar-a-ĝu-ne “when I was placing” 2nd sg. ĝ a r - r a - z u - n e *ĝar-a-zu-ne “when you were placing” 3rd sg. anim. ĝ a r - r a - n e 2 *ĝar-a-ane-e “when he/she was placing” 3rd sg. inan. ĝ a r - r a - b e 2 *ĝar-a-be-e “when it was placing” 1st pl. – – 2nd pl. – –
3rd pl. ĝ a r - r a - n e - n e *ĝar-a-anene-e “when they were placing”
Imperfective
1st sg. ĝ a 2 - ĝ a 2 - d a - ĝ u 1 0 - n e *ĝaĝa-ed-a-ĝu-ne “when I place” 2nd sg. ĝ a 2 - ĝ a 2 - d a - z u - n e *ĝaĝa-ed-a-zu-ne “when you place” 3rd sg. anim. ĝ a 2 - ĝ a 2 - d a - n e 2 *ĝaĝa-ed-a-ane-e “when he/she places” 3rd sg. inan. ĝ a 2 - ĝ a 2 - d a - b e 2 *ĝaĝa-ed-a-be-e “when it places” 1st pl. – – 2nd pl. – –
3rd pl. ĝ a 2 - ĝ a 2 - d a - n e - n e *ĝaĝa-ed-a-anene-e “when they place”
The directive case in the forms of the third person are not visible in writing. In Sumerian-Akkadian bilingual texts such forms are usually rendered by an infinitive construction such as ina šakāniya, etc. Their meaning is often temporal, “when I place(d),” etc. However, the Akkadian equivalent does not express any aspect or tempus.
Example 16.14
e 2 - g a l a n - n a - k a k u 4 - r a - n e 2 After he entered the heavenly palace, ĝ e š - t u 9 ĝ e š t u m u - u n - ĝ a 2 - ĝ a 2 he was paying attention. egal an.ak.a kur-a-ane.e ĝeštug mu-n.ĝaĝa.e
e n - e ĝ e š g u - z a p a r a 1 0 m a ḫ - a O lord, when you were taking seat on the d u r 2 ĝ a r - r a - z u - n e throne, the august dais. en.e guza parag mah.a dur.Ø ĝar-a-zu-ne
t e r a - r a - d a - ĝ u 1 0 - n e 160 When I strike the cheek. te.Ø rara.ed-a-ĝu-ne
A slight irregularity can be found with the verb ĝ e n , “to go.” While the perfective forms follow the regular pattern (e.g., ĝ e n - n a - z u - n e , “when you were going”), the imperfective forms lack the non-finite marker -/ed/ and the subordinator:
Example 16.15
š u š i n k i - š e 3 d u - n e - n e When they were going to Susa Šušin.še du-anene.e
160 Akkadian version: ina mašādiya.



CHAPTER 16. NON-FINITE VERBAL FORMS 213
Since the directive case is not explicitly written in the third person forms, examples such as the latter may also have an entirely different meaning: “(for) their (men) who go to Susa” (*duanene.Ø/e/ra).
Finally, the form is also used with the adjective dele (dili), “single,” in examples such as d e l e ĝ u 1 0 - n e , “on my own.” Similar to the imperfective base of ĝ e n , the word is not followed by a subordinator.

CHAPTER 17. COMPOUND OR PHRASAL VERBS 217
17. Compound or Phrasal Verbs
Literature: ATTINGER 1993: 179–82; ATTINGER 2019; EDZARD 2003: 142–50; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 226–27; KARAHASHI 2000
The Sumerian lexicon contains only a limited number of verb bases (e.g., š u m 2 , “to give,” s i 3 g , “to put”). Some important activities such as “to see,” cannot be expressed by a verb base alone. Similar to combining signs to form new concepts — the verb g u 7 , “to eat,” for instance, consists
of the signs for mouth ( ) and food/bread ration ( ) —, Sumerian can combine a verb base with a noun to form new semantic meanings. Very often, the noun in question is a body part. The verb for “to see, to look at,” for instance, uses the noun i g i , “eye.” Compound verbs have been debated quite a lot, and there exist different opinions as to their linguistic character. As ATTINGER 2019 points out, texts of the Old Babylonian period show that the nominal part is sometimes incorporated and may appear directly before the base in finite forms: m u - u n - k i - ĝ a r instead of k i m u - u n - ĝ a r , “he firmly founded,” and s i m u - u n - s i - s a 2 - e - n e instead of s i m u - u n s a 2 - e - n e , “they walk straight.” In the latter example, the noun is written twice, once before the verbal phrase and once directly preceding the base s a 2 .
Similar to causative constructions (see Chapter 14.1.7.4), which involves an additional participant, a compound verb adds an additional participant to the intransitive or transitive sentence. Let us compare the following causative statement:
Example 17.1
ensi2-ke4 sipa-ra ninda mu-un-gu7-e ensik.e sipad.ra ninda.Ø mu-n.gu.e
city governor.ERG shepherd.DAT bread.ABS VEN-3sgOO-to eat.3sgA
The city governor gave the shepherd bread to eat.
Whether causative sentence or not, the direct object n i n d a does not change its role. It remains the direct object of the verb g u 7 , “to eat.” The relationship of n i n d a and g u 7 can be compared to compound verbs, which form a close semantic unit. The function of the noun is to modify the meaning of the verb. Similar to Diri-compounds on a graphemic level, the meaning of a compound verb is not always easy to determine. Examples are k i — a ĝ 2 , “to love,” ĝ a l 2 — t a k a 4 , “to open,” and n a — d e 5 - g , “to clear, clarify.” However, the semantics of many compound verbs can be deduced. ATTINGER 2019 warns that applying semantic criteria alone distorts the boundaries between compound verbs and idioms.
17.1. What are compound verbs?
A very important criterion for the existence of compound verbs are so-called verbalizers or auxiliary verbs. The most productive among them are d u 1 1 - g , “to say,” and a 5 - k , “to do, make.” The former is attested in 200 different compounds, the latter in more than 500: e.g., e š - b a r k i ĝ 2 — d u 1 1 - g , “to come to a decision,” n a m - g e 4 - m e - e š 3 — a 5 - k , “to treat collegially.” The “nominal” part often functions like a direct object and as such appears before the verbal chain in the absolutive case. The separate placement of the nominal part probably speaks, according to ZOLYOMI 1996, against the linguistic phenomenon of noun incorporation. In Zolyomi’s definition, compound verbs are constructions involving a verb and a noun, where the noun is not co-referenced



and thus forms a semantic unit with the verb. ATTINGER 1993 and 2019, who argues for noun incorporation, specifies several criteria to recognize compound verbs: 1. Verbal phrases of the type VERB ... d u 1 1 - g / a 5 - k are probably compound verbs. In such constructions, the compound has often a resultative meaning: e.g., s a 2 , “to be equal” > s a 2 d u 1 1 - g , “to reach.” Attinger excludes such cases, where the nominal part is followed by another case marker than the absolutive. 2. A verbal phrase of the type NOUN VERB (e.g., š u t a 3 - g , “to cover, adorn”), which is not used substantively — the lexeme n a - d e 5 - g , for instance, means “advice,” but can also be used as compound n a — d e 5 - g , “to clear, clarify” —, becomes object to the verbalizers d u 1 1 - g or a 5 - k . The nuances between a simple compound form like š u — t a 3 - g and š u t a 3 - g d u 1 1 - g are not always clear. 3. A compound verb may also be recognized, if it is preceded by the proclitic n u - or the elements n a m - and n i ĝ 2 - to form nouns (see Chapter 5.2.2): e.g., n i ĝ 2 - s i - s a 2 , “justice.” 4. The unmarked (absolutive) nominal part is not (or rarely) referred to by a pronominal marker in the verbal phrase. This criterion was criticized by ZOLYOMI 1996. ATTINGER 2019 highlights the distinction between transitive verbs oriented towards the object (e.g., ḫ e 2 - n a - a b - š u m 2 m u , “he may give it to him”) and those verbs that are construed with directive or locative and are oriented towards the place of the process (e.g., g u 3 m u - n e - d e 2 - e , “he addresses them”). 5. ATTINGER 2004 refers to another criterion, namely the presence of interrogative pronouns (see Chapter 7.2). Interrogative pronouns are placed directly before the verb phrase: e.g., i 7 a - b a a m u - u n - b a - a l - e , “who digs the canal?” With many verbs that appear to be compound verbs, the interrogative pronoun appears before the nominal part: e.g., a - b a - a i g i m u - n i i n - d u 8 , “who looks at him?”
In most compound verbs, the nominal part appears to be in the absolutive case. Unfortunately, several of the better attested nouns end in a vowel. These are, for instance, a 2 , “arm,” ĝ i r i 3 , “food,” g u 2 , “neck,” g u 3 , “voice,” i g i , “eye,” n i 2 , “self,” š u , “hand,” and z i , “breath/throat.” The noun e n i m , “word,” shows clearly that it appears in the absolutive case:
Example 17.2
e2 gu7-li dumu ur-dun gudu4-ka e guli dumu Urdun gudu.ak.a house PN son PN priest.GEN.LOC
du11-ge dumu ur-den-lil2-[la2-ke4] Dug.e dumu Ur-Enlil.ak.e PN son PN.GEN.ERG
enim bi2-in-ĝar-ra enim.Ø b.i-n.ĝar.Ø-a word.ABS OO-3sgA.to set.3sgP-NOM
That Duge, son of Ur-Enlil, laid claim on the house of Guli, son of Urdun, the gudug-priest. (ITT 3, 6567 o 2–4)
The example, taken from a legal text of the Ur III period, uses the compound verb e n i m — ĝ a r , “to lay claim (on).” The semantic object (i.e., the noun that is being laid claim on), is marked with the locative (inanimate noun) or dative (animate noun). Similarly, several other nominal parts



CHAPTER 17. COMPOUND OR PHRASAL VERBS 219
clearly show that they appear in the absolutive case: e.g., ĝ a l 2 , “?,”161 n a m , “fate,” š a 3 - g , “heart,” and z a 3 - g , “side.”
The question arises whether the nominal part of a compound verb is co-referenced in the verbal chain. If it appears in the absolutive, one expects a direct object marker there. Of course, it would only clearly appear in imperfective, cohortative, or imperative forms.
Example 17.3
igi zi bar-mu-un-ši-ib igi zid.Ø bar-mu-n.ši-b eye just.ABS to split-VP-IPP.TERM-3sgP
Look at him favorably (Sumer and Ur Lament 456; OB)
This example uses the compound verb i g i — b a r , “to look at.” The semantic object of this compound verb can be either marked with a locative or a terminative. If it is marked with a locative, the compound verb means “to look into something, to check, to examine”:
Example 17.4
en arataki-ke4 im-ma igi i-ni-in-bar en Arata.ak.e im.a igi.Ø i-ni-n.bar.Ø lord GN.GEN.ERG clay.LOC eye.ABS VP-LOC1-3sgA.to open.3sgP
The lord of Arata examined the tablet. (Enmerkar and the Lord of Arata 539; OB)
Much more frequent (see Example 17.3) is the terminative: “to look at something/someone.” Several changes in the compound verb’s morphology happened in the first half of the second millennium BCE, which likely go back to the influence of Akkadian.162
The nominal part of some compound verbs, however, may be marked with the directive case. This is the case with the well-attested compound verb š u — t i , “to receive,” lit. “to bring something near to one’s hand.” The nominal part of this compound verb is marked with the directive, which corresponds to the indirect object in the verbal chain (see Chapter 14.1.3).163 Since the word š u ends in a vowel, the directive is not explicitly marked in writing. The semantic object is in the absolutive (Example 17.5):164
Example 17.5
il2-le nam-ensi2 šu e-ma-ti Il.e nam-ensik.Ø šu.e i-m-b.a-(n.)ti.Ø PN.ERG ensi-ship.ABS hand.DIR VP-VEN-IO3SG-INAN-ERG-to bring close-ABS
Il received the governorship.
161 The word ĝ a l 2 , otherwise only used as verbal basis, appears in the compound verb ĝ a l 2 — t a k a 4 , “to open.” 162 The nominal part may be followed by a possessive suffix referring to the subject. The nominal part may be in the locative case. Instead of the terminative, the person looked at may be marked with a dative. Instead of an adjective, there is a preference of adverbs to indicate the manner of looking (instead of i g i z i rather i g i z i - d e 3 - e š 2 and similar). The gender of the IPP may be different to the gender of the noun being looked at. Finally, the prefix chain may be marked both with dative and terminative; see ZOLYOMI 2007. 163 EDZARD 2003: 145–6 analyzes an absolutive case on š u : “to bring something/someone close to the hand.” 164 The phrasal verbs š u — d u 8 , “to hold” (lit. “to fasten something to the hand”), š u — g i 4 , “to restitute” (lit. “to return to one’s hand”), and š u — u s 2 , “to dispatch” (lit. “to let something follow the hand”), behave similarly.



The animate or inanimate noun, from which someone or something is received, is marked with the terminative case. Literally, the compound verb is understood here as “to let something be brought near to the hand for someone/something” (Example 17.6):
Example 17.6
lugal-a-ne2 sizkur2 ra2-zu-ne2 gu3-de2-a-aš2 lugal-ane.e sizkur arazu-ane.Ø Gudea.še king-POSS.ERG prayer supplication.ABS PN.TERM
en dnin-ĝir2-su-ke4 šu ba-ši-ti
en Nin-Ĝirsu.ak.e šu.e b.a-(n.)ši-(n.)ti.Ø lord DN.ERG hand.DIR IO
His king, lord Ninĝirsu, accepted prayers and supplication from Gudea. (Gudea Cylinder A ii 21–2; Lagaš II)
Texts of the Old Babylonian period show some changes. Example 17.7 marks the semantic object with the locative case. Here, it appears that the nominal part is in the absolutive case:
Example 17.7
dutu er2-na šu la-ba-an-ši-in-ti Utu.e er-ane.a šu.Ø nu-i-b.a-n.ši-n.ti.Ø DN.ERG tear-3sgan.LOC hand.ABS NEG-VP
Utu did not accept his tears.
17.2. Marking the semantic object
While most often the nominal part is in the absolutive case, the semantic object of the compound verb appears in different cases. This can be the locative 2 or the locative 3, but also other dimensional cases. Lexical texts, unless they contain whole sentences or phrases (see, for instance, the legal phrasebook Ana ittišu and related texts), provide only the basic form without any indication of the cases (e.g., i g i — b a r : naplusu, “to look at”). Compound verbs usually carry an idiomatic meaning, although the original literal meaning often helps to remember the type of the semantic object. Sumerian compound verbs are usually not literally represented by Akkadian verbs: e.g., k i — a ĝ 2 corresponds to Akk. râmu, “to love,” not(!) **ašra madādu.165
Etymology Meaning
š u — b a l a “hand” + “to cross over” to change
š u — b a r “hand” + “to open” to release
š u — d a g “hand” + “to demolish” to roam about; to
abandon
š u — d u 7 “hand” + “to be fitting” to complete
š u — d u 8 “hand” + DIR + “to spread” to hold
165 This can, for instance, also be found in bilingual compositions. See, for instance, GELLER 2005.



CHAPTER 17. COMPOUND OR PHRASAL VERBS 221
š u — g i 4 “hand” + “to turn” to return; to repeat
š u — g i d 2 “hand” + “to be/make long” to accept; to examine
š u — m u 2 “hand” + “to grow” to pray
š u — p e š “hand” + “to be thick” to expand
š u — t a 3 - g “hand” + “to touch” to decorate166
š u — t a k a 4 “hand” + “to abandon” to send
š u +DIR— t i “hand” + “to come close” to receive
š u — u r 3 “hand” + “to drag” to erase
š u — z i - g “hand” + “to rise” to raise the hand; to
plunder
Other compound verbs involving the noun š u , “hand,” show different grammatical agreement patterns. The verb š u — u r 3 , “to erase,” for instance, literally means “to rub the hand on something.” Here, the nominal part is marked with the absolutive case. The semantic object is in the locative case and agreed upon by the oblique object in the verbal chain (Example 17.8):
Example 17.8
lu2 mu-sar-ra-ba šu bi2-ib2-ur3-a lu mu-sar-a-be.a šu.Ø b.i-b.ur.e-a man inscription-DEM.LOC hand.ABS OO-ABS.to rub.ERG-SO
The man who erases this inscription.
Another compound verb that marks the semantic object in the verbal chain with the locative 2 is e n i m — ĝ a r , “to raise a claim on something” (see also Chapter 14.1.7.2). In Example 17.9 the semantic object is inanimate, thus the noun is marked with the locative case and the verb with the locative 2 case. Example 17.10 uses an animate noun as semantic object:
Example 17.9
ki-sur-ra lagaški-ka lu2 uri5ki-ma-ke4 enim bi2-ĝar kisura Lagas.ak.a lu Urim.ak.e enim.Ø b.i-(n.)ĝar.Ø
boundary Lagas.GEN.LOC man Ur-GEN.ERG word.ABS OO-ERG.to place.ABS
The man of Ur laid claim on the boundary of Lagas. (RIME 2.13.6.1, 6–8; oAkk royal)
Example 17.10
ur-dba-ba6 dumu ur-sa6-ga-ke4 lu2-nam2-maḫ-ra enim in-ni-ĝa2ĝarar Ur-Baba.a(k) dumu Ur-saga.ak.e Lu-nam-maḫ.ra enim.Ø i-nn.i-(n.)ĝar.Ø
PN son PN.GEN.ERG PN.DAT word.ABS VP-OO-ERG.to place.ABS
166 š u — t a 3 - g can also represent the nominal part of a compound verb š u - t a 3 - g — d u 1 1 - g .



Ur-Baba, son of Ur-saga, laid claim on Lu-nammaḫ. (Fs. Pettinato 183, 3, rev. 8–10; Ur III legal)167
Another frequently attested noun used in compound verbs is i g i , “eye.” The compound verb i g i — b a r , “to look at,” means literally “to direct the eye.” The recipient is marked with the terminative case:
Example 17.11
lu2-e igi mu-un-ši-in-bar lu.e igi.Ø mu-n.ši-n.bar.Ø man.ERG eye.ABS VEN-IPP.TERM-3sgA.to open.3sgP
The man looked at him/her.
Etymology Meaning
i g i — b a r “eye” + “to be separated” to look at
i g i — d u 8 + LOC3 to look at
igi—gub
i g i — ĝ a l 2 “eye” + “to be present” to look at
i g i — ĝ a r “eye” + “to place” to look at
i g i — i l 2 “eye” + “to raise” to look at/towards
i g i — k a r 2 “eye” + “to shine” to examine
i g i — l a 2 “eye” + “to hang” to watch
i g i — s a 6 - g “eye” + “to be good” to look at favorably
i g i — s u ḫ “eye” + “to tear out” to choose
i g i — t u m 3 “eye” + “to bring” to spy
Occasionally, the nominal part of the compound verb may be qualitifed by an adjective (e.g., z i d , “just”) or is followed by an adverb (e.g., z i - d e 3 - e š 2 ). In both cases, translations reflect this adverbially.
Example 17.12
igi zi mu-un-ši-in-bar igi zid.Ø mu-n.ši-n.bar.Ø eye just.ABS VEN-IPP.TERM-3sgA.to open.3sgP
The man looked favorably at him/her.
167 The spelling ĝa2ĝarar with two phonetic complements is not unusual for Ur III documents.



CHAPTER 17. COMPOUND OR PHRASAL VERBS 223
Texts of the Old Babylonian period can use an adverb instead: e.g., i g i z i - d e 3 - e š 2 ...
A compound verb with a similar connotation is i g i — d u 8 , “to look at” (lit. “to open the eye on something/someone”). The semantic object of this verb is marked with the locative 3 or directive:
Example 17.13
en arataki-ke4 gig-e igi bi2-in-du8 en Arata.ak.e gig.e igi.Ø b.i-n.duḫ.Ø
lord GN.GEN.ERG wheat.DIR eye.ABS OO-3sgA.to open.3sgP
The lord of Arata looked at the wheat.
Another body part used for compound verbs is a 2 , “arm, strength.” The compound verb a 2 — a ĝ 2 , “to instruct,” literally means “to measure the arm with something/someone,” and therefore is construed together with a comitative:
Example 17.14
dnin-sikil-a-da a2 mu-da-aĝ2 Ninsikila.da a.Ø mu-(n.)da-(n.)aĝ.Ø DN.COM arm.ABS VEN-IPP.COM-3sgA.to measure.3sgP
He commanded/instructed Ninsikila.
In bilingual contexts it is usually equated with a form of wu’’uru, “to give an order.” The compound verb a 2 — a ĝ 2 , however, can also be understood in a more literal sense within a legal context: e.g., a 2 - b e 2 i 3 - a ĝ 2 - ĝ a 2 equated with idīšu imaddad, “he pays his wage.” Note, however, the following example:
Example 17.15
den-lil2-le a2-be2 mu-e-da-an-aĝ2 Enlil.e a-be.Ø mu-(n.)da-(n.)aĝ.Ø DN.ERG arm-POSS.ABS VEN-IPP.COM-3sgA.to measure.3sgP
Enlil commanded it. (Iddin-Dagan B 11; literary; OB)
The same verbal base a ĝ 2 , “to measure,” is used with the noun k i , (probably) “place,” with the idiomatic meaning “to love.” The semantic object is marked with the dative:
Example 17.16
iri-inim-ge-na-ra ki mu-na-aĝ2 Iri-inim-gen.a(k).ra ki.Ø mu-nn.a-(n.)aĝ.Ø PN.DAT place.ABS VEN-IO-3sgA.to measure.3sgP
He loved Iri’inimgena(k).
One can observe some irregularities in the use of compound verbs, particularly in later texts of the second and first millennia BCE. The nominal part can sometimes appear together with the base in the verbal chain.
Example 17.17
ša4-be2 ur-saĝ-ur-saĝ-e-ne si mu-un-si-sa2-e-ne



šag-be.e ursaĝ-ursaĝ-ene.e si.Ø mu-n.si:sa.ene heart-DEM.DIR hero.REDUPL-PLUR.ERG horn.ABS VEN-OO.horn-to be equal.3sgA
The heroes go straight into its (i.e., the temple’s) interior.
Example 17.18
e2 ĝe6ĝepar en-na mu-un-ki-ĝar e gepar en.a(k).Ø mu-n.ki:ĝar.Ø
house cloister en.GEN.ABS VEN-3sgA.place(.DIR).to place.3sgP
He founded the temple, the residence of the en-priest.
The compound verb s i — s a 2 , “to put in order (also “be/make straight, to go straight/directly; to prepare properly”),” probably derives from lining up the horns of a row of animals. Its literal meaning is “to make the horns equal.” The semantic object is usually marked with the directive:
Example 17.19
lu2 be6-lu5-da diĝir-re-ne-ke4 si bi2-sa2-sa2-a lu beluda diĝir-ene.ak.e si.Ø b.i-n.sasa.Ø-a man ritual deity-PLUR.GEN.DIR horn.ABS OO-3sgA.to make equal.REDUPL.3sgP-NOM
The person, who put in order the rituals of the gods.
17.3. Auxiliary verbs and verbalizers
Another type of compound verb concerns those formed with the verbal base z a . This verbal base combines with nominal parts that represent onomatopoeias (see BLACK 2003). Here, the ideophone functions like a direct object and is thus marked with an absolutive. Such verbs are only attested in literary contexts. The ideophone is formed of a repeated consonantal pattern with alternating vowels /u/ and /a/: – dub-dab—za, “to clatter” – bud-bad—za, “to clatter” (said of hailstones) – pud-pad—za, “to thud” – suḫ-saḫ—za, “to thud” – zig-zag—za, “to rumble” – dum-dam—za, “to growl” – ḫun-ḫa—za, “to bark” – gum-gam—za, “to snarl” – kun-kan—za, “to splash”
Example 17.20
im-dug-ge na4šu-gen7 ĝurgu-ba dub-da-ab ḫa-ma-ab-[za] imdug.e šu.gen ĝurgu-be.a dubdab.Ø ḫa-(i)-m-ba-b.za.Ø sling shot.ERG stone.EQU shoulder-POSS.LOC ONOM.ABS MOD-VP-VEN-MED-3sgA.AUX.3sgP
Clay sling shots clatter on their backs like hammerstones. (Šulgi D 185–6; OB)
Ideophones like these are also attested with other verbs such as d u 1 1 - g , “to speak/make,” a 5 - k , “to make,” and š a 4 . All of these verbs are used as auxiliary verbs. The forms with d u 1 1 - g connect with nouns (e.g., š e - e r - k a - a n — d u 1 1 - g , “to decorate” < “to say/make an ornament”) or



CHAPTER 17. COMPOUND OR PHRASAL VERBS 225
compound verbs (e.g., š u - t a 3 — d u 1 1 - g , “to decorate” < “to say/make the hand touch”).168 The nuance between a compound verb š u - t a 3 - g and š u - t a 3 - g — d u 1 1 - g , remain elusive. Both forms can occur in the same text.
Example 17.21
ni2 me-lim4 ḫuš-a saĝ-ki-na še-er-ka-an ba-ni-in-du11 ni melim ḫuš.a saĝki-ane.a
She decorated her forehead with terror and fearsome radiance. ni melem ḫuš.a saĝki-ane.a šerkan.Ø ba-ni-n.dug.Ø
The verbalizer a5-k, “to do, to make,” is even attested in more than 500 combinations.169
17.4. Artificial compound verbs
Occasionally, we also find artificial inventions of compound verbs. The verb š u — ḫ u - u z , for instance, means something like “to burn.”170 It appears to be construed from the noun š u , “hand,” and a verbal base ḫ u - u z . Indeed, the form is a loan from Akkadian šūḫuzu, “to make hold; to kindle a fire.” Similar to any other compound verb, it also appears in finite forms: e.g., š u b i 2 i n - ḫ u - ḫ u - u z . Another example is s a ĝ — r i g 7 , “to bestow, give as gift,” which is a loan from Akkadian šarāku. The nominal part here is often marked with the terminative:
Example 17.22
e2 lugal-na-ke4 saĝ-še3 im-mi-rig7 e lugal-ane.ak.e saĝ.še i-m-b.i-(n.)rig.Ø house king-POSS.GEN.DIR head.TERM VP-VEN-OO-3sgA.to donate.3sgP
He gave (the boat) as a gift to the temple of his master. (Gudea Statue D iii,11–2; Lagaš II)
17.5. Unclear etymology
Similar to some Diri compounds, the etymology of compound verbs is not always easy to discern from the verb’s constituents. The compound verb ĝ a l 2 — t a k a 4 means “to open.” Phonetic spellings such as ĝ a 2 - l a confirm that the “nominal” part needs to be read ĝ a l 2 .
Etymology
ĝ a l 2 — t a k a 4 — + “to leave behind” petû to open
ĝ a 2 - l a — d a g — + “to demolish” naparkû to cease, to be idle
m u š 3 - - t u m 2 “face” + “to bring” naparkû to stop working
n a — d e 5 - g — + “to collect”
ašāru; elēlu; ḫasāsu
to give instructions; to purify, to cleanse; to perish
168 See ATTINGER 1993; ATTINGER 2021: 284–301. 169 See ATTINGER 2005; ATTINGER 2021: 144–59. 170 The late lexical compendium Antaĝal equates it with Akk. šummû.



n e — s u - u b “brazier” + “to rub” našāqu to kiss
s a — g i 4 “sinew” + “to turn” to prepare
u 2 - g u — d e 2 — + “to pour” ḫalāqu to lose
u 3 — k u ( 4 ) “sleep” + “to place” ṣalālu to sleep



CHAPTER 18. SUBORDINATION 227
18. Subordination
Literature: JAGERSMA 2010: 583–626; ZÓLYOMI 2017: 94–100;
18.1. Sentences and Clauses
Up to this point, the majority of examples concerned simple sentences. A simple sentence contains one clause, which may be a verbal statement: e.g., g a m i - r i - i n - g u 7 , “she fed you milk” (*ga.Ø mu-ri-n.gu.Ø). A sentence containing a conjugated verb is considered a verbal sentence. There are also several types of non-verbal sentences. The most common is a nominal sentence such as the following copular clause: a m a - ĝ u 1 0 - m e , “you are my mother” (*ama-ĝu-men). Two other types are the adjectival sentence and the adverbial sentence.
Complex sentences, on the other hand, contain two or more clauses. The individual clauses may have the same syntactic status and are thus coordinated, as the following example demonstrates:
Example 18.1
u 4 b a d 3 ĝ a r 7 - d u 2 171 mu-ri-iq ti-id-nim m u - d u 3 - a ud bad Gardu.a(k) Murīq Tidnim.Ø mu-(n.)du.Ø-a.a
day wall Amorite.GEN PN.ABS VEN-3sgA.to build.3sgP-NOM.LOC
When he built the Amorite wall called The One, Which Keeps the Tidnum At Bay
u3 n e 3 ĝ a r 7 - d u 2 m a - d a - n e 2 - e b i 2 - i n - g e 4 - a u ne Gardu.a(k) mada-ane.e b.i-n.ge.Ø-a.a CON troops Amorite.GEN land-POSS.DIR OO-3sgA.to return.3sgP-NOM.LOC
and returned the Amorite troops (back) to their lands.
In this example from a royal inscription (see Text ###), both clauses – (1) b a d 3 ĝ a r 7 - d u 2 muri-iq ti-id-ni-im m u - d u 3 and (2) n e 3 ĝ a r 7 - d u 2 m a - d a - n e 2 - e b i 2 - i n - g e 4 – share the same syntactic function. In this case, both clauses are linked by the conjunction u3, which is not obligatory. Both clauses are dependend on the head noun u 4 - d , “day,” which is used for temporal clauses and can be combined with LOC (“on the day”), ABL (“since the day”), or TERM (“to the day”). In the example above, the LOC is added to each of the subordinated clauses.
Clauses may also be coordinated by using the verbal prefix -/nga/-, even though this is attested only in rare instances:172
Example 18.2
sipa zi gu3-de2-a gal mu-zu gal i3-ga-tum2-mu sipad zid Gu-de-a.e gal.Ø mu-(n.)zu.Ø gal.Ø i-nga-(b.)tum.e
shepherd just PN.ERG great.ABS VEN-3sgA.to know.3sgP great.ABS VP-COO-(3sgP).to carry.3sgA
The just shepherd Gudea knows great things and can realize great things as well. (Gudea Cylinder A vii,9–10; Lagaš II)
171 The reading ĝ a r 7 - d u 2 has been recently suggested as a new reading for m a r - t u , “Amorite.” 172 See Chapter 12.1.3.



Apart from coordinating clauses, in a complex sentence, one clause may also be subordinate to the other, main, clause. We have seen this already in the discussion of the modal prefix ḫ a - , which is sometimes used in a conditional sense (see Chapter 15.1.4):
Example 18.3
a-ra2 ḫe2-e-šid niĝ2-ka9-še3 i3-dugud ara.Ø ḫa-(i)-e.šid.Ø niĝkas.še i-dugud.Ø multiplication.ABS MOD-VP-2sgA.to count.3sgP accounting.TERM VP-to be heavy.3sgS
(Even) if you can recite the multiplication table,173 the accounting is difficult (for you). (Dialogue 2, 34; OB)
This chapter deals with subordinate clauses, which have a wide range of functions in Sumerian. Most subordinate clauses are nominalized, but there are several conjunctions that do not require nominalized forms (e.g., t u k u m - b e 2 , “if”). Also, we have seen, that some prefixes can convey a subordinate meaning. Forms with the prefix of anteriority, for instance, are subordinate to the verb that follows (see Chapter 15.1.2).
Example 18.4
dnin-ĝir2-su2-ke4 sa-šuš gal-ne2 u3-ni-šuš Nin-Ĝirsu.ak.e sašuš gal-ane.Ø u-nn.i-(n.)šuš.Ø DN.ERG battle-net great-POSS.ABS ANT-LOC2-3sgA.to throw.3sgP
šu maḫ ĝiri3 maḫ-ne2 an-ta ḫe2-ĝa2-ĝa2 šu maḫ ĝiri maḫ-ane.Ø an.ta ḫa-(i)-(n.)ĝaĝa.e hand august foot august-POSS.ABS heaven.ABL MOD-(VP)-OOSYN.to place.3sgA
May Ninĝirsu, after having thrown his great battle-net on him, place his august hand and his august feet from above on him. (Ent. 28–29 vi,33–6; Lagaš; ED IIIb)
According to their syntactic function, nominalized clauses can be divided into (1) content clauses (see Chapter 18.2) and (2) relative clauses (see Chapters 18.3 and 18.4). Content clauses behave like nouns and are therefore independent. These can be followed by case markers etc. Relative clauses behave like adjectives, which modify a participant (i.e., a head noun) that often appears, but is optional. In both cases the verb is followed by a nominalizing suffix /a/. Nominalized clauses can appear in any syntactic function within a sentence.
The nominalizing suffix -/a/ (or -/’a/; glossed NOM) follows the finite verbal form and always appears after the pronominal suffix. Since attaching the suffix creates a noun phrase, it can be followed by any case postposition, the enclitic copula, or possessive suffixes.
18.2. Content clauses
Nominalized clauses containing a finite verbal form can be divided into complement and adverbial clauses. The former concerns clauses, which are subject or object to verbs such as d u 1 1 ( g ) , “to say,” g e ( n ) , “to make firm, to prove,” and z u , “to know, to learn.” A complementary clause that is subordinated is here not obligatory as the following examples show:
Example 18.5
tukum-be2 ad-da-ne2 u3 ama-ne2 tukum-be adda-ane u ama-ane.(ra)
173 Literally “Should you ...”



CHAPTER 18. SUBORDINATION 229
Dkkk
nu ad-da-ĝu10 nu ama-ĝu10 ba-an-du11 nu adda-ĝu.Ø ama-ĝu.Ø ba-n.dug.Ø Dkkk
If (the adopted son) says to his father and his mother, “You are not my father and you are not my mother.” ()
ḫ a - m u - r a - a n - t u m 2 - m u i n - n a - a n - d u 1 1 He told him: “She should bring him to you.” ḫa-mu-ra-n.tum.e i-nn.a-n.dug.Ø
i 3 - z a ḫ 3 i n - n a - a n - d u 1 1 “She escaped,” he told him. i-zaḫ.Ø i-nn.a-n.dug.Ø
In all these examples, the content clause (direct speech) is not subordinated to the form of the verb d u 1 1 ( g ) . However, there is ample evidence that the complimentary clause can be subordinated:
Example 18.6
a m a - a r - g e 4 - ĝ u 1 0 i n - ĝ a r - r a b i 2 - i n - d u 1 1 He said that he would establish my freedom. ama.ra-ge-ĝu.Ø i-n.ĝar.Ø-a b.i-n.dug.Ø
Adverbial clauses show much more variety. The following subchapters discuss adverbial clauses followed by different dimensional cases.
18.2.2. Adverbial clauses with the locative
Adverbial clauses followed by LOC can express a temporal meaning “when.” Here, LOC follows the nominalized adverbial clause. The case marker may or may not be explicitly written:
Example 18.7
ensi2 e2-mi2-še3 e-ĝen-na-a e2 muḫaldim-ma ba-sa6 ensik emi.še i-ĝen.Ø-a.a e muhaldim.a ba-(ni-)sag.Ø
city ruler.ABS women’s quarter.TERM VP-to go.3sgS-NOM.LOC house cook.LOC MED-LOC1-to be good.3sgS
When the ruler came to the women’s quarters, (the offering) was prepared in the kitchen.
This type of construction appears in earlier texts of the late Early Dynastic period, particularly in economic records. In royal inscriptions and later towards the end of the third millennium BCE nouns expressing time such as u 4 ( d ) , “day,” i t u ( d ) , “month,” or m u , “year,” are used in initial position. The subordinated expressions are appositions to the respective head nouns.
Example 18.8
u 4 n i b r u k i - t a i 3 - i m - e - r e - e š 2 - š a - a On the day, they came from Nippur. ud Nibru.ta i-m(u)-er.eš-a.a
In such constructions the subordinated clauses never contain an element that co-refers to the head noun. Hence, they are not considered relative clauses. Constructions with the head noun u 4 ( d ) are the most common. The finite verbal form in the subordinated phrase can be perfective (for past events) or imperfective (for future events). Note that there is also a conditional clause introduced with u 4 - d a , “if.”



The head noun m u , “year,” is rarely used in such constructions.174 It should not be mistaken by the head noun m u that introduces reason clauses. LOC is also used in reason clauses (see Chapter 18.2.5).
A slightly different type of temporal clause uses LOC after a headless genitive construction with the meaning “as soon as” (JAGERSMA 2010: 625), but also “when,” “after,” and “while,” depending on the context:
Example 18.9
bar še-ba-ka lu2 ḫe2-ši-ge4-ge4-a-ka bar še-be.ak.a lu.Ø ḫa-(i)-(n.)ši-(n.)gege.e-a.ak.a outside barley.POSS.GEN.LOC man.ABS MOD-VP-IPP.TERM-3sgP.to return.3sgA-NOM.GEN.LOC
As soon as he sent him a messenger concerning that barley.
18.2.3. Adverbial clauses with the ablative
Instead of LOC a content clause can also be marked by ABL. Here, it expresses a temporal meaning “since” or “after.”
Example 18.10
G N k i n a m - l u g a l š u b a - a b - t i - a - t a After GN had received kingship. GN.e nam-lugal.Ø šu.e ba-b.ti.Ø-a.ta
The subordinated clause does not necessarily need to contain a finite verbal form as can be shown in the subsequent parallel constructions:
Example 18.11
i t u - t a u 4 8 b a - r a - z a l - l a - t a From when out of the month 8 days had passed. itud.ta ud 8.Ø ba-ta-zal.Ø-a.ta
i t u - t a u 4 8 z a l - l a - t a From when out of the month 8 days had passed. itud.ta ud 8.Ø zal.Ø-a.ta
Similar to the locative use in such temporal clauses, we find also constructions with head nouns, here, in particular u 4 - d , “day”:
Example 18.12
u 4 e 2 - g a l - e b a - a b - t u m 2 - m a - t a Since the palace took him away, ... ud egal.e ba-b.tum.Ø-a.ta
Another head noun found in this context and expressing a temporal meaning is e g i r , “back” (
). Literally, such a construction means “from the back of.” The noun e g i r is used as the nomen regens of a genitive construction, both with finite verbal form in the subordinate clause as well as non-finite form:
Example 18.13
e g i r P N b a - u š 2 - š a 4 - t a After PN had died, ...
174 In year names, mu is not used as the head noun for a subordinated clause: e.g., m u m a 2 d e n - k i b a - a b - d u 8 , “Year: Enki’s boat was caulked.”



CHAPTER 18. SUBORDINATION 231
egir PN.Ø ba-uš.Ø-a.a(k).ta
e g i r u d u u r 4 - r a - t a After the sheep had been shorn. egir udu.Ø ur.Ø-a.a(k).ta
Alternatively, Sumerian can use the prefix of anteriority (see Chapter 15.1.2).
18.2.4. Adverbial clauses with the terminative
Last but not least, content clauses followed by the terminative case can express the meaning “until.” The subordinated clauses contain a negative verbal form and thus need to be translated as “before,” similar to Akkadian constructions with adi lā, “until not, before.” Such constructions may also be preceded by u 4 - d .
While in these constructions the subordinated verb is negated, Sumerian offers another construction to express the meaning “until.” Here, we find the subjunction en-na corresonding to Akkadian adi. It may derive from the interrogative pronoun e n - š e 3 or e n 3 - š e 3 , “until when” (also e n 3 t u k u m - š e 3 ; Akk. adi mati). There are three different constructions that use en-na:
Perfective Temporal clause Main clause e n - n a perfective perfective e n - n a imperfective modal e n - n a perfective + terminative modal
Example 18.14
e n - n a e n i m - b e 2 i 3 - t i l P N i 3 - d a b 5 Until this matter is settled, PN has taken it. enna inim-be.Ø i-til.Ø PN.e i-(n.)dab.Ø
e n - n a e n s i 2 - r a i 3 - n a - a b - b e 2 Until he speaks to the governor, enna ensik.ra i-nn.a-b.e.e
k i š e b 3 - b a - n e 2 ḫ e 2 - m a - a n - t u m u 3 he should bring me his sealed document. kišeb-ane.Ø ḫa-ma-n.tumu.Ø
e n s i 2 e n - n a i 3 - ĝ e n - n a - a š Until the governor has arrived, ensik.Ø enna i-ĝen.Ø-a.še
m i n - a - b e 2 e n - n u n - g a ḫ e 2 - t i both of them are to stay in jail. min-a-be.Ø ennuĝ.a ha-(i)-til.Ø
18.2.5. Reason clauses
Sumerian has five ways to express reasons, but these are not attested at the same time. In Old Sumerian texts as well as in Sumerian texts of the Old Akkadian period we find a construction with the head noun b a r , “outside.” The full construction is *b a r noun-a k - a , “in the outside of noun,” whereas noun can either be a noun or a nominalized clause:
Example 18.15
b a r - ĝ u 1 0 - a “on my behalf”175 bar-ĝu.a
b a r e g 2 - b a - k a “because of those ditches” bar eg-be.ak.a
175 Compare Akkadian aššumīya, “on my behalf.”



b a r š e - b e 2 n u - d a - s u 3 - s u 3 - d a - k a “because he was unable to repay that barley” bar še-be.Ø nu-(n.)da-susu-ed.Ø-a.ak.a
By the end of the third millennium BC the noun b a r is replaced by the noun m u , “name,” and the terminative case: *m u noun-a k - š e . This expression can be compared to Akkadian aššum, “because,” deriving from ana and šum, “for the name of”:
Example 18.16
m u š e - n a - š e 3 “because of his barley” mu še-ane.a(k).še
m u n u - d a - s u - s u - d a - š e 3 “because it cannot be repaid by him” mu nu-(n.)da-susu-ed.e-a.a(k).še
Very rare in earlier Sumerian is the cluster of morphemes /akanam/ to express a reason: e.g., ĝ a 2 k a - n a m , “because of me.” Its younger equivalent is /akeš/: e.g., ĝ a 2 - k e 4 - e š , “because of me” (*ĝa-ak-eš). Both m u and this latter ending can also be combined to express a reason: m u l u 2 I M - k a b a - a n - k u - r e - e š - š a - k e 4 - e š , “because they were turned into liars” (ba-n.kur.eša.ak.eš). Occasionally, the comitative case can also express reason, but this is only rarely attested:
Example 18.17
k i š e b 3 u r - m e s - e i 3 - r e 6 - a - d a Because Urmes brought the sealed document, ... kišeb Urmes.e i-(n.)re.Ø-a.da
e n s i 2 - r a d i n a n a - k e 4 k i a n - n a - a ĝ 2 - ĝ a 2 - d a Because Inana loves the governor, ... ensik.ra Inana.k.e ki.Ø a-nn.a-(n.)aĝ.Ø-a.da
18.2.6. Conditional clauses
Besides of the use of conjunctions such as u 4 - d a or t u k u m - b e 2 , the prefix /u/, used as prefix of anteriority (see Chapter 15.1.2) or the modal prefix /ḫa/ (see Chapter 15.1.4) can carry conditional meaning.
The conjunction u 4 - d a , “if,” is only attested in texts from Lagaš. Its etymology is probably *ud.a, “in the time (that).” The verbal form in the conditional clause is imperfective; the verbal form in the main clause is either imperfective or modal.
Example 18.18
u 4 - d a n u - š e 3 - s a 1 0 - s a 1 0 If he does not buy it from him, uda nu-(i)-(n.)ši-(b.)sasa.e
u g u l a l i b i š - b e 2 n a - n a - t a 3 - g e the overseer should not let (his) anger about this touch him! ugula.e libiš.be.Ø na-nn.a-(b.)tag.e
The conjunction does not necessarily be at initial position. The use of the perfective in the conditional clause following u 4 - d a is rarely attested.
With the texts dating to the Lagash II-period, conditional clauses can be introduced by the conjunction t u k u m - b e 2 . The first part of this conjunction appears in different spellings. The
most common spelling is ŠU.NIG2.TUR.LA2 ( ), but shorter spellings such as ŠU.NIG2.TUR



CHAPTER 18. SUBORDINATION 233
( ) or even ŠU.TUR ( ) are used as well.176 The verb used in the condition (protasis) is perfective; the apodosis uses the imperfective:
Example 18.19
tukum-be2 lu2-u3 kiri6 lu2-ka ĝeš in-sag3 tukum-be lu.e kiri lu.ak.a ĝeš.Ø i-n.sag.Ø
COND. man.ERG orchard man.GEN.LOC tree.ABS VP-3sgA.to cut.3sgP
1/3 ma-na ku3-babbar i3-la2-e 1/3 mana kug-babbar.Ø i-(b.)la.e
NUM mina silver.ABS VP-3sgP.to weigh out.3sgA
If a man cuts down a tree in another man’s orchard, he shall pay 1/3 mina of silver.
Example 18.20
tukum-be2 dumu-<munus> lu2 ba-an-sag3 tukum-be dumu-munus lu.(ak) ba-n.sag.Ø
niĝ2-ša3-ga-ne2 a im-šub-šub niĝ-šag-ane.e a.Ø i-m-(b.)šubšub.Ø
1/3 ma-na ku3-babbar i3-la2-e 1/3 mana kug-babbar.Ø i-(b.)la.e
NUM mina silver.ABS VP-3sgP.to weigh out.3sgA
If (a man) beats the child of a man, (and) caused her to have a miscarriage, he shall pay 1/3 mina of silver.
A third way of expressing conditional clauses are two perfective finite forms, the first positive, the second negative, of the same verb. This type of construction may also be introduced by t u k u m be2:
Example 18.21
b a - a n - š u m 2 l a - b a - a n - š u m 2 t a b - d a That if he gives it, (all is well), but if he does not give it, it is to be doubled. ba-n.šum.Ø nu-ba-n.šum.Ø tab.ed.a(k)
18.2.7. Comparative clauses
A subordinated clause can end in the equative case - g e n 7 . The basic meaning of the equative is “like, as.” Hence, it can be used to form comparative clauses:
Example 18.22
a b 2 a m a r - b e 2 - š e 3 i g i ĝ a l 2 - l a - g e n 7 Like a cow keeping an eye on its calf. ab amar-be.še igi.Ø ĝal-a.gen
n i ĝ 2 e 2 - n i n n u ( ... ) m u - n a - d u 3 - a - g e n 7 Just as he had built for him the Eninnu. niĝ Eninnu.Ø (...) mu-nn.a-(n.)du.Ø-a.gen
176 For the short spelling ŠU.TUR see, for instance, Lugalzagesi 1 iii,13.



Apart from the comparative sense, EQU on a subordinated clause may also express a temporal meaning deriving from literal “it is like the day when ...”:
Example 18.23
u 4 a l a n d s u l - g e - e i n - g u b - b a - g e n 7 - a m 3 When Sulge set up a statue, ud alan.Ø Sulge.e i-n.gub.Ø-a.gen-am
t u m u 3 - d a m (the offerings) are to be brought in. tumu.ed.Ø-am
In this example sentence from an Ur III account the equative expresses a temporal sense, which literally can be translated: “It is like the day when ...” A third meaning is causative:
Example 18.24
m u n u s k u r - š e 3 i - i m - ĝ e n - n e - e n - n a - g e n 7 Woman, since you came to the mountain. munus.Ø kur.še i-m-ĝen.en-a.gen
18.3. Relative clauses
A relative clause is dependent on a head noun, which is referred to by one of the participants in the subordinated clause. The relative clause may contain a non-finite verb or a finite verb:
Example 18.25
gur4-gur4 i3 itud-da dnin-ĝir2-su2-ke4 ab-ta-gu7-a gurgur i itud.a(k).Ø Nin-Ĝirsu.ak.e a-b.ta-(b.)gu.(e)-a.Ø vessel oil month.GEN.ABS DN.ERG VP-IPP.ABL-3sgP.to eat.3sgA-NOM.ABS
mu-na-dim2 mu-nn.a-(n.)dim.Ø VEN-DAT-3sgA.to fashion.3sgP
He fashioned for him a gurgur-vessel, from which Ninĝirsu consumes monthly oil. (Ent. 34 ###)
In this example from the famous silver vase of Enmetena the type of vessel, a gurgur-vessel, is qualified by a relative clause: i 3 i t u d - d a d n i n - ĝ i r 2 - s u 2 - k e 4 a b - t a - g u 7 - a .177 The relative clause functions like an adjective following the noun. The whole noun phrase is direct object to the verb in the main clause and thus is marked with the ABS.
Compare the following two examples, each containing a subordinated clause:
Example 18.26
l u 2 e 2 i n - d u 3 - a - r a They gave it to the man, lu e.Ø i-n.du.Ø-a.ra
m u - n a - a n - š u m 2 - m u - u š who has built the house. mu-nn.a-n.šum.eš
l u 2 e 2 d u 3 - a - r a He decided a good fate for the man, lu e.Ø du-a-a(k).ra
n a m d u 1 0 m u - n i - t a r who built the house. nam dug.Ø mu-nn.i-(n.)tar.Ø
177 See Text ###.



CHAPTER 18. SUBORDINATION 235
Example 18.27
lu2 e2 in-du3-a-ra mu-na-an-šum2-mu-uš lu e.Ø i-n.du.Ø-a.ra mu-nn.a-n.šum.eš man house.ABS VP-3sgA.to build.3sgP-NOM.DAT VEN-DAT-3plA.to give.3plA
They gave it to the man, who has built the house. (###)
Example 18.28
lu2 e2 du3-a-ra nam du10 mu-ni-tar lu e.Ø du-a.a(k).ra nam dug.Ø mu-nn.i-(n.)tar.Ø man house.ABS to build.PFTPART.GEN.DAT fate good.ABS VEN-OO-3plA.to decide.3plP
He decided a good fate for the man, who built the house. (###)
The first example contains a finite relative clause. The second example uses a non-finite form. From the spelling with the dative postposition alone, it is not clear whether the participle stands on its own or is followed by a GEN, (lit.) “the man of temple-building.” The latter, however, is confirmed by spellings such as l u 2 e 2 d u 3 - a - k e 4 (Gudea Statue G vi,18).
Compare further the following two statements, both occurring in the Gudea Cylinder inscription:
Example 18.29
e n i m d n a n š e - e m u - n a - d u 1 1 - g a - a š As for the word that Nanše has spoken to him, enim Nanše.e mu-nn.a-(n.)dug.Ø-a.še
s a ĝ s i g b a - š i - ĝ a r he lowered (his) head. saĝ sig.Ø ba-(b.)ši-(n.)ĝar.Ø
Example 18.30
e n i m d u 1 1 - g a d n i n - ĝ i r 2 - s u - k a - š e 3 As for the word spoken by Ninĝirsu enim dug-a Nin-Ĝirsu.ak.a(k).še
s a ĝ s i g b a - š i - ĝ a r he lowered (his) head. saĝ sig.Ø ba-(b.)ši-(n.)ĝar.Ø
Both sentences occur in the cylinder inscription of Gudea. In Example 18.29, the head noun e n i m is modified by the following subordinate clause d n a n š e - e m u - n a - d u 1 1 - g a . The subordinate clause functions like an adjective. Hence, the TERM can only occur after the subordinate clause. Within the relative clause, Nanše is marked by the ergative, since the goddess is the agent of the verbal action expressed by the clause. Since enim is the head noun, it serves as the virtual patient of a transitive relative clause: d n a n š e - e e n i m m u - n a - d u 1 1 , “Nanše spoke a word to him.”
Example 18.30, in its meaning rather similar to the first, uses non-finite d u 1 1 - g a instead (see Chapter 16). Corresponding to Nanše in the first example, the acting participant in the subordinate clause is here in the genitive: “word spoken by Ninĝirsu(k).” This construction, very common in Sumerian, has virtually the same meaning as the subordinated finite clause, but is less versatile. The subordinated finite clause allows to add adverbial cases as, for instance, the dative in the first example.
18.4. Headless relative clauses
Often the nominalized clause lacks a head noun. Here, the nominalized clause itself represents a noun phrase.



Example 18.31
e 2 - ĝ u 1 0 m a - d u 3 - n a You, who will build my temple for me. e-ĝu.Ø ma-(b.)du.en-a
m u - e - š i - i n - g e 4 - n a m It is him, who sent me to you. mu-e.ši-n.ge.en-a-am
The first example relative clause cannot be translated “my house, which you will build for me,” since the possessive can only occur after the relative clause: e 2 m a - d u 3 - n a - ĝ u 1 0 (e ma(b.)du.en-a.Ø).
Example 18.32
i 3 - z a - ḫ a i n - n a - a n - d u 1 1 He said to him that she had run off. i-zaḫ.Ø-a.Ø i-nn.a-n.dug.Ø
In the previous example, the nominalized clause i 3 - z a - ḫ a , “that (s)he had run off,” forms the direct object of the verb in the main clause. The nominalized clause could have been headed by a noun such as g e m e 2 .

Texts 15 (Gudea Statue B)
Gudea Statue B, also known as “Architecte au plan” (Louvre Museum, AO 2), is a smaller-than life-size seated statue of the city governor of Lagash, which dates to c. 2100 BCE or slightly earlier. The statue was found by French archaeologists (Ernest de Sarzec) in the remains of a Parthian palace built by a local ruler with the name Adad-nādin-ahhē over what remained of the Eninnu temple in Girsu (modern Tellō).190 This ruler intentionally collected artifacts and arranged them in the palace’s courtyard and at different parts of the building. He also incorporated earlier architecture into his own building and used mud-bricks, whose dimensions correspond to third millennium customs, which he stamped with his own brick stamp.
Statue B bears the longest among Gudea’s statue inscriptions and is only surpassed by the cylinder inscriptions. The first part of the text (referred to as column i) is a self-contained text, which is carved into the stone in smaller characters. The main part of the text starts in column ii.
Text passages to be read:
Col. “i”
Col. ii,1–iii,5 For Ninĝirsu, the strong warrior of Enlil, Gudea, the one with an eternal name, the city governor of Lagaš, the shepherd called into the heart by Ninĝirsu, looked upon faithfully by Nanše, given strength by Nindara, the man, placed under the order by Baba, born child of Ĝatumdu, bestowed upon with authority and an august sceptre by Igalim, provided vastly with life force by Sulšaga(na), made appear as just leader in the assembly by Ninĝešzida, his god.
Col. iii,6–v,11
Col. v,12–vi,28 For Ninĝirsu, his lord, he made appear the eternal thing: He built for him his Eninnu – White Anzu-bird, and restored it. In its midst he built his beloved high chapel with fragrant cedar wood. When he built the temple of Ninĝirsu, Ninĝirsu, his beloved king, opened the roads for him from the upper sea to the lower sea. From Amanum, the mountain range of cedars, he made into rafts, cedar wood, 60 kuš long, cedar wood, 50 kuš long, and boxwood, 25 kuš long, and brought them down. He set up the šarur-weapon, the flood of the battle, for him. He set up the šargaz-weapon, the copper peg with seven spikes, for him. He set up his copper peg for him; He set up his copper peg ... for him. He fashioned big doors from cedar wood. He decorated them with shining blossoms and placed them in the Eninnu for him. He installed (cedar beams) as roof beams on his august temple, a place where cooling water is being poured.
Col. vi,29–vi,37

Offerings for Gudea’s Statue
Column “i” 1 e2 dnin-ĝir2-su2 e Nin-Ĝirsu.a(k) 2 lugal-na-ta lugal-ane.a(k).ta 3 alan gu3-de2-a alan Gu-de-a 4 ensi2 ensik 5 lagaški Lagaš(.ak) 6 lu2 e2-ninnu lu E-ninnu.Ø 7 in-du3-a-ke4 i-n.du.Ø-a.ak.e 8 1 sila3 kaš 1 sila kaš.Ø 9 1 sila3 inda3 1 sila ninda.Ø 10 1⁄2 sila3 zi3-dub-dub 1/2 sila zi-dub-dub.Ø 11 1⁄2 sila3 niĝ2-ar3-ra imĝaĝa3(ZIZ2.AN) 1/2 sila niĝ-ara imĝaĝa.Ø 12 ⸢sa2⸣-du11 ba-ĝal2-la-am3 sadug(.Ø) b.a-ĝal.Ø-a-am 13 ensi2 ensik 14 enim bi2-ib2-gi4-gi4-a enim.Ø b.i-b.gege.e-a 15 me dnin-ĝir2-su2-ka me Nin-Ĝirsu.ak.a(k) 16 ba-ni-ib2-la2-a b.a-ni-b.la.e-a.a(k) 17 sa2-du11-na sadug-ane.a 18 e2 dnin-ĝir2-su-ka-ta e Nin-Ĝirsu.ak.a(k).ta 19 enim ḫe2-eb2-gi4 enim.Ø ha-b(.i)-ge.Ø 20 ka-ka-ne2 ḫe2-keš2 kag-ane ha-(i)-keš.Ø
From the temple of Ninĝirsu, his lord, for the statue of Gudea, the city governor of Lagaš, the man, who built the Eninnu: 1 liter beer, 1 liter bread, half a liter of heaped-up flour, (and) half a liter of emmer groats are the regular offerings that are present. The regular offerings of a city governor, who revokes them and deprives it from the rites of Ninĝirsu, from the temple of Ninĝirsu they may be deprived; his (statue’s) mouth may be bound (i.e., stay closed).
The text in column “i” can be divided into two parts. The first part (lines 1–12) deals with the offerings allocated to the statue of Gudea. The main clause of this sentence can be found in line 12: “(These) are the regular offerings that are present for (the statue).” The verbal prefix b.a- represents the indirect object, which is co-referenced by the directive on the noun phrase in lines 3–7. The enclitic copula is used here, as this line summarizes the different commodities in the previous lines 8–11.
The second part of the inscription (lines 13–20) is morphologically more complex. This part of the text deals with a future city governor, who discontinues the offerings. The construction in lines 13–9 is noteworthy: The main clause contains the compound verb enim—ge4, which means here “to revoke, refuse (lit. to return the word on something).”194 The semantic object is marked with a LOC2: *sadug-ane.a (line 17). The possessive suffix refers here to the city governor (line 13), who represents the head noun to two subordinate clauses (lines 14 and 15–16). The presence of the possessive suffix suggests an anticipatory genitive, which needs
194 See Attinger 2021: 396 s.v. ge4, enim(KA) ge4.



CHAPTER 18. SUBORDINATION 261
to be analyzed after the last subordinate clause. Without subordinate clauses one expects here a form ensi2-ka sa2-du11-ne2, (lit.) “of the city governor his regular offerings.”
The head noun ensi2 is followed by two statements that represent subordinate clauses. Both clauses qualify the city governor. The first of these statements contains the afore-mentioned verb. Semantic object is here certainly the regular offerings, but these are only referred to in the verbal prefix chain with the locative 2 prefix: enim.Ø b.i-b.gege.e-a (line 14). The second subordinated statement uses the verb la2, which in this context means “to deprive of.” This particular meaning can be found in contexts where offerings are “diminished” (la2 = Akk. maṭû).195 It is very likely that at the end of this line a genitive case is to be expected to mark the nomen rectum of the anticipatory genitive. The referent for the locative 1 infix is likely the statue.
195 See G. Zolyomi (2020): “On the interpretation of Gudea Statue B i 15–16,” in: CM 50, 804–817.



Introducing the city governor
With column ii the main inscription starts. Most of this section (ii,4–iii,5) deals with Gudea and his various epithets. Many of these epithets follow the standard phraseology of royal inscripitions, which can be found already in (Lagashite) inscriptions of the late Early Dynastic period. Structurally, the inscription follows building inscriptions.

Slightly more complicated is the situation with the verb d e 6 / t u m 2 , “to bring.” Here, in fact, we can differentiate between two verbs (see Sallaberger 2005). The verb with the perfective stem t u m 2 means “to bring” when the object can move on its own. Examples concern people, animals, and boats: e.g., d a m - n e 2 b a - t u m 2 - m a - a , “when he brought his spouse” (ba-n.tum.Ø-a.a).
199 Steinkeller 2015: 149 reads this line kiĝ2 a-ra2 ba-be4, “they (all) shared work equally.” 200 Alternatively nu-gub, “did not place.” 201 See Steinkeller 2015: 151 with the translation “dirge-mother.”

Within the boundaries of Lagash, no-one took a person involved in a lawsuit to the place of oathtaking. No debt-collector did enter a man’s house.
Line 11: The sign used in the stone inscriptions of Gudea for the verb “to enter” is REC 145. The abbreviation REC refers to the sign list THUREAU-DANGIN 1898. This sign can also be referred to according to the sign list SCHNEIDER 1935: KWU 634. This sign can also be found outside the lapidary inscriptions in economic records. The Gudea Cylinders use in the same context the sign KWU 147, which corresponds to the later sign LIL. Starting with texts of the Old Akkadian period there is also the sign KWU 636 used for the verb ku4, “to enter”: e.g., a-ša3-ga ba-a-da-an-kux(KWU 636), “(the barley) was brought into the field” (JCS SS 5, 143 o,3).
Presargonic royal inscriptions from Lagaš use in the context of bringing the statue into the temple the sign DU, for which the reading kux is suggested: e.g., den-lil2-la e2-a mu-na-ni-DU, “he brought (the statue) into the temple for Enlil.”
Alternatively it can also be encoded as KWU 147 referring to the sign list SCHNEIDER 1935. The sign corresponds to the sign LIL later on. The Gudea Cylinder inscription uses in the same context



The given sign form is the one found in texts of the Ur III period. The reading ku4 is inprecise. Usually it is referred to by the number in the sign list SCHNEIDER 1935: KWU 147 = LIL. This sign is not used in Early Old Babylonian and later texts any longer to write the verb “to enter.” It does not, however, become obsolete and can be used, e.g., as syllabic sign lil in Akkadian texts of much later periods. For the verb “to enter” texts use KU4 (= KWU 636; ), which is still differentiated from the sign TU (= KWU 635; ). Later on, both signs converge. The sign inventory YBC 4615 clearly differentiates between KWU 635 and KWU 636. There is yet another sign that indicates the verb “to enter”: KWU 634 ( ). This sign is used, for instance, in stone inscriptions such as Gudea’s statue inscriptions ( ). Gudea’s Cylinder inscription, however, use in the same context KWU 147.

CHAPTER 18. SUBORDINATION 283
Texts 15 (Gudea Cylinder)
The term Gudea Cylinders refers to two large hollow clay cylinders excavated in Tellō, the ancient site of Ĝirsu: Cylinder A is 61 cm high and has a diameter of 32 cm; Cylinder B is 56.5 cm high and has a diameter of 33 cm. The thickness of the cylinder walls is 2.9 and 2.5 cm respectively. Additionally, there are twelve cylinder fragments (some of which join), which are associated with the cylinders, but do not fit into any of the breaks, particularly in Cylinder B. It remains unanswered, whether this separate cylinder was part of the composition and thus formed the first part of a trilogy, or whether it represented a different composition. One fragment mentions that “Gudea will build my (i.e., Ninĝirsu’s) house for me” ([ g u 3 ] - d e 2 - a [ e 2 ] - ĝ u 1 0 m a - [ d ] u 3 e ). For a discussion of the different arguments pro or contra a third cylinder see AVERBECK 1997: 40–9.
The structure of the Cylinders is as follows: A i,1–11 prologue A i,12–vii,8 Gudea’s dream and its interpretation A vii,9–xii,19 incubation of a message dream A xii,20–xxv,21 construction of the new Eninnu A xxv,22–xxx,5 furnishing the Eninnu A xxx,6–16 song of praise; colophon B i,1–11 prologue B i,12–ii,6 preparation of the induction of Ninĝirsu and Baba into the Eninnu B ii,7–xiii,10 induction of Ninĝirsu and Baba B xiii,11–xxiv,8 dedication of the Eninnu B xxiv,9–17 song of praise; colophon

CHAPTER 18. SUBORDINATION 299
Texts 17 (Iddin-Dagan B)
The short royal hymn Iddin-Dagan B is one of the texts belonging to the so-called Tetrad, i.e., a group of four compositions which were taught towards the end of a scribe’s basic training. On compilation tablets, the sequence of the four texts is Lipit-Eštar B, Iddin-Dagan B, Enlil-bānī A, and Nisaba A. The hymn is best attested on a multi-column tablet published as SRT 52 and a sixsided prism published as TCL 16, 88. The cuneiform rendering given below follows the distribution of lines on these manuscripts. The tablet H 156 is a compilation tablet from Me-Turan (mod. Tell Haddad), which originally contained all four texts. A Type III source with the beginning of the text is PBS 5, 64. Additionally, parts of the composition are known from the bilingual manuscripts UET 6, 88. Most manuscripts are short extracts on Type IV or lenticular tablets. Apart from unprovenienced sources, the lentils come from Uruk.211

CHAPTER 19. DIRECT AND INDIRECT SPEECH 315
19. Direct and Indirect Speech
Sumerian has several ways to flag direct speech. One more elusive way is by the choice of orthography. In dialogues between male and female speakers the female speaker is often written in Emesal (formatted bold; see Chapter 1.1). The following example is attested in a love song:
Example 19.1
nin9-ĝu10 tab-ba-be2 ga-mu-ra-de6 dinana tab-ba-be2 ga-mu-ra-de6 ses tab-ba-be2 a-ma-i-ra-ta
a-ba-a ma-ab-ze2-ze2 a-ba-a ma-ab-ze2-ze2 gu-be2 ma-a-ra a-ba-a ma-ab-ze2-ze2
(Utu:) “My sister, let me bring it to you (already) twined. Inana, let me bring it to you (already) twined.” (Inana:) “Brother, after you have brought it to me (already) twined, who will warp it for me? Who will warp it for me? Who will warp that flax for me?” (Dumuzi-Inana A 25–9)
Only the first two lines of this quote are written in the main dialect. The remainder uses Emesalorthography. The verbal form a - m a - i - r a - t a , “after you have brought it to me,” combines a nominalized form with the prefix of anteriority (see Chapter 15.1.2), followed by the ablative case. The verb base is ir, which is the corresponding Emesal form of the verb t u m u 2 , “to bring.” The finite form m a - a b - z e 2 - z e 2 , “he/she will warp it for me,” uses the Emesal verb base z e 2 for d u n , “to warp.” Finally, the form m a - a - r a is the Emesal for ĝ a 2 - r a , “for me.”
Some Sumerian literary texts, particularly the School Dialogues, separate the speakers by double rulings.
Far more common are clear introductions before the direct speech. Here, we usually find a finite form of the compound verb g u 3 - - d e 2 , “to call out:”
Example 19.2
u4-ba den-ki-ke4 den-lil2-ra gu3 mu-na-de2-e ud-be.a Enkik.e Enlil.ra gu.Ø mu-nn.a-(b.)de.e day-POSS.LOC DN.ERG DN.DAT voice.ABS VEN-DAT-3sgP.to pour out.3sgA
On that day, Enki addressed Enlil: “...” (Sheep and Grain 180; OB)
The direct speech may also be followed by a form of the verb d u 1 1 - g , “to say:”
Example 19.3
eg2 ki-sur-ra dnanše ĝa2-kam i3-mi-du11 eg kisura Nanše.ak.Ø ĝa.ak-am i-m-b.i-(n.)dug.Ø dyke boundary DN.GEN.ABS IND.GEN-COP VP-VEN-OO-3sgA.to say.3sgP
“The boundary dyke of Nanše is mine,” he declared. (Ent. 1 iv,26–29; ED IIIb; Lagaš)



Example 19.4
ḫu-ru ama-ar-gi4-me in-ĝar-ra lu2-enim-ma-be2 ga-mu-tumu2-<de3> bi2-in-eš ab-ĝu.Ø
cow-POSS.ABS
“Should my cow go to the steppe, I will not part from my cow,” I said. (Three Ox-Drivers from Adab 10; OB)
19.2. The particle (-)/eše/ Literature: EDZARD 2003: 157–8;
Example 19.5
ab2-ĝu10 edin-še3 u3-ba-ĝen ab2-ĝu10-ta ba-ra-e3-de3-en e-še ab-ĝu.Ø edin.še u-ba-ĝen.Ø ab-ĝu.ta bara-ed.en eše cow-POSS.ABS steppe.TERM ANT-MED-to go.3sgS cow-POSS.ABL MOD-to go out.1sgS DIAL
“Should my cow go to the steppe, I will not part from my cow,” I said. (Three Ox-Drivers from Adab 10; OB)
Besides of the use of conjunctions such as u 4 - d a or t u k u m - b e 2 , the finite prefix u expressing an anterior action or the modal prefix ḫa can carry conditional meaning.

ka5a idigna-še3 ĝeš3 i3-sur-sur-ra a-eštubku6 ba-zi-ge-en e-še ka.e idigna.še ĝeš.Ø i-(b.)sursur.Ø-a a’eštub.Ø ba-(b.)zig.en eše fox.ERG Tigris.TERM urine.ABS VP-3sgA.to drip.3sgP-NOM tide.ABS MED-3sgP.to rise.2sgA DIAL
