CHAPTER 19. DIRECT AND INDIRECT SPEECH 317
Excursus E: Legal texts
Characteristic features of legal texts are seal impressions, named witnesses, and often (preserved or not) an envelope. Statistically most common are loan contracts (see Chapter 19.7). Several of the types of legal texts are also studied and copied by apprentice scribes in the Old Babylonian period. These so-called model contracts differ from actual legal documents due to the lack of seal impressions, witnesses, and a date. The tablet format may show differences as well. Different such contracts may also be collected on compilation tablets. Model judicial documents or court cases are far less common.
19.4. Witnesses
After the legal clauses the witnesses are listed. Individual witnesses are introduced by igi PN-še3,
“before PN” ( PN ). This construction is the standard for the Ur III period legal texts. In the Old Babylonian period igi appears on its own. Often also only the first witness is introduced by igi and the following witnesses by a simple Personenkeil. The later witness lists are also often followed by the expression kišeb3 lu2-ki-enim-ma-bi-me-eš, “they are the seals of the witnesses.” Sometimes this is followed by the verbal form ib2-ra ###
19.5. Date
For the date and how it is formatted on legal documents compare Chapter 10.7.
19.6. Sealing
For the date and how it is formatted on legal documents compare Chapter 10.8.
19.7. Loan contract212
Loan contracts, mostly of private nature, deal usually with silver ( ) or grain ( ), rarely also
with copper ( ), oil ( ), or wool ( ). The loaned commodity is often characterized as u r 5
š e 3 , “as interest-bearing loan” ( ). Silver yields 20 % interest; barley 33 %. The interest
referred to as m a š 2 ( ) is given relative to one kor of barley: For silver loans we find the expression m a š 2 5 g i ĝ 4 1 g i ĝ 4 - t a , “the interest (is in) five shekels (silver) one shekel each”
( ); for barley loans the interest clause is m a š 2 1 . 0 . 0 š e g u r 0 . 1 . 4
t a , “the interest (is in) one kor barley one barig and four ban each” ( ).
The loan may also be given without any interest. The formula here is m a š 2 n u - t u k u or m a š 2
n u - u b - t u k u , “it bears no interest” ( ( ) ).
The document was issued when the loan was accepted. The seal impressed on a loan contract or its envelope is the seal of the party, who accepts the loan. The operational clause follows the formulary of receipts that use a form of the compound verb š u — t i , “to accept” (see Chapter 10.10.1).
212 LUTZMANN 1976; SALLABERGER 1999: 218–20; DAHL 2020: ###.



Loan contracts also inform when payment is due. This can be expressed in different ways. Barley loans are usually repaid in Month III after harvest was done. Silver loans could be repaid during the whole year. Loan contracts may also add the place of return. The verbs used in this clause are g i 4 , “to return,” s u , “to repay,” š u m 2 , “to give,” a ĝ 2 , “to measure” (barley), and l a 2 , “to weigh” (silver). Examples are e ĝ i r b u r u 1 4 - š e 3 / s u - s u - d a m , “it is to be paid after the harvest” (NBC 10492, r 1–2).
Loan contracts may also contain clauses that stipulate penalties, if the loan is not repaid. This can be expressed in a conditional sentence that is followed by the oath formula and hence subordinated. Examples are t u k u m - b e 2 n u - s u i b 2 - t a b - b a - a , “if it will not be repaid, that it will be doubled” (NATN 164 o,5); t u k u m - b e 2 n u - a ĝ 2 / a b - t a b - b e 2 - a , “if it will not be measured, that it will be doubled” (AUCT 3, 319 r,2–3). The amount, usually doubled, can also be expressed: t u k u m - b e 2 n u - s u - g e 2 - d e 3 / 5 . 1 . 4 g u r a ĝ 2 - e - d a , “should he not repay it, that 5.1.4 gur (barley) are to be measured” (UDT 31 o,7–r,1; the loaned amount is 2.3.2 gur).
Example 19.8 Obverse 1 10-la2-1 giĝ4 igi-4-ĝal2 ku3-babbar 9 shekels and 1/15th of silver, 2 si-i3-tum niĝ2-ka9-ak the remainder of the accounting, 3 maš2 5 giĝ4 1 giĝ4-ta – the interest (is in) five shekels (silver) one shekel each –, 4 ki a-ad-da-be2-ta from Addabe, 5 lu2-giri17-zal Lugirizal 6 šu ba-an-ti received. 7 iti sig4-ga enim / gi4-gi4-da Reverse 1 mu lugal-be2 i3-pa3 2 igi ur-dasar-lu-ḫi 3 igi lu2-diĝir-ra 4 igi da-da (blank) 5 iti bara2-[za3-ĝa]r 6 ⸢mu⸣ s[i-mu-ru-u]mki / ⸢ba⸣-ḫul
Seal 1 lu2-giri17-zal 2 dumu ses-kal-la
Silver and barley loans are also very common in the Old Babylonian period.213 These, however, show some differences in the formulary. After the loaned commodity appears usually the type of
loan. Common loan types are u r 5 - r a , “interest-bearing loan” ( ),š u - l a 2 , “” ( ), and
e š - d e 2 - a , “” ( ). It is followed by the interest clause, often m a š 2 i n - t u k u , “it bears
interest” ( ). Alternatively, Old Babylonian loan contracts use the expression m a š 2
g i - n a d a ḫ - ḫ e 2 - d a m , “a customary interest is to be added” ( ), or
m a š 2 g i - n a b a - a b - d a ḫ - e , “a customary interest he will add” ( ).
213 SKAIST 1994.



CHAPTER 19. DIRECT AND INDIRECT SPEECH 319
19.8. Sale contract
Sale contracts of the Ur III period are much more scarce than loan contracts or other administrative documents (see Chapter Excursus A:). Most sale contracts originate from Nippur. The level of language and writing proficiency is noticeable across the corpus. Unorthographic spellings are commonly found in the documents and may indicate poor school education by the scribes who composed them. It is also striking that in contrast to later (Old Babylonian) sale documents, the formulary of Ur III sale contracts shows a greater deal of variety.
Excluded in the Ur III period is the purchase of agricultural land, which may not have been in private hand in this period. Real estate that appears in sale contracts consists of houses and date palm orchards. Sales also concerned animals (cattle and equids) and individuals such as slaves. The sale object appears at the beginning of the document. Examples are 1 saĝ ninta, “one male slave” (###), 1 1⁄3 sar 6 giĝ4 e2 du3-a u3 kislaḫ, “one and a third sar and six giĝ of a built-up house-lot and empty ground,” or 3 udu, “three sheep.”
The purchase price is usually given after the sale object. The most common term is niĝ2-šam2, “purchase price” ( ). If the sale object is a person, then the form is often niĝ2-šam2-ma-ne2 or only šam2-ma-ne2. Persons are usually named with mu-ne2-em, “it is his/her name” (
).
Sale contracts, written in the Sumerian language, are more standardized in the Old Babylonian period.
19.9. Promissory Note
Promissory notes or debt notes are attested for the first time in the Old Babylonian period. The
operational phrase is commodity ugu PN in-tuku, “PN owes commodity” ( PN ).
19.10. Marriage agreement
A clause found in Sumerian marriage contracts dating to the Old Babylonian period is nam-dam
še3 in-tuku-am3, “(that) he took her as wife” ( ) followed by an oath formula. The contracts also stipulate what property of the wife has been brought into the household of the husband: PNf PN dam-a-na-ra in-na-ni-in-ku4, “She brought it into (the house of) PN, her
spouse” ( ###).



Marriage contracts may also contain other clauses such as what happens in case either party claims that the other party is not spouse. The way this is expressed reminds of adoption contracts and can also be found in law collections.
A daughter may also be given as wife to someone: nam-dam-še3 in-na-an-šum2 (
).
19.11. Division of inheritance
An important term to look out for is ḫa-la, “share” ( ).
19.12. Adoption
Dating to the Old Babylonian period
19.13. Judicial documents
In the majority of cases the known judicial documents of the Ur III period are not copies that would be issued to the individual parties of a legal case, but are archival texts stored in the central archives. This is suggested by the good number of tablets, which compile different cases (so-called
Sammelurkunden). Concluded legal cases are referred to as d i t i l - l a ( ).
Court documents may concern a wide range of topics.

III
The capacity units b a n 2 and b a r i g a use their own number signs. The vertical wedge in Ur III and Old Babylonian accounting is preceded by a vertical curvilinear impression in Early Dynastic texts. Ur III accounting marks 5 ban2 by inserting crossing wedges. In Old Babylonian metrology 4 ban2 look like 3 ban2 with an additional Winkelhaken on the right; 5 ban2 with the addition of two Winkelhaken on the right. Note that the ED accounting bundles 4 bariga into one gur, whereas Ur III and later 5 bariga bundle into one gur. The unit g u r is counted with the horizontal wedge.
Example 21.2
5 3(barig) 1(ban2) 4 5/6 sila3 i3 ḫi.a gur



The numerical notations in this example can be annotated as 5(aš) 3(barig) 1(ban2) 4 5/6 sila3 or by using the place value system 5,3.1 4 5/6 sila3. If in this example the gur-value is omitted, we write 0,3.1.
Lagaš (ED IIIb period)

Text ### (Building inscription)
The present text is one of the few surviving royal inscriptions of the early Old Babylonian king Urdukuga, who ruled Isin at the beginning of the 19th century BCE. The inscription is found on a few clay cones, inscribed on both the flat side of the cone’s head as well as around the nail part.
u r u 1 6 - n — massive, powerful

For Dagan, the great lord of the mountains, the god who created him, Urdukuga, the shepherd who brings everything for Nippur, the august farmer of An and Enlil, provider of the Ekur, who heaps up abundance for Ešumeša and Egalmaḫ, who returns the regular offerings to the deities, which were taken from the sanctuaries, the mighty king, the king of Isin, the king of Sumer and Akkad, spouse, looked favorably upon by Inana, built the Etuškiĝara in Isin, his beloved pure abode.

YBC 472 Obverse 1 d a - d a - g a Dadaga.(ra) 2 u 3 - n a - a - d u 1 1 u-nn.a-e.dug.Ø 3 1 e m e 6 m a ḫ 2 diš eme maḫ 4 1 d u r 3 ĝ e š diš dur ĝeš.Ø 5 m u - k u × (DU) d š a r a 2 - t a mukuř Šara.a(k).ta Reverse 1 l u g a l - k u 3 - g a - n e 2 Lugal-kug-ane.(ra) 2 ḫ e 2 - n a - a b - š u m 2 - m u ḫa-(i)-nn.a-b.šum.e 3 d u b - b a - n e 2 š u ḫ a - b a - š i - i b 2 - t i - ĝ e 2 6 dub-ane.Ø šu.e ḫa-ba-(n.)ši-b.teĝ.e
Speak to Dadaga: one large female donkey (and) one rearing male donkey Lugalkugane may give to him from the deliveries of Šara. His tablet he may receive.
Text 10 (Royal inscription)
This is the only longer inscription by king Anam (or Dingiram), an early Old Babylonian king of Uruk, who ruled at the end of the nineteenth century BCE. The inscription is carved onto a limestone tablet (YBC 2145) in two columns. A hand copy was published as YOS 1, 36. The inscription concerns the same temple as the foundation tablet discussed earlier (Text 3).

9. DAS VERBUM
f. Partizipien:
Es gibt im Sumerischen zwei Partizipien:



CHAPTER 22. GLOSSARY 377
• sog. ḫamṭu-Partizip (B-a):215
zB sum-ma *sum-a "gegeben, der, der gegeben hat"
ki-áƞ(a) *ki-aƞ-a "geliebt"
sar-ra *sar-a "geschrieben" etc.
Das Perfekt-Partizip kann wie in anderen Sprachen als Adjektiv dienen (zB lugal-ki-áƞ-ani *lugal-ki-aƞ-a-ani "sein geliebter König").
• sog. marû-Partizip (B(-ed))
Beim Präsens-Partizip wird entweder der marû-Stamm alleine oder durch Anfügung eines edMorphems geschrieben.216
zB sum-mu *sum-ed "gebend; der, der gibt"
Auch Präsens-Partizipien können in adjektivischer Verwendung auftreten. Dabei sorgt das edMorphem oftmals für einen finalen Aspekt (zB ƞeštu-sum-mu *ƞeštug-sum-e(d) "die zu gebende Waffe"). Das Verb du11/dug4 - e "sprechen, sagen" (akk. qabû(m)) hat beispielsweise ein eigenes marû-Partizip: di "sagend".
i. Satzverbindende Elemente:
Von den modalen Präfixen zu trennen sind der Prospektiv (u-) und inga-:
Prospektiv (u-)
Das prospektive u- drückt eine Vorzeitigkeit zu einem nachfolgenden Verb aus. Ein gutes Beispiel bietet die Briefeinleitungsformel: ù-na-a-du11 *u-(æ-)n.a-e.dug "Nachdem du zu ihm gesagt hast." Dietz Otto Edzard fasst diese letzte Form als einen abgeschwächten Imperativ auf "Würdest du ihm bitte sagen." In altsumerischen Briefen findet sich an selber Stelle du11-ga-na *dug+ã-n.a(-b) "Sage es ihm!".
Satzverbindendes inga
In seiner Vollform tritt inga- an erster Stelle der Verbalkette auf. Befindet sich jedoch davor ein modales Präfix oder ein Ventiv-/m/, bleibt von diesem Marker nur /nga/ übrig. In altsumerischen Texten findet sich in der Schrift meist e-ga-°. inga- drückt eine Folge aus ("und daher, und auch, etc."):
zB gal mu-zu gal ì-ga-túm-mu *gal mu-(b.)zu.e gal inga-(b.)tum.e "Großes weiß er und Großes setzt er auch in die Tat um."
In Verbindung mit anderen modalen Präfixen bildet inga- einige Sonderbedeutungen (zB šinga-° "auch, in der Tat, wie wir bereits wissen").
o. Versuch einer zusammenfassenden Darstellung des Verbums:
215 Dieses -a begegnet beispielsweise auch bei Adjektiven. Dort hat es eine determinierende Funktion: diƞir-zi "ein gerechter Gott" ̆ diƞir-zi-da "der (bestimmte) gerechte Gott". 216 Die Verwendungsweisen von -ed (auch in finiten Verbalformen) folgen weiter unten.



In den vorangegangenen Kapiteln wurde nun das Verbum in seinen Grundzügen dargestellt. Nun soll versucht werden, eine möglichst übersichtliche Zusammenfassung gegeben zu werden:
Man unterscheidet bei einem Satz im Sumerischen zuallererst zwei Aspekte: (1) Transitiv (zum Verbum ist ein Objekt vorhanden, das jedoch nicht außerhalb der Verbalkette geschrieben sein muss) und (2) Intransitiv (hauptsächlich bei Verba, die eine Bewegung ausdrücken, zB du/ƞen „gehen“). Je nach Aspekt ergeben sich für die Verbalkette unterschiedliche „Konjugations“Muster.
Unterscheidungen bei der Formenbildung des sumerischen Verbums finden sich auch bei zwei weiteren Aspekten, die unabhängig von Transitivität und Intransitivität verwendet werden: (a) ḫamṭu (perfektivisch-präterital) und (b) marû (präsentisch-futurisch). Die Interpretation der Zeiten ergibt sich durch Vergleiche zum Akkadischen.
Der Verbalkette an sich weist eine festgesetzte Ordnung auf. Kernpunkt bildet die Verbalbasis, bei der Unterschiede zwischen ḫamṭu und marû sowie auch jeweils zwischen Singular und Plural auftreten können:
du11/dug4 „sagen, sprechen“ sg. pl.
ḫamṭu du11/dug4 e
marû e e
Da das Sumerische eine agglutierende Sprache darstellt, wird die Verbalbasis in den einzelnen Formen (1. sg. c., 2. pl. etc.) nicht abgeändert wie beispielsweise im Akkadischen, sondern durch Voran- bzw. Nachstellen von Morphemen näher bestimmt. Dabei gibt es ebenfalls Unterschiede zwischen ḫamṭu und marû, die jedoch nur in der transitiven bzw. Zweiteilnehmer-„Konjugation“ auftreten:
Bei ḫamṭu wird vor der Basis auf den Ergativ verwiesen und nach derselben auf den Absolutiv. Der Absolutivverweis findet sich jedoch nur in der 1. und 2. Person Singular (*°.en). Die Pluralformen weisen dagegen eine Doppelpräsenz vor und nach der Basis durch den Ergativverweis auf (*°n.B.eš).
Bei marû befindet sich vor der Basis der Absolutiv- und nach der Basis der Ergativverweis.
Die einfachste Form einer Verbalkette benötigt vor dem vorangestellten bzw. präfigierten Morphem noch ein sog. „Konjugationspräfix“ bzw. einen Verbalindikator, der anzeigt, dass danach eine Verbalform folgt (zB æ-, mu-, ba-/bi-).

23.5. Online resources
CDLI Cuneiform Digital Library Initiative (https://cdli.mpiwg-berlin.mpg.de) The CDLI aims to catalog all cuneiform artifacts that are housed in collections worldwide. It provides meta data and often images and transliterations of texts. Text artifacts referred to by museum number are best found using the museum siglum and a five digit number (e.g., YBC 02177). Each artifact is assigned its unique ID (so-called P-number). YBC 2177 has the P-number P467305, which can also be linked directly by the URL https://cdli.mpiwg-berlin.mpg.de/P467305. Compositions use a Q-number. Seals and Seal impressions use a S-number.
eBL Electronic Babylonian Literature (https://www.ebl.lmu.de) Despite its name the eBL is continuously growing and houses not only editions of Akkadian texts, but also Sumerian texts of different genres. Additionally it offers electronic sign lists, a dictionary, and a powerful search engine.
ORACC Open Richly Annotated Cuneiform Corpus (http://oracc.museum.upenn.edu)
ePSD Electronic Pennsylvania Sumerian Dictionary (http://oracc.museum.upenn.edu/epsd2/sux)
ETCSL Electronic Text Corpus of Sumerian Literature (https://etcsl.orinst.ox.ac.uk) The ETCSL provides composite editions of many of the extant Sumerian literary compositions.
ETCSRI Electronic Text Corpus of Sumerian Royal Inscriptions (http://oracc.museum.upenn.edu/etcsri/)
CCP Cuneiform Commentaries Project (https://ccp.yale.edu)
BDTNS Database of Neo-Sumerian Texts (http://bdtns.filol.csic.es)
ArchiBab Archives Babyloniennes (XXe-XVIIe Siècles av. J.-C.) (http://www.archibab.fr)
